===Original===
> When the other events of the Spanish war shall be lost in the obscurity of time, or only traced by disconnected fragments, the story of Zaragoza, like some ancient triumphal pillar standing amidst ruins, will tell a tale of past glory, and already men point to the heroic city and call her Spain.

===Chinese===
> 当半岛战争中的其余事迹都湮没于时间的幽暗，或仅余断简残篇可供追索时，萨拉戈萨的故事，将如一座矗立于废墟之间的古代凯旋柱，诉说着往昔的荣光；而今，人们已然指着这座英雄之城，称她为西班牙。

## I / 第一章

===Original===
It was, I believe, the evening of the eighteenth when we saw Saragossa in the distance. As we entered by the Puerta de Sancho we heard the clock in the Torre Nueva strike ten. We were in an extremely pitiful condition as to food and clothing. The long journey we had made from Lerma through Salas de los Infantes, Cervera, Ágreda, Tarazona, and Borja, climbing mountains, fording rivers, making shortcuts until we arrived at the high road of Gallur and Alagón, had left us quite used up, worn out, and ill with fatigue. In spite of all, the joy of being free sweetened our pain.

We were four who had succeeded in escaping between Lerma and Cogollos by freeing our innocent hands from the rope that bound together so many patriots. On the day of the escape, we could count among the four of us a total capital of eleven reales; but after three days of marching, when we entered the metropolis of Aragón and balanced our mutual cash, our common wealth was found to be a sum total of thirty-one *cuartos*. We bought some bread at a little place next the Orphanage, and divided it among us.

Don Roque, who was one of the members of our expedition, had good connections in Saragossa, but this was not an hour to present ourselves to anyone. We postponed until the next day this matter of looking up friends; and as we could not go to an inn, we wandered about the city, looking for a shelter where we could pass the night. The market scarcely seemed to offer exactly the comfort and quiet which our tired bodies needed. We visited the leaning tower, and although one of my companions suggested that we should take refuge in the plaza, I thought that we should be quite the same as if altogether in the open country. The place served us, none the less, for temporary refuge and rest, and also as a refectory, where we despatched happily our supper of dry bread, glancing now and then at the great upright mass of the tower, whose inclination made it seem like a giant leaning to see who was running about his feet. By the light of the moon that brick sentinel projected against the sky its huddled and shapeless form, unable to hold itself erect. The clouds were drifting across its top, and the spectator looking from below trembled with dread, imagining that the clouds were quiet and that the tower was moving down upon him. This grotesque structure, under whose feet the overburdened soil has settled, seems to be forever falling, yet never falls.

===Chinese===
我想，那已是十八日的傍晚，我们远远望见了萨拉戈萨（Saragossa）。从圣乔门（Puerta de Sancho）入城时，听见新塔（Torre Nueva）上的钟敲了十下。我们衣食两缺，境况凄惨至极。从莱尔马取道萨拉斯-德洛斯因方特斯、塞维拉、阿格雷达、塔拉索纳、博尔哈，一路翻山越岭、涉水渡河、抄走捷径，直到踏上加柳尔至阿拉贡（Alagón）的大道，这趟漫长的旅程已把我们拖得筋疲力尽、形容枯槁、积劳成疾。尽管如此，重获自由的欢愉仍为我们冲淡了苦楚。

我们一行四人，是在莱尔马与科戈略斯（Cogollos）之间逃出来的，把我们清白无辜的双手从那根捆缚着众多爱国志士的绳索里解脱了出来。逃出那天，我们四人清点全部家当，共计十一里亚尔（reales）；可是徒步行军三日之后，待到进入阿拉贡（Aragón）的首府、把各人的现钱归拢合账，我们共同的家底拢共只剩三十一个夸阿托（cuartos）了。我们在孤儿院隔壁的一家小铺买了些面包，四人均分了。

唐·罗克（Don Roque）是我们这支逃亡队伍里的一员，在萨拉戈萨颇有些熟人故旧，但眼下这个时辰，实在不宜登门求见任何人。寻访亲友之事，只好推迟到次日；我们又住不起客栈，便在城里四处游荡，寻觅一处过夜的栖身之地。市场那地方，似乎断然给不了我们疲惫的身躯所急需的安逸与清静。我们逛到那座斜塔跟前，虽然有一个同伴提议到广场上落脚，我却觉得那同露宿荒郊野外没什么两样。不过这地方倒也够我们暂避一时、歇息片刻，还兼作饭堂，我们在那里快快活活地吃了一顿干面包当晚餐，不时抬眼望一望塔身那巍然矗立的庞然大物——它倾侧的姿势，活像一个巨人俯下身来，要看是谁在他脚边跑来跑去。月光下，这座砖砌的哨兵把蜷曲无形的一团影子投上天幕，怎么也直不起身来。云朵从塔顶飘掠而过，从底下仰望的人吓得发颤，恍惚只当云是静止的，倒是那塔正朝自己压将下来。这座奇形怪状的建筑，脚下不堪重负的泥土已然沉降，仿佛永远正在倾倒，却又永远倒不下来。

===Original===
We passed through the avenue of the Coso again from this house of giants as far as the Seminary. We went through two streets, the Calle Quemada and the Calle del Rincón, both in ruins, as far as the little plaza of San Miguel. From here, passing from alley to alley, and blindly crossing narrow and irregular streets, we found ourselves beside the ruins of the monastery of Santa Engracia, which was blown up by the French at the raising of the first siege. The four of us exclaimed at once in a way to show that we all thought the same thing. Here we had found a shelter, and in some cosy corner under this roof we would pass the night!

The front wall was still standing with its arch of marble, decorated with innumerable figures of saints which seemed undisturbed and tranquil as if they knew nothing of the catastrophe. In the interior we saw broken arches and enormous columns struggling erect from the debris, presenting themselves, darkling and deformed, against the clear light flooding the enclosure, looking like fantastic creatures generated by a delirious imagination. We could see decorations, cornices, spaces, labyrinths, caverns, and a thousand other fanciful architectural designs produced by the ruins in their falling. There were even small rooms opened in the spaces of the walls with an art like that of Nature in forming grottos. The fragments of the altarpiece that had rotted because of the humidity showed through the remains of the vaulting where still hung the chains which had suspended the lamps. Early grasses grew between the cracks of the wood and stone. Among all this destruction there were certain things wholly intact, as some of the pipes of the organ and the grating of the confessional. The roof was one with the floor, and the tower mingled its fragments with those of the tombs below. When we looked upon such a conglomeration of tombs, such a myriad of fragments that had fallen without losing entirely their original form, and such masses of bricks and plaster crumbled like things made of sugar, we could almost believe that the ruins of the building had not yet settled into their final position. The shapeless structure appeared to be palpitating yet from the shock of the explosion.

Don Roque told us that beneath this church there was another one where they worshipped the relics of the holy martyrs of Saragossa; but the entrance to this subterranean sanctuary was closed up. Profound silence reigned, but, penetrating further, we heard human voices proceeding from those mysterious deeps. The first impression produced upon us by hearing these voices was as if the spirits of the famous chroniclers who wrote of the Christian martyrs, and of the patriots sleeping in dust below, were crying out upon us for disturbing their slumbers.

===Chinese===
我们从这巨人之家再度穿过科索大街（Coso）的林荫道，一直走到神学院；又穿过克马达街（Calle Quemada）与林孔街（Calle del Rincón）两条街——两处都已沦为废墟——来到圣米格尔（San Miguel）小广场。由此再钻进一条条小巷，七拐八绕、深一脚浅一脚地穿过那些狭窄歪斜的街巷，我们就到了圣恩格拉西亚修道院（Santa Engracia）的废墟旁；这修道院是在第一次围城解除之际被法军炸毁的。我们四人齐声叫了起来，那口气表明大家想到一处去了：过夜的地方这不就寻着了嘛！就在这残顶之下觅个安逸角落，将就过上一夜！

正面那堵墙还立着，连着那道大理石门拱，拱上雕着数不清的圣徒像，个个安然恬静，仿佛对这场浩劫一无所知。往里面看，但见断裂的拱券和硕大的石柱从瓦砾堆中挣扎着挺立起来，衬着泻满这方废园的明亮天光，显得幽暗而扭曲，宛如谵妄的想象孕育出的种种怪物。还辨认得出种种雕饰、檐口、空穴、迷径、洞窟，以及废墟倾颓之际造出的千百种奇思妙想的建筑格局。墙身的空隙间甚至辟出了一个个小间，其手笔之巧，直可同大自然凿造洞窟的造化相比。祭坛后屏因潮气而朽坏的残片，从穹顶的残余间露出来，那里还悬着当年吊挂灯盏的铁链。新生的嫩草从木头和石头的缝隙间钻出来。在这一片毁败之中，竟有些东西完好无损，比如管风琴的几根音管，还有告解室的格栅。屋顶与地面已合为一体，塔楼的碎块同底下坟墓的残石混作一处。眼见这般坟冢的堆叠、这般坠落却大致未失原形的无数残片、这般酥脆得如同糖塑的砖块灰泥，我们几乎要相信，这栋建筑的废墟还没有落定到它最终的位置。这无形体的庞然巨构，仿佛仍在爆炸的震悸中颤动不息。

唐·罗克告诉我们，这座教堂底下还有一座教堂，供奉着萨拉戈萨诸位圣殉道者的圣髑；只是这座地下圣所的入口已被封堵。四下里万籁俱寂，但再往深处走，我们却听见人声从那神秘的幽深之处传来。乍闻这些人声，我们的头一个感觉是：仿佛那些撰写基督徒殉道者事迹的著名编年史家的英灵，连同长眠于下方尘土中的爱国志士，正在厉声呵斥我们搅扰了他们的沉眠。

===Original===
On the instant, in the glare of a flame which illuminated part of the scene, we distinguished a group of persons sheltering themselves, huddling together in a space between two of the fallen columns. They were Saragossa beggars, who had made a palatial shelter for themselves in that place, seeking protection from the rain with beams of wood and with their rags. We also made ourselves as comfortable as might be in another place, and covering ourselves with a blanket and a half, prepared to go to sleep.

Don Roque said to me, “I know Don José de Montoria, one of the richest citizens of Saragossa. We were both born in Mequinenza. We went to school together, and we played our games together on the hills of Corregidor. It is thirty years since I have seen him, but I believe that he will receive us well. Like every good Aragonese, he is all heart. We will find him, fellows; we will see Don José de Montoria. I am of his blood on the maternal side. We will say﻿—”

But Don Roque was asleep, and I also slept.

===Chinese===
顷刻间，一蓬火光亮起，照亮了眼前的一角，我们这才辨出一群人正蜷作一团，在两根倒伏的石柱之间栖身。原来是萨拉戈萨的乞丐们，他们用木梁和身上的破衣烂衫遮挡雨水，在那里给自己营造了一座宫殿般的栖身之所。我们也在另一处各自安顿下来，能多舒服便多舒服，身上盖着一条半毯子，准备入睡。

唐·罗克对我说：“我认识唐·何塞·德·蒙特里亚（Don José de Montoria），萨拉戈萨数得着的富户之一。我们俩都是梅基嫩萨（Mequinenza）生人，一同上的学，一同在科雷希多尔山丘上玩耍。我有三十年没见他了，不过我相信他会好好接待我们。同所有地道的阿拉贡人一样，他是个热心肠。我们会找到他的，伙伴们；我们会见到唐·何塞·德·蒙特里亚的。论母系，我与他还有血缘之亲。我们要登门求见，就说——”

可是唐·罗克已经睡着了，我也睡着了。

## II / 第二章

===Original===
The place where we lay down did not by any blandishments invite us to sleep luxuriously until morning, and certainly a mattress of broken stones is conducive to early rising. We wakened with the dawn; and as we had to spend no time in making a toilet before a dressing-table, we were soon ready to go out and pay our visits.

The idea came to all four of us at once that it would be a good thing to have some breakfast, but at the same time we agreed unanimously that it was impossible, as we had not the wherewithal to carry out such a high purpose.

“Don’t be discouraged, boys,” said Don Roque; “because very soon I will take you all to the house of my friend, who will take good care of us.”
===Chinese===
我们躺卧的那个地方，丝毫没有用什么温存的手段来诱我们酣睡到天亮，而一张碎石铺成的褥垫，无疑最宜早起。破晓时分我们便醒了；既然不必在梳妆台前费工夫梳洗打扮，不多一会儿就已收拾停当，可以出门拜客了。

我们四个人不约而同地想到，吃一顿早饭该是件美事；但随即又一致公认，此事绝无可能，因为要实现如此崇高的目标，我们手头缺着必不可少的款项。

“别泄气，孩子们，”唐·罗克说，“用不了多久，我就带你们统统到我朋友家里去，他会好好照应咱们的。”

===Original===
While he was saying this, we saw emerging from our inn two men and a woman, of those who had been our companions there. They looked as if they were accustomed to sleep in the place. One of them was a cripple, a poor unfortunate who ended at his knees, and put himself in motion by the aid of crutches, swinging himself forward on them as if by oars. He was an old man, with a jovial face well burned by the sun. As he saluted us very pleasantly in passing, wishing us a good morning, Don Roque asked him in what part of the city was the house of Don José de Montoria. The cripple replied:﻿—

“Don José de Montoria? I know him as if he were the apple of my eye. It is twenty years since he used to live in the Calle de la Albardería. Afterwards he moved to another street, the Calle de la Parra, then﻿—but you are strangers, I see.”
===Chinese===
他正说着这话，我们便瞧见从我们投宿的那家客店里走出来两个男人和一个女人，都是先前同我们宿在那里的伙伴。看那样子，他们是睡惯了这个地方。其中一个是个残疾人，一个双腿齐膝而止的可怜的不幸者，靠一副拐杖行动，撑着双拐把自己一荡一荡地送向前去，就跟划桨似的。他是个老头儿，一张乐呵呵的脸，叫太阳晒得黢黑。打我们跟前经过时，他客客气气地向我们问好，祝我们早安，唐·罗克便趁机问他，唐·何塞·德·蒙特里亚（Don José de Montoria）的宅子在城里哪一带。那残疾人答道：——

“唐·何塞·德·蒙特里亚？我认得他，就像认得我自己的眼珠子。他住阿尔巴德利亚街（Calle de la Albardería）那会儿，已是二十年前的事了。后来他搬到另一条街，帕拉街（Calle de la Parra），再后来——可我看你们是外乡人。”

===Original===
“Yes, my good friend, we are strangers; and we have come to enlist with the troops of this brave city.”

“Then you were not here on the fourth of August?”

“No, my friend,” I answered him; “we were not present at that great feat of arms.”

“You did not see the battle of Eras?” asked the beggar, sitting down in front of us.

“We did not have that felicity either.”

“Well, Don José Montoria was there. He was one of those who pulled the cannon into place for firing. Well, well, I see that you haven’t seen a thing. From what part of the world do you come?”
===Chinese===
“是的，我的好朋友，我们是外乡人；我们来投这座英勇城市的队伍当兵。”

“那么，八月四日你们不在这儿？”

“不在，我的朋友，”我答道，“那件伟大的战功，我们没有躬逢其会。”

“那么埃拉斯（Eras）之战你们也没瞧见？”乞丐问着，在我们跟前坐了下来。

“那份眼福我们也没有。”

“咳，唐·何塞·蒙特里亚就在那儿。帮着把大炮拖上炮位开火的人里头，就有他一个。得，得，我看你们压根儿什么也没瞧见过。你们是从世界上哪个角落来的？”

===Original===
“From Madrid,” said Don Roque. “So you are not able to tell me where my dear friend Don José lives?”

“Well, I should think I can, man, well, I should think I can!” answered the cripple, taking from his pocket a crust of dry bread for his breakfast. “From the Calle de la Parra he moved to the Calle de Enmedio. You know that all those houses were blown up. There was Stephen López, a soldier of the Tenth Company of the First Regiment of Aragón Volunteers, and he alone, with forty men, himself forced the French to retire.”

“That must have been a fine thing to see!” said Don Roque.

“Oh, if you did not see the fourth of August you have seen nothing,” continued the beggar. “I myself also saw the fourth of June, because I was crawling along the Calle de la Paja, and I saw the woman who fired off the big cannon.”
===Chinese===
“从马德里（Madrid）来，”唐·罗克说。“这么说，您没法告诉我，我亲爱的朋友唐·何塞住在哪儿啦？”

“瞧您说的，我当然能，老兄，我当然能！”残疾人答道，一面从口袋里掏出一块干硬的面包皮来当早饭。“从帕拉街他又搬到了恩梅迪奥街（Calle de Enmedio）。您总知道，那些房子全都叫炸飞了。有个埃斯特万·洛佩斯（Stephen López），是阿拉贡（Aragón）义勇军第一团第十连的士兵，单是他一个人，带着四十个人，硬是逼得法军退了下去。”

“那一定好看得很！”唐·罗克说。

“噢，您要是没瞧见八月四日，那就等于什么也没瞧见，”乞丐接着说。“连六月四日我也亲眼瞧见了，那天我正顺着帕哈街（Calle de la Paja）往前爬，我瞧见了那个亲手放响大炮的女人。”

===Original===
“We have already heard of the heroism of that noble woman,” said Don Roque; “but if you could make up your mind to tell us﻿—”

“Oh, of course. Don José de Montoria is a great friend of the merchant Don Andrés Guspide, who on the fourth of August was firing from near the narrow street of the Torre del Pino. Hand-grenades and bullets were raining all about him, and my Don Andrés stood like a rock. More than a hundred dead lay about him, and he alone killed fifty of the French.”

“Great man, this one! And he is a friend of my friend?”
===Chinese===
“那位高贵女人的英勇事迹，我们已经有所耳闻，”唐·罗克说，“不过您要是肯费心给我们讲一讲——”

“噢，那还用说。唐·何塞·德·蒙特里亚跟商人唐·安德烈斯·古斯皮德（Don Andrés Guspide）是莫逆之交，八月四日那天，唐·安德烈斯就在皮诺塔（Torre del Pino）旁的窄街上放枪。手榴弹和子弹雨点似的落在他四周，可我的唐·安德烈斯站着像块岩石。他身边横着的死尸不下一百具，单是他一个人，就结果了五十个法国佬。”

“好一位了不起的人物！他竟是我朋友的朋友？”

===Original===
“Yes, señor,” replied the cripple; “and they are two of the best gentlemen in all Saragossa, and they give me a little something every Saturday. For you must know that I am Pepe Pallejas, and they call me Sursum Corda, as twenty-four years ago I was sacristan of the Church of Jesus, and I used to sing﻿—But this is not coming to the point, and I was going on to say I am Sursum Corda, and perhaps you have heard about me in Madrid?”

“Yes,” said Don Roque, yielding to his generous impulses; “it seems to me that I have heard the Señor Sursum Corda mentioned there, haven’t we, boys?”
===Chinese===
“是的，先生，”残疾人答道，“他们两位是全萨拉戈萨（Saragossa）顶好的绅士，每逢礼拜六都赏我一点儿东西。您可知道，我就是佩佩·帕列哈斯（Pepe Pallejas），人家管我叫 Sursum Corda（拉丁语，意为‘举起你们的心’），因为二十四年前，我是耶稣教堂（Church of Jesus）的教堂司事，平日里总唱——咳，这扯不到正题上；我是想说，我就是 Sursum Corda，兴许你们在马德里也听人说起过我？”

“是的，”唐·罗克说，一时屈从了自己那股慷慨的热肠，“我记得在那儿也听人提起过 Sursum Corda 先生来着，是不是，孩子们？”

===Original===
“Well, it’s likely, and you must know that before the siege I used to beg at the door of this monastery of Santa Engracia, which was blown up by the bandits on the thirteenth of August. I beg now at the Puerta de Jerusalem, at the Jerusalem Gate﻿—where you will be able to find me whenever you like. Well, as I was saying, on the fourth of August I was here, and I saw Francisco Quílez come out of the church, first sergeant of the First Company of fusileers, who, you must already know, with thirty-five men, cast out the bandits from the Convent of the Incarnation. I see that you look surprised﻿—yes! Well, in the orchard of the convent at the back is where the Lieutenant Don Miguel Gila died. There are at the least two hundred bodies in that orchard; and there Don Felipe San Clement, a merchant of Saragossa, broke both his legs. Indeed, if Don Miguel Salamero had not been present﻿—don’t you know anything about that?”

“No, sir, my friend,” said Don Roque; “we don’t know anything about it, and although we have the greatest pleasure in your telling us of so many wonders, what most concerns us now is to find out where we are going to find my old friend Don José. We four are suffering from a disease called hunger, which cannot be cured by listening to the recounting of sublimities.”
===Chinese===
“嗯，那是自然。您还得知道，围城以前，我就在这圣恩格拉西亚（Santa Engracia）修道院门口讨饭，八月十三日，它叫那伙强盗给炸上了天。如今我在耶路撒冷门（Puerta de Jerusalem）讨饭，就在耶路撒冷城门那儿——你们几时要找我，上那儿准能找到。好，我正说呢，八月四日那天我就在这儿，瞧见弗朗西斯科·基莱斯（Francisco Quílez）从教堂里出来，他是燧发枪手第一连的一等中士，这个您想必早知道了，他带着三十五个人，把强盗们从道成肉身修道院（Convent of the Incarnation）里撵了出去。我瞧你们一脸的惊奇——可不是！还有呢，修道院后头的果园，就是唐·米格尔·希拉（Don Miguel Gila）中尉阵亡的地方。那园子里的尸首，少说也有两百具；萨拉戈萨的商人唐·费利佩·圣克莱门特（Don Felipe San Clement），就是在那儿摔断了双腿。说真的，要不是唐·米格尔·萨拉梅罗（Don Miguel Salamero）当时在场——这些事你们一点儿也不知道？”

“不知道，先生，我的朋友，”唐·罗克说，“我们什么都不知道；听您讲这么些奇观，我们固然有无穷的乐趣，只是眼下最要紧的，是弄明白上哪儿去找我的老朋友唐·何塞。我们四个害着一种叫作饥饿的病，这病靠听人讲崇高事迹，是治不好的。”

===Original===
“Well, now, in a minute I will take you where you want to go,” replied Sursum Corda, offering us a part of his crust; “but first I will tell you something, and that is that if Don Mariano Cereso had not defended the Castle Aljafería as he did defend it, nothing would have been done in the Portillo quarter. And this man, by the grace of God, this man was Don Mariano Cereso! During the attack of the fourth of August, he used to walk in the streets with his sword in its antique sheath. It would terrify you to see him! This Santa Engracia quarter seemed like a furnace, señors. The bombs and the hand-grenades rained down; but the patriots did not mind them any more than so many drops of water. A good part of the convent fell down; the houses trembled, and all this that we see seemed no more than a barrier of playing cards, by the way it caught fire and crumbled away. Fire in the windows, fire at the top, fire at the base! The French fell like flies, fell like flies, gentlemen. And as for the Saragossans, life and death were all the same to them. Don Antonio Quadros went through there, and when he looked at the French batteries, he was in a state to swallow them whole. The bandits had sixty cannon vomiting fire against the walls. You did not see it? Well, I saw it, and the pieces of brick of the wall and the earth of the parapets scattered like crumbs of a loaf. But the dead served as a barricade﻿—the dead on top, the dead below, a perfect mountain of the dead. Don Antonio’s eyes shot flame. The boys fired without stopping. Their souls were all made of bullets! Didn’t you see it? Well, I did, and the French batteries were all cleaned out of gunners. When he saw one of the enemy’s cannon was without men, the commander shouted, ‘An epaulet to the man who spikes that cannon!’ Pepillo Ruiz started and walked up to it as if he was promenading in a garden among butterflies and may flowers, only here the butterflies were bullets, and the flowers were bombs. Pepillo Ruiz spiked the cannon, and came back laughing. And now another part of the convent was falling down. Whoever was smashed by it, remained smashed! Don Antonio Quadros said that that did not bother him any, and seeing that the enemy’s batteries had opened a large hole in the wall, went to stuff it full of bags of wool. Then a bullet struck him in the head. They brought him here; he said that was nothing either, and died.”
===Chinese===
“好，这就来，我马上带你们上要去的地方，”Sursum Corda 答道，把他的面包皮掰了一角分给我们，“可先得告诉你们一件事：要不是唐·马里亚诺·塞雷索（Don Mariano Cereso）那样死守了阿尔哈费里亚城堡（Aljafería），波蒂略门（Portillo）那一区就什么都办不成。而这个人，托上帝的福，这个人就是唐·马里亚诺·塞雷索！八月四日进攻那阵子，他佩着剑在街上走来走去，那剑鞘还是件老古董。见了他那副样子，您准得吓一跳！圣恩格拉西亚这一区活像一座大火炉，先生们。炸弹和手榴弹跟下雨似的；可爱国志士们拿它们只当几滴水珠。修道院塌掉了好大一半；房屋簌簌发抖，我们眼前这一切，瞧它烧起来、塌下去的样子，简直不过是一道纸牌码成的屏障。窗户里是火，房顶上是火，墙根底下还是火！法国佬像苍蝇一样往下倒，像苍蝇一样往下倒，先生们。至于萨拉戈萨人，生和死在他们眼里全是一回事。唐·安东尼奥·夸德罗斯（Don Antonio Quadros）打那儿经过，他瞧着法军的炮阵，那副神气恨不得把它们囫囵吞下去。强盗们有六十门大炮对着城墙喷吐火焰。您没瞧见？我可瞧见了：墙上的砖块和胸墙上的泥土，散开来就像一条面包上的面包屑。可死人成了街垒——死人在上，死人在下，端端正正一座死人山。唐·安东尼奥两眼喷火。小伙子们放枪放个不停。他们的魂灵全是子弹做的！您没瞧见？我瞧见了，法军的炮位上，炮手叫扫了个精光。指挥官瞧见敌人一门炮上没了人，就喊道：‘谁去把那门炮钉死，赏他一副肩章！’佩皮略·鲁伊斯（Pepillo Ruiz）应声而出，朝那门炮走去，那样子就像在花园里散步，身边尽是蝴蝶和五月的花；只不过在这座园子里，蝴蝶是子弹，花是炸弹。佩皮略·鲁伊斯把那门炮钉死了，一路笑着走回来。这当口，修道院又塌下来一片。谁叫它砸着，谁就砸定了！唐·安东尼奥·夸德罗斯说这全碍不着他什么事，眼见敌人的炮火在墙上轰开一个大窟窿，他便去拿羊毛袋把窟窿堵上。于是一颗子弹打中了他的脑袋。人们把他抬到这儿来；他说这也不算什么，说完就死了。”

===Original===
“Oh,” said Don Roque, impatiently, “we are sufficiently astonished, Señor Sursum Corda, and the most pure patriotism inflames us to hear you relate such great deeds; but if you could only make up your mind to tell us where﻿—”

“Good Lord!” exclaimed the beggar, “who said I wouldn’t tell you? If there is any one thing I know better than another, and have seen most of anything in my life, it is the house of Don José de Montoria. It is near the San Pablo. Oh, you did not see the hospital? Well, I saw it. There the bombs fell like hail; the sick, seeing that the roofs were falling down, threw themselves from the windows into the street. Others crawled or rolled down the stairs. The partitions burned, and you could hear wailings. The lunatics bellowed in their cages like mad beasts. Many of them escaped and went through the cloisters, laughing and dancing with a thousand fantastic gestures that were frightful to see. They came out into the street as on carnival day; and one climbed the cross in the Coso, where he began a harangue, saying that he was the River Ebro, and he would run over the city and put out the fire. The women ran to care for the sick, who were all carried off to Del Pilar and to La Seo. You could not get through the streets. Signals were given from the Torre Nueva whenever a bomb was coming, but the uproar of the people prevented their hearing the bells. The French advanced by this street of Santa Engracia. They took possession of the hospital and of the Convent of San Francisco. The fighting began in the quarter of the Coso, and in the streets thereabouts. Don Santiago Sas, Don Mariano Cereso, Don Lorenzo Calvo, Don Marcos Simono, Renovales, Martin Albantos, Vicente Codé, Don Vicente Marraco, and others fearlessly attacked the French. And behind a barricade made by herself, awaited them, furious, gun in hand, the Countess de Bureta.”
===Chinese===
“哦，”唐·罗克不耐烦地说，“我们惊也惊够了，Sursum Corda 先生，听您叙述这般伟大的功业，最纯洁的爱国热忱正在我们胸中燃烧；可您要是能拿定主意告诉我们那地方——”

“老天爷！”乞丐嚷道，“谁说我不告诉你们？要说这世上哪一样东西我认得最熟、哪一样事情我这辈子见得最多，那就是唐·何塞·德·蒙特里亚的宅子了。就在圣巴勃罗（San Pablo）附近。哦，你们没瞧见那座医院？我可瞧见了。那儿炸弹落得像冰雹；病人们眼看房顶往下塌，便从窗户往街上跳。另一些人连爬带滚地下了楼梯。隔板烧着了，四下里一片哭号。疯子们在笼子里像野兽似的吼叫。许多疯子逃了出来，在回廊里又是笑又是舞，做出千奇百怪的样子，瞧着叫人胆寒。他们上了街，就像过狂欢节似的；有一个爬上科索大街（Coso）的十字架，就发表起演说来，说自己是埃布罗河（Ebro），要从城里漫过去，把大火浇灭。女人们跑去照应病人，病人全给抬到了皮拉尔圣母（Del Pilar）和塞奥（La Seo）。街上简直没法穿行。每有炸弹飞来，新塔（Torre Nueva）上就打出信号，可人声鼎沸，把钟声埋住了，谁也听不见。法军顺着这条圣恩格拉西亚街往前推进。他们占据了医院和圣方济各修道院（Convent of San Francisco）。战斗在科索大街区和周围的街巷里打响。唐·圣地亚哥·萨斯（Don Santiago Sas）、唐·马里亚诺·塞雷索、唐·洛伦索·卡尔沃（Don Lorenzo Calvo）、唐·马科斯·西莫诺（Don Marcos Simono）、雷诺瓦莱斯（Renovales）、马丁·阿尔万托斯（Martin Albantos）、维森特·科德（Vicente Codé）、唐·维森特·马拉科（Don Vicente Marraco），还有好些别的人，都奋不顾身地向法军扑去。而在一道她自己亲手垒起的街垒后头，怒气冲天、手里端着枪等着法军的，正是布雷塔伯爵夫人（Countess de Bureta）。”

===Original===
“What a woman, a countess, making barricades and firing guns!” cried Don Roque, enthusiastically.

“You did not know it?” he returned. “Well, where do you live? The Señora Maria Consolación Azlor y Villavicencio, who lives near the Ecce Homo, also walked through the streets, saying words of good cheer to those who were discouraged. Afterwards she made them close the entrance to the street, and herself took the lead of a party of peasants, crying, ‘Here we will all die before we will let them pass!’ ”

“Oh, what sublime heroism!” exclaimed Don Roque, yawning with hunger. “How much I should enjoy hearing those tales of heroism told on a full stomach! So you say that the house of Don José is to be found﻿—”
===Chinese===
“多了不起的女人！一位伯爵夫人，垒街垒，放火枪！”唐·罗克兴高采烈地喊道。

“这个你们竟不知道？”他反问道。“那你们到底是打哪儿来的？住在埃切·奥莫（Ecce Homo）附近的玛丽亚·孔索拉西翁·阿斯洛尔-比利亚维森西奥（María Consolación Azlor y Villavicencio）夫人，也走遍了各条街，给泄了气的人说宽心鼓劲的话。后来她叫人把街口堵死，亲自领着一队乡民，喊道：‘我们就在这儿死个干净，也决不放他们过去！’”

“哦，何等崇高的英雄主义！”唐·罗克喊道，饿得直打呵欠。“这些英雄事迹，要是填饱了肚子再听，该有多受用！那么您是说，唐·何塞的宅子找得到——”

===Original===
“It is just around there,” said the cripple. “You know already that the French had entangled themselves and stuck fast near the Arch of Cineja. Holy Virgin del Pilar, but that was where they killed off the French! The rest of the day was nothing beside it. In the Calle de la Parra and the Square of Estrevedes, in the Calles de los Urreas, Santa Fe, and Del Azoque, the peasants cut the French to pieces. The cannonading and the roar of that day still ring in my ears. The French burned down the houses that they could not defend, and the Saragossans did the same. There was firing on every side. Men, women, and children﻿—it was enough to have two hands to fight against the enemy. And you did not see it? You really have seen nothing at all! Well, as I was saying, Palafox came out of Saragossa towards﻿—”
===Chinese===
“就在那一带，”残疾人说。“您已经知道了，法军在西内哈拱门（Arch of Cineja）附近陷住了阵脚，动弹不得。神圣的皮拉尔圣母啊，就在那儿，法国佬可叫杀了个够！那天余下的事跟这一比都不算什么。在帕拉街和埃斯特雷维德斯广场（Square of Estrevedes），在乌雷亚斯街、圣菲街和阿索克街（Calles de los Urreas, Santa Fe, Del Azoque），乡民们把法国佬剁了个稀烂。那一天的炮声和喧嚣，至今还在我耳朵里响。法国佬放火烧了他们守不住的房子，萨拉戈萨人也照样烧。四面八方都在开火。男人、女人、孩子——只消有两只手，就能跟敌人打。你们竟没瞧见？你们真是什么也没瞧见过！好，我正说呢，帕拉福克斯（Palafox）出了萨拉戈萨城，朝——”

===Original===
“That’s enough, my friend,” said Don Roque, losing patience. “We are charmed with your conversation; but if you can take us this instant to the house of my friend, or direct us so that we can find it, we will go along.”

“In a minute, gentlemen. Don’t hurry,” replied Sursum Corda, starting off in advance with all the agility of which his crutches were capable. “Let us go there. Let us go, with all my heart. Do you see this house? Well, here lives Antonio Laste, first sergeant of the Fourth Company of Regulars, and you must know he saved from the treasury sixteen thousand, four hundred pesos, and took from the French the candles that they stole from the church.”

“Go on ahead, go on, friend,” I said, seeing that this indefatigable talker intended stopping to give all the details of the heroism of Antonio Laste.
===Chinese===
“够了，我的朋友，”唐·罗克说，耐心到了头。“同您聊天我们快活得很；可您要是能马上带我们到我朋友家去，或是指条道儿叫我们找得着，我们这就走。”

“这就来，先生们，别忙，”Sursum Corda 答道，凭着那双拐杖所能施展的全部敏捷，抢先上了路。“咱们上那儿去。走吧，我打心眼里愿意。你们瞧见这座房子没有？喏，这儿住着安东尼奥·拉斯特（Antonio Laste），正规军第四连的一等中士，您总得知道，他从金库里救出了一万六千四百比索，还把法国佬从教堂里偷走的蜡烛夺了回来。”

“您头里走，走吧，朋友，”我说，眼见这位不知疲倦的健谈家又要停下脚步，把安东尼奥·拉斯特的英勇事迹原原本本细讲一遍。

===Original===
“We shall arrive soon,” replied Sursum; “on the morning of the first of July I was going past here, when I encountered Hilario Lafuente, first corporal of fusileers of the Parish of Sas, and he said to me, ‘Today they are going to attack the Portillo;’ then I went to see what there was to see and﻿—”

“We know all about this, already,” said Don Roque. “Let us go on fast. We can talk afterwards.”

“This house which you see here burned down and in ruins,” continued the cripple, going around a corner, “is the one that burned on the fourth, when Don Francisco Ipas, sublieutenant of the Second Company of fusileers of the parish of San Pablo, stood here with a cannon, and these﻿—”
===Chinese===
“快到了，”Sursum 答道；“七月一日早上我打这儿经过，迎面撞见伊拉里奥·拉富恩特（Hilario Lafuente），萨斯教区（Parish of Sas）燧发枪手的一等下士，他对我说：‘今天他们要攻打波蒂略门；’我就去瞧瞧有什么可瞧的，后来——”

“这些我们全知道了，”唐·罗克说。“快走吧。回头再聊。”

“你们瞧这边这座烧成废墟的房子，”残疾人拐过街角，接着往下讲，“就是四号那天烧掉的，那天唐·弗朗西斯科·伊帕斯（Don Francisco Ipas），圣巴勃罗教区燧发枪手第二连的少尉，带着一门炮守在这儿，还有这些——”

===Original===
“We know the rest, my good man,” said Don Roque. “Forward, march! and the faster the better.”

“But much better was what Codé did, the farmer of the parish of La Magdalena, with the cannon of the Calle de la Parra,” persisted the beggar, stopping once more. “When he was going to fire the gun, the French surrounded him, everybody ran away; but Codé got under the cannon, and the French passed by without seeing him. Afterwards, helped by an old woman who brought him some rope, he pulled that big piece of artillery as far as the entrance of the street. Come, I will show you!”

“No, no, we don’t want to see a thing. Go along ahead.”
===Chinese===
“往下我们都知道了，我的好人，”唐·罗克说。“开步——走！越快越好！”

“可是科德干的那一手才叫精彩呢，”乞丐偏不罢休，又站住了，“就是拉马格达莱纳教区（La Magdalena）的那个庄稼汉，使的是帕拉街的那门炮。他正要开炮，法军把他围住了，人全跑光了；可科德钻到了炮底下，法军打旁边过，愣是没瞧见他。后来多亏一个老婆婆给他送来绳子，他把那门大家伙一路拖到了街口上。来，我指给你们看！”

“不，不，我们什么也不想看。您头里走吧。”

===Original===
We kept at him, and closed our ears to his tales with so much obstinacy, that at last, although very slowly, he took us through the Coso and the Market to the Calle de la Hilarza, the street wherein stood the house of the person whom we were seeking.
===Chinese===
我们就这样一个劲儿催他，铁了心不听他的故事，他终于——尽管慢得出奇——领我们穿过科索大街和市场，来到伊拉尔萨街（Calle de la Hilarza），我们要寻的那个人，宅子就在这条街上。

## III / 第三章

===Original===

But, alas! Don José de Montoria was not in his house, and we found it necessary to go a little way out of the city to look for him. Two of my companions, tired of so much going and coming, left us with the idea of trying on their own account for some military or civil situation. Don Roque and myself therefore started with less embarrassment on our trip to the country house, the “*torre*,” of our friend. (They call country houses *torres* at Saragossa.) This was situated to the westward of the town; the place bordered on the Muela road, and was at a short distance from the Bernardona road. Such a long tramp was not at all the right thing for our tired bodies, but necessity obliged us to take this inopportune exercise. We were very well treated when we at last met the longed-for Saragossan and became the objects of his cordial hospitality.

Montoria was occupied, when we arrived, in cutting down olive-trees on his place, a proceeding demanded by the military exigencies of the plan of defence established by the officers in the field, because of the possibility of a second siege. And it was not our friend alone who destroyed with his own hands this heritage of his hacienda. All the proprietors of the surrounding places occupied themselves with the same task, and they directed the work of devastation with as much coolness as if they were watering or replanting, or busy with the grape harvest.

Montoria said to us, “In the first siege I cut down my trees on my property on the other side of the Huerva; but this second siege that is being prepared for us is going to be much more terrible, to judge by the great number of troops that the French are sending.”

===Chinese===

唉，可惜！唐·何塞·德·蒙特里亚并不在家，我们只好出城走上一小段路去找他。我的两位同伴厌倦了这来回奔波，便离开了我们，打算各自去谋个军职或文职。于是唐·罗克和我两人落得轻省，动身前往我们朋友的乡间宅邸“托雷”（torre）。（在萨拉戈萨，人们把乡间宅邸叫作“托雷”（torres）。）宅子坐落在城西，地界毗邻穆埃拉大道（Muela），离贝尔纳多纳大道（Bernardona）也不远。这样一趟长途跋涉，对我们疲惫的身子实在不合适，但迫于无奈，也只得受用这不合时宜的锻炼。等我们终于见到这位渴慕已久的萨拉戈萨人、置身于他热忱的款待之中时，真是受到了极好的礼遇。

我们到达时，蒙特里亚正忙着砍伐自家地里的橄榄树——这是野战军官们制定的防御计划出于军事急需所要求的举措，为的是防备可能到来的第二次围城。亲手毁掉自家庄园这份祖业的，还不止我们这位朋友一人。周围各处田庄的主人们都忙于同样的活计，他们指挥这场摧毁作业时的镇定从容，仿佛不过是在浇水、补种，或是忙葡萄收获。

蒙特里亚对我们说：“第一次围城时，我砍的是韦尔瓦河（Huerva）对岸我产业上的树；可眼下人家正给我们张罗的这第二次围城，瞧法国人开来的大批人马，怕是要可怕得多了。”

===Original===

We told him the story of the surrender of Madrid, and as this seemed to depress him very much, we praised the deeds done at Saragossa between the fifteenth of June and the fourteenth of August with all sorts of grandiloquent phrases. Shrugging his shoulders, Don José said, “All that was possible to be done was done.”

At this point Don Roque began to make personal eulogies of me, both military and civil, and he overdrew the picture so much that he made me blush, particularly as some of his announcements were stupendous lies. He said, first, that I belonged to one of the highest families of lower Andalusia, and that I was present as one of the marine guards at the glorious battle of Trafalgar. He said that the junta had made me a great offer of a concession in Peru, and that during the siege of Madrid I had performed prodigies of valor at the Puerta de los Pozos, my courage being so great that the French found it convenient after the capitulation to rid themselves of such a fearful foe, sending me with other Spanish patriots to France. He added that my ingenuity had made possible the escape of us four companions who had taken refuge in Saragossa, and ended his panegyric by assuring Don José that for my personal qualities also I deserved distinguished consideration.

Meantime Montoria surveyed me from head to foot, and if he observed the bad cut of my clothes and their many rents, he must also have seen that they were of the kind used by a man of quality, revealing his fine, courtly, and aristocratic origin by the multiplicity of their imperfections. After he had looked me over, he said to me, “*Porra!* I shall not be able to enlist you in the third rank of the company of fusileers of Don Santiago Sas, of which I am captain, but you can enter the corps where my son is; and if you don’t wish to, you must leave Saragossa, because here we have no use for lazy men. And as for you, Don Roque, my friend, since you are not able to carry a gun, *porra*! we will make you one of the attendants in the army hospital.”

===Chinese===

我们给他讲了马德里投降的经过；这事似乎让他大为沮丧，于是我们搬出种种夸张的辞藻，盛赞萨拉戈萨人在六月十五日到八月十四日之间建立的功勋。唐·何塞耸了耸肩，说道：“能做的，都做了。”

说到这里，唐·罗克开始对我本人夸赞起来，军功民政一齐上，而且把话说得如此过头，听得我面红耳赤——尤其因为他讲的有些话简直是惊天动地的谎言。他说，首先，我出身下安达卢西亚数一数二的高门世家，还曾以海军见习军官（marine guards）的身份，亲历了光荣的特拉法尔加海战。他说，议会曾向我提出优厚的许诺，要把秘鲁的一项特许权益给我；又说马德里被围期间，我在波索斯门（Puerta de los Pozos）屡建神勇奇功，我的胆气如此之大，以至法国人觉得，投降协定签订之后还是除掉我这个可怕的敌手为妥，便把我同其他西班牙爱国者一道解往法国。他还说，我们四个避难到萨拉戈萨的同伴之所以能够脱身，全亏了我的机巧；最后他向唐·何塞保证，单凭我个人的品性，也当得起特别的器重——这才给他的颂词收了尾。

这当儿，蒙特里亚把我从头到脚打量了一番；他固然看得出我那身衣服剪裁拙劣、处处开裂，想必也看得出那本是体面人穿的衣装——恰恰凭着这重重叠叠的破损，透露出主人精致的、雍容的、贵族式的出身。他把我端详过后，对我说：“*波拉*！（porra——西班牙语咒骂语，本义‘棍棒’）我没法把你编进圣地亚哥·萨斯燧发枪手连的第三排，我正是那连的上尉；不过你可以进我儿子所在的部队；你要是不愿意，那就得离开萨拉戈萨，因为我们这儿用不着懒汉。至于你，我的朋友唐·罗克，既然你扛不动枪，*波拉*！我们就安排你去军医院当一名看护。”

===Original===

When Don Roque had heard all this, he managed to express, by means of rhetorical circumlocution and graceful ellipses, the great necessity of a piece of meat for each one of us, and a couple of loaves of bread apiece. Then we saw the great Montoria scowl, looking at us so severely that he made us tremble, fearing that we were to be sent away for daring to ask for something to eat. We murmured timid excuses, and then our protector, very red in the face, spoke as follows﻿—

“Is it possible that you are hungry? *Porra!* Go to the devil with a hundred thousand *porras*! Why haven’t you said so before? Do I look like a man capable of letting my friends go hungry? *porra!* You must know that I always have a dozen hams hanging from the beams of my storeroom, and I have twenty casks of Rioja, yes, sir. And you are hungry, and you did not tell me so to my face without any roundabout fuss? That is an offence to a man like me. There, boys, go in and order them to cook four pounds of beef and six dozen eggs, and to kill six pullets, and bring from the wine-cellar seven jugs of wine. I want my breakfast, too. Let the neighbors come, the workmen, and my sons too, if they are anywhere about. And you, gentlemen, be prepared to punish it all with my compliments, *porra*! You will eat what there is without thanking me. We do not use compliments here. You, Señor Don Roque, and you, Señor Don Araceli, are in your own house today, *porra*! tomorrow and always, *porra*! Don José de Montoria is a true friend to his friends. All that he has, all that he owns, belongs to his friends.”

The brusque hospitality of the worthy man astonished us. As he did not receive our compliments with good grace, we decided to leave aside the artificial formalities of the court, and, assuredly, the most primitive fashions reigned during the breakfast.

===Chinese===

唐·罗克听罢，便借助修辞上的迂回铺陈和得体的省略，委婉地道出了这样一件迫在眉睫的大事：我们每人急需一块肉，还得各配两个面包。只见大名鼎鼎的蒙特里亚眉头一拧，狠狠地瞪着我们，瞪得我们直打颤，生怕就为敢讨口吃的而被撵走。我们怯生生地小声赔着不是，这时，我们的庇护人涨红了脸，如此说道——

“你们居然是饿了？*波拉*！带上十万个*波拉*见鬼去吧！怎么不早说？我像是那种能让朋友挨饿的人吗？*波拉*！你们要知道，我储藏室的房梁上常年挂着一打火腿，还有二十桶里奥哈（Rioja）美酒，千真万确，先生。你们饿了，居然不当着我的面直截了当地说，偏要这么绕来绕去？对我这样的人，这简直是种冒犯。去，孩子们，进屋去，吩咐他们煮四磅牛肉、六打鸡蛋，宰六只小母鸡，再从酒窖里取七壶酒来。我自己的早饭也要。叫邻居们来，工匠们来，我那几个儿子，只要在近处，也都叫来。你们呢，诸位，准备把这些统统‘惩办’掉，承我的情，*波拉*！有什么就吃什么，不必谢我。我们这儿不兴客套。你，唐·罗克先生，还有你，唐·阿拉塞利先生，今天就是在自己家里，*波拉*！明天也是，永远是，*波拉*！唐·何塞·德·蒙特里亚对朋友才是真朋友。他的一切，他的全部家当，都属于他的朋友。”

这位可敬之人粗豪直率的待客之道令我们大为惊讶。他既不肯和颜悦色地领受我们的道谢，我们便决定把宫廷里那套矫饰的虚礼搁置一旁；千真万确，早餐席上奉行的是最原始的规矩。

===Original===

“Why don’t you eat more?” Don José asked me. “It seems to me that you are one of these compliment-makers who expect to live on compliments. I don’t like that sort of thing, my young gentleman. I find it very trying, and I am going to beat you with a stick to make you eat. There, despatch this glass of wine! Did you find any better at court? Not by a long way. Come now, drink, *porra*! or we shall come to blows.” All this made me eat and drink more than was good for me; but it was necessary to respond to the generous cordiality of Montoria, and too it was not worth while to lose his good will for one indigestion more or less.

After the breakfast, the work of cutting down trees was continued, and the rich farmer directed it as if it were a festival performance.

“We will see,” he said, “if this time they will dare to attack the Castle. Have you not seen the works that we have built? They will find it a very complicated task to take them. I have just given two hundred bales of wool, a mere nothing, and I would give my last crust.”

===Chinese===

“你怎么不再多吃点？”唐·何塞问我，“依我看，你就是那种指望靠客套话过活的客套匠。这种做派我不喜欢，我的少爷先生。这让我很难受，我要拿棍子揍你，逼你吃。来，把这杯酒打发掉！宫里能找到比这更好的吗？差得远呢。来，喝，*波拉*！不然我们可要动拳头了。”这一番话害得我吃喝得过了量；可蒙特里亚的慷慨热忱不能不报答，再说，为了一两回消化不良就失掉他的好感，也不值得。

早饭之后，砍树的活计继续进行，这位富有的庄主指挥起来，就像在操办一场节庆演出。

“我们倒要看看，”他说，“这一回他们还敢不敢来攻打城堡。你们没见过我们修的工事吧？他们要想夺取，会发现那是桩顶棘手的差事。我刚捐了两百包羊毛，区区之数，不值一提；就是要我交出最后一块面包皮，我也舍得。”

===Original===

When we returned to the town, Montoria took us to look over the defensive works that were built in the western part of the city. There was in the Portillo gate a large semicircular battery that joined the walls of the Convent de los Fecetas with those of the Augustine friars’ convent. From this building to the Convent of the Trinitarios extended a straight wall, with battlements along all its length and with a good pathway in the centre. This was protected by a deep moat that reached to the famous field of Las Eras, scene of the heroic deeds of the fifteenth of June. Further north, towards the Puerta Sancho, which protected the breastworks of the Ebro, the fortifications continued, terminated by a tower. All these works, constructed in haste, though intelligently, were not distinguished by their solidity. Any one of the enemy’s generals, ignorant of the events of the first siege, and of the immense moral force of the Saragossans, would have laughed at those piles of earth as fortifications offering material for an easy siege. But God ordains that somebody must escape once in a while the physical laws that rule war. Saragossa, compared with Amberes, Dantzig, Metz, Sebastopol, Cartagena, Gibraltar, and other famous strongholds, was like a fortress made of cardboard.

And yet﻿—!

===Chinese===

我们回到城里之后，蒙特里亚带我们去察看城西修筑的防御工事。波蒂略门处有一座巨大的半圆形炮台，将费塞塔斯修道院（Convent de los Fecetas）的院墙与奥古斯丁会修士修道院的院墙连成一体。从这座建筑到三一会修道院（Trinitarios），横亘着一道直墙，沿墙全长筑着垛口，正中辟有一条像样的甬道。甬道外护着一道深壕，一直延伸到著名的拉斯埃拉斯田野（Las Eras），那是六月十五日种种英雄事迹的发生之地。再往北，朝圣乔门方向——圣乔门拱卫着埃布罗河沿岸的胸墙——工事继续延伸，末端是一座塔楼。所有这些工事虽系仓促筑成，倒也颇见章法，只是坚固不足。敌军将领若对第一次围城的种种事变一无所知，对萨拉戈萨人巨大的精神力量也一无所知，准会耻笑这些土垒，只当是供一场轻松围攻取用的材料。然而上帝有旨：总得有人时不时逃脱那支配战争的物理法则。萨拉戈萨若拿去同安特卫普、但泽、梅斯、塞瓦斯托波尔、卡塔赫纳、直布罗陀等著名的要塞相比，简直就是一座纸板糊成的堡垒。

然而——！

## IV / 第四章

===Original===
Before we left his house, Montoria became vexed at Don Roque and me because we would not take the money that he offered us for our first expenses in the city; then were repeated the blows on the table, and the rains of “*porras*” and other words that I will not repeat. But at last we arrived at an arrangement honorable for both parties.

And now I begin to think I am saying too much about this singular man before I describe his personality. Don José was a man of about sixty years of age, strong, high-colored, of overflowing health, well placed in the world, contented with himself, fulfilling his destiny with a quiet conscience. His was an excess of patriotic virtues and of exemplary customs, if there can be an excess of such things. He was lacking in education, that is to say, in the finer and more distinguished training which in that time some of the sons of such families as his were beginning to receive. Don José was not acquainted with the superficialities of etiquette, and by character and custom was opposed to the amenities and the white lies which are a part of the foundations of courtesy. As he always wore his heart upon his sleeve, he wished everybody to do the same, and his savage goodness tolerated none of the frequent evasions of polite conversation. In angry moments he was impetuous, and let himself be carried to violent extremes, of which he always repented later. He never dissimulated, and had the great Christian virtues in a crude form and without polish, like a massive piece of the most beautiful marble where the chisel has traced no lines. It was necessary to know him to understand him, making allowances for his eccentricities, although to be sure he should scarcely be called eccentric, when he was so much like the majority of the men of his province.

His aim was never to hide what he felt; and if this occasionally caused him some trouble in the course of life in regard to questions of little moment, it was a quality which always proved an inestimable treasure in any grave matter, because, with his soul wholly on view, it was impossible to suspect any malice or any double dealing whatever. He readily pardoned offenders, obliged those who sought favors, and gave a large part of his numerous goods to those in need. He dressed neatly, ate abundantly, fasted with much scrupulousness during Lent, and loved the Virgin del Pilar with a fanatical sort of family affection.

===Chinese===
还没等我们离开他家，蒙特里亚就跟唐·罗克和我怄上了气，因为我们不肯收下他掏给我们作进城后头一笔开销的钱；于是拍桌子声再度大作，“*波拉*”如骤雨般劈头盖脸，还夹着别的字眼，我就不复述了。不过到最后，我们总算达成了一项保全双方面子的安排。

写到这里我才发觉，还没描画这位奇人的品貌，我已经把他讲得太多了。唐·何塞年约六十，身子骨结实，面色红润，健康之气几乎要溢出体外；家道殷实，于世安稳，对自己心满意足，怀着一颗安宁的良心践行着自己的天命。他的爱国德性与模范操行多得近乎过剩——倘若这类东西也会“过剩”的话。他欠缺教育，确切地说，欠缺那种更精细、更上等的陶冶；在那个年头，像他这样人家的子弟，有的已经开始受这种陶冶了。唐·何塞不通礼节上的种种虚文，而且无论禀性还是习惯，都同社交场里的那些体面客套势不两立——须知那些温婉的应酬、无伤大雅的场面谎，本是人间礼数的根基之一。他自己素来把心挂在袖口上，也巴不得人人都这样，于是他那股子未加驯化的善良，便容不得文雅交谈里司空见惯的种种躲闪迂回。动起怒来他性如烈火，任着性子冲到暴烈的极端，事后却总是悔恨。他从不作伪，一身基督徒的大德还是粗坯，未经打磨，好比一块最华美的大理石，凿子尚未在上面划过一道线。要懂他，须先结识他，对他的乖僻多加担待；不过说句实话，他也实在算不上乖僻，因为他同本省大多数男人原本就是一个模样。

他立身处世，从不刻意掩藏自己的感受；这在日常无关紧要的小事上偶尔给他招来些麻烦，可一逢重大关头，这份品性却总是无价之宝——因为他的灵魂整个儿摆在明处，任谁也无法疑心他怀有什么恶意、耍什么两面手腕。冒犯他的人，他轻易就宽恕；有求于他的人，他慨然相助；偌大家私的一大部分，他都散给了穷乏之人。他衣着整洁，食量豪宏，大斋期斋戒得一丝不苟，对皮拉尔圣母更怀着一近乎狂热的、家人般的挚爱。

===Original===
His language was not, as we have shown, a model of elegance, and he himself confessed, as the greatest of his defects, the habit of saying *porra* every minute, and again, *porra*! without the slightest necessity. But more than once I heard him say, knowing his fault, he had not been able to correct it, for the *porras* came out of his mouth without his knowing it.

Don José had a wife and three children. She was Doña Leocadia Sarriera, by birth a Navarraise. The eldest son and the daughter were married, and had given grandchildren to the old man. The younger son was called Augustine, and was destined for the church, like his uncle of the same name, the Archdeacon of La Seo. I made the acquaintance of all these on the same day, and found them the best people in the world. I was treated with so much kindness that I was overwhelmed by their generosity. If they had known me since my birth, they could not have been more cordial. Their kindness, springing spontaneously from their generous hearts, touched my very soul; and as I have always had a faculty for letting people love me, I responded from the first with a very sincere affection.

“Señor Don Roque,” I said that night to my friend as we were going to bed in the room which was given us, “I have never seen people like these. Is everybody in Aragón like this?”

“There are all kinds,” he answered; “but men made of stuff like Don José and his family are plentiful in this land of Aragón.”

===Chinese===
前文已经说过，他的言谈算不得典雅的典范；他自己也供认不讳：他最大的毛病，就是每隔一分钟便要骂一声 *波拉*，跟着又是一声 *波拉*！全无半点必要。可我不止一次听他亲口说，他明知这个毛病，却怎么也改不掉，因为那些 *波拉* 总是不知不觉便从他嘴里溜了出来。

唐·何塞有一位太太、三个子女。太太是堂娜·莱奥卡迪娅·萨列拉（Doña Leocadia Sarriera），本是纳瓦拉（Navarre）人。长子与女儿都已成婚，给老人添了孙辈。次子名叫奥古斯丁，注定要献身教会，与那位同他同名同姓的叔父——塞奥的副主教——走的是同一条路。这一家人，我都在同一天里结识了，而且发现他们是天底下顶好的人。他们待我那样恩厚，那份慷慨简直压得我喘不过气。哪怕他们从我落生那天就认识我，也不过如此热诚了。他们的好意发乎慷慨的天性，自然流露，一直沁到我魂灵深处；而我素来便有一份招人疼爱的本事，也就从第一天起报以一片至诚。

当天夜里，在我们分到的那间屋子里准备就寝时，我对朋友说：“唐·罗克先生，我从没见过这样的人家。阿拉贡人人都是这般模样吗？”

“什么样的人都有，”他答道，“不过像唐·何塞和他一家人这种材质的人，在阿拉贡这片土地上可多得很。”

===Original===
Next day we occupied ourselves with my enlistment. The spirit of the men who were enlisting filled me with such enthusiasm that nothing seemed to me so noble as to follow glory, even afar off. Everybody knows that in those days Saragossa and the Saragossans had obtained a fabulous renown, that their heroism stimulated the imagination. Everything referring to the famous siege of the immortal city partook on the lips of narrators of the proportions and colors of the heroic age. With distance, the actions of the Saragossans acquired great dimensions. In England and Germany, where they were considered the Numantines of modern times, those half-naked peasants, with rope sandals on their feet and the bright Saragossan kerchief on their heads, became like figures of mythology.

“Surrender, and we will give you clothes,” said the French in the first siege, admiring the constancy of a few poor countrymen dressed in rags.

“*We do not know how to surrender*,” they made answer; “*and our bodies shall be clothed with glory.*”

The fame of this and other phrases has gone round the world.

===Chinese===
第二天，我们便张罗起我投军的事。应征入伍的人们那股子热气，把我烧得满腔激情，只觉得天底下再没有比追随荣耀——哪怕远远地追随——更高尚的事了。谁都知道，那些日子里，萨拉戈萨和萨拉戈萨人已经赢得了神话般的声名，他们的英雄事迹激荡着人们的想象。凡与这座不朽之城那场著名围城相关的一切，自讲述者口中道出，都带上了英雄时代的规模与色彩。距离一远，萨拉戈萨人的壮举便愈发显得宏大。在英国和德意志，人们把他们视为当代的努曼西亚人（Numantines，古西班牙抗罗马、死守孤城的英雄）；那些半裸着身子的庄稼汉，脚上蹬着麻绳凉鞋，头上扎着鲜艳的萨拉戈萨头巾，俨然已化作神话里的人物。

第一次围城时，法军眼见几个衣衫褴褛的贫苦乡民这般坚贞，不胜钦佩，便喊话说：“投降吧，我们给你们衣裳穿。”

他们回答道：“我们不懂怎样投降；我们的躯体，将以荣耀为衣裳。”

这一句以及另外几句豪言的名声，早已传遍全世界。

===Original===
But let us go back to my enlistment. There was an obstacle in the way, Palafox’s manifesto of the thirteenth of December, in which he ordered the expulsion of all strangers within a period of twenty-four hours. This measure was taken on account of the numbers of people who made trouble, and stirred up discord and disorder; but just at the time of my arrival another order was given out, calling for all the scattered soldiers of the Army of the Centre which had been dispersed at Tudela, and so I found a chance to enlist. Although I did not belong to that army, I had taken a place in the defence of Madrid and the battle of Bailén. These were reasons which, with the help of my protector Montoria, served me in entering the Saragossan forces. They gave me a place in the battalion of volunteers of the Peñas of San Pedro, which had been badly weakened in the first siege, and I received a uniform and a gun. I did not enter the lines, as my protector had said, in the company of the clergyman of Santiago Sas, because this valiant company was composed exclusively of residents of the parish of San Pablo. They did not want any young men in their battalion; for this reason Augustine Montoria himself, Don José’s son, could not serve under the Sas banner, and enlisted like myself in the battalion of Las Peñas de San Pedro. Good luck bestowed upon me a good companion and an excellent friend.

From the day of my arrival I had heard talk of the approach of the French army; but it was not an incontrovertible fact until the twentieth. In the afternoon a division arrived at Zuera, on the left bank of the river, to threaten the suburb; another, commanded by Suchet, encamped on the right above San Lamberto. Marshal Moncey, who was the general in command, placed himself, with three divisions, near the canal, and on both sides of the Huerva. Forty thousand men besieged us.

It is known that the French, impatient to defeat us, began operations early on the twenty-first, attacking simultaneously and with great vigor Monte Torrero, and the Arrabal, the suburb on the left of the Ebro, points without whose possession it was impossible to dream of conquering the valiant city. But if we were obliged to abandon Torrero on account of the danger of its defence, Saragossa displayed in the suburb such audacious courage that that day is known as one of the most brilliant of all her brilliant history.

From four o’clock, from day-dawn, the battalion of Las Peñas de San Pedro guarded the front of the fortifications, from Santa Engracia to the Convent of Trinitarios, a line which seemed the least exposed in all the circuit of the city. Behind Santa Engracia was established the battery of Los Mártires; from there ran the battlements of the wall as far as the Huerva bridge, defended by a barricade; it deflected afterwards towards the west, making an obtuse angle, and joining another redoubt built in the Torre del Pino; it continued in a straight line as far as the Convent of Trinitarios, and enclosed the Puerta del Carmen.

===Chinese===
闲话少叙，还是回到我投军的事上来。半路上横着一个障碍：帕拉福克斯十二月十三日的告示，限令二十四小时之内将一切外来人等驱逐出城。此举是因为生事捣乱、挑动嫌隙、搅扰治安的人实在太多；可不巧正赶上我进城的时候，又颁下另一道命令，召集在图德拉（Tudela）溃散的中央军团（Army of the Centre）的散兵，我便得了应募的机会。我虽不属于那支军队，却参加过马德里保卫战和拜伦（Bailén）战役。凭这些资历，再加上我的保护人蒙特里亚从中照应，我终于进了萨拉戈萨的行伍。他们把我安置进圣佩德罗岩（Las Peñas de San Pedro）志愿兵营——这个营在第一次围城中折损惨重——我还领到一套军装和一支枪。我并没有像我那位保护人先前说的那样，编入圣地亚哥·萨斯神父的连队，因为那支英勇的队伍清一色由圣巴勃罗教区的居民组成。他们营里不收年轻后生；为此，连唐·何塞的儿子奥古斯丁·蒙特里亚本人，也没能在萨斯的旗帜下效力，同我一样进了圣佩德罗岩营。好运赐了我一位好同伴、一位难得的挚友。

从我进城那天起，便听人纷纷议论法军将至；可直到二十日，此事才算铁板钉钉。当天下午，法军一个师开抵河左岸的苏埃拉（Zuera），威胁着城郊；另一个师由絮歇（Suchet）统带，在右岸圣兰贝托（San Lamberto）上游一带扎营。总司令蒙塞元帅（Marshal Moncey）亲率三个师，进驻运河近旁、韦尔瓦河两岸。四万兵马，把我们围了个严实。

众所周知，法军急不可耐地要击垮我们，二十一日一清早便动手开战，同时以雷霆之势猛攻托雷罗山（Monte Torrero）与阿拉巴尔（Arrabal）——埃布罗河左岸的那片郊区；不先夺下这两处，梦想征服这座英勇之城便无从谈起。托雷罗山因防守艰险，我们只得放弃；可在郊区一役，萨拉戈萨展现出的勇悍气概，使这一天跻身她全部煌煌历史上最光辉的日子之一。

从凌晨四点、天光初破时分起，圣佩德罗岩营便扼守着防线正面，从圣恩格拉西亚直到三一会修道院——在全城环围各段之中，这一线似乎最不吃紧。圣恩格拉西亚背后设着洛斯·马尔蒂雷斯炮台（Los Mártires）；城墙的雉堞自那里延伸至韦尔瓦河桥，由一道街垒扼守；随后城墙折向西行，转出一个钝角，与筑在皮诺塔的另一座棱堡相接；再成一直线延伸到三一会修道院，将卡门城门（Puerta del Carmen）围裹在内。

===Original===
Whoever has seen Saragossa can well understand my imperfect description, for the ruins of Santa Engracia still remain, and in the Puerta del Carmen may still be seen, not far from the Glorieta, its ruined architrave and worm-eaten stones.

We were, as I have said, occupying the position described, and part of the soldiers had a bivouac in a neighboring orchard, next to the Carmen college.

Augustine Montoria and I were inseparable. His serene character, the affection he showed me from the moment we met, and the inexplicable concord in our thoughts, made his company very agreeable. He was a young man of beautiful figure, with large brilliant eyes and open brow, and an expression marked by a melancholy gravity. His heart, like that of his father, was filled by generosity which overflowed at the least impulse; but he was not likely to wound the feelings of a friend, because education had taken from him a great deal of the national brusqueness. Augustine entered manhood’s estate with the security of a kind heart, firm and uncorrupted judgment, with a vigorous and healthy soul; the wide world only was the limit of his boundless goodness. These qualities were enriched by a brilliant imagination of sure and direct action, not like that of our modern geniuses, who most of the time do not know what they are about. Augustine’s imagination was lofty and serene, worthy of his education in the great classics. Although with a lively inclination to poetry﻿—for Augustine was a poet﻿—he had learned theology, showing ability in this as in everything. The fathers at the Seminary, who were fond of the youth, looked upon him as a prodigy in the sciences, human and divine, and they congratulated themselves on seeing him with one foot at least over the threshold of the Church.

The Montoria family had many a pleasant anticipation of the day when Augustine would say his first mass, as a holy event that was fast approaching. Yet﻿—I am obliged to say it﻿—Augustine had no vocation for the Church. Neither his family nor the good fathers of the Seminary understood this, nor would they have understood it, even if the Holy Spirit had come down in person to tell them. This precocious theologian, this humanist who had Horace at the ends of his fingers, this dialectician who in the weekly discussions astonished the fathers with intellectual gymnastics of scholastic science, had no more vocation for the Church than Mozart for war, Raphael for mathematics, or Napoleon for dancing!

===Chinese===
凡是见过萨拉戈萨的人，都不难会意于我这番粗陋的描述：圣恩格拉西亚的废墟至今犹存，而离格洛列塔（Glorieta）不远的卡门城门，那残破的门楣与虫蛀的石块也仍历历可见。

我已说过，我们占着上述阵地，一部分士兵在邻近一座果园里露营，紧挨着卡门学院。

奥古斯丁·蒙特里亚同我形影不离。他性情沉静，自我们相识那一刻起便待我亲厚，加之我们二人心思的契合奇妙得难以言喻，使他的陪伴令人极为熨帖。他是个身姿俊美的青年，眼睛大而明亮，额头开阔，神情带着一缕忧郁的庄重。他的心同他父亲的一样，盛满了稍一触动便要四溢的慷慨；但他不至于刺伤朋友的感情，因为教养已把他身上那种国民性的粗豪磨去了大半。奥古斯丁跨入成年时，怀着一颗善心的笃定、一份坚定而不受腐蚀的判断力，和一个强健而健全的灵魂；他那无边的善意，唯以广阔世界为界。这些资质又得了一副才华照人的想象力的滋养——那想象力行事笃定而直截，不像我们当今的天才们，多半时候连自己正干什么都不知晓。奥古斯丁的想象力高远而澄静，配得上他所受的伟大古典学问的教养。他虽怀着对诗歌的活泼倾慕——因为奥古斯丁是个诗人——学的却是神学，而且在这门学问上如同在一切事情上一样，都显露出才能。神学院里那些疼爱这青年的神父们，把他看作人间与神圣两门学问上的奇才，见他至少已有一只脚踏过教会的门槛，无不暗自庆幸。

蒙特里亚一家满怀欣悦，翘盼着奥古斯丁首祭弥撒那一天的到来，把那当作一件日渐临近的圣事。然而——我不得不说——奥古斯丁对教会并无圣召。这一点，他的家人不明白，神学院的好神父们也不明白；哪怕圣灵亲自降临、当面告知，他们照样不会明白。这位早慧的神学家，这位把贺拉斯（Horace）玩于指掌的人文学者，这位在每周论辩中用经院学问的智力体操令众神父叹服的论辩家，对教会的圣召，并不比莫扎特之于打仗、拉斐尔之于数学、拿破仑之于跳舞多出一分！

## V / 第五章

===Original===
“Gabriel,” he said to me one morning, “dost thou not feel like smashing something?”
“Augustine, dost thou not feel like smashing something?” I responded. It will be seen that we were “thee-ing” and “thou-ing” each other after three days’ acquaintance.
“Not very much,” he said, “suppose the first ball strikes us dead!”
“We shall die for our country, for Saragossa; and although posterity will not remember us, it is always an honor to fall on the field of battle for a cause like this.”
“You are right,” he answered sadly; “but it is a pity to die. We are young. Who knows for what we are destined in life?”
“Life is a trifle, and its importance is not worth thinking of.”

===Chinese===
“加夫列尔，”一天早晨他对我说，“你就不觉得手痒，想砸点什么吗？”

“奥古斯丁，你就不觉得手痒，想砸点什么吗？”我应道。可见我们相识才三天，就已经彼此“你”呀“你”地相称，不见外了。

“不怎么想，”他说，“万一头一颗子弹就把我们撂倒了呢！”

“我们将为祖国、为萨拉戈萨而死；虽说后人不会记得我们，可为这样的事业战死沙场，终究是件光荣的事。”

“你说得对，”他黯然答道，“可是，死总归可惜。我们还年轻。谁知道老天这辈子给我们安排了什么呢？”

“性命是桩小事，它有多了不起，犯不着费神去想。”

===Original===
“That is for the aged to say, but not us who are just beginning to live. Frankly, I do not wish to die in this terrible circle which the French have drawn about us. In the other siege, however, all the students of the Seminary took arms, and I confess that I was more valiant then than now. A peculiar zeal filled my blood, and I threw myself into places of greatest danger without fear of death. Today does not find me the same. I am timid and afraid, and when a gun goes off, it makes me tremble.”
“That is natural. Fear does not exist when one does not realize the danger. As far as that is concerned, they say the most valiant soldiers are the raw recruits.”
“There is nothing in that. Indeed, Gabriel, I confess that the mere question of dying does not strike me as the greatest evil. But if I die, I am going to entrust you with a commission which I hope you will fulfil carefully like a good friend. Listen well to what I tell you. You see that tower that leans this way, as if to see what is passing here, or hear what we are saying?”
“The Torre Nueva? I see it. What charge are you going to give me for that lady?”
Day was breaking, and between the irregular-tiled roofs of the city, between the spires and minarets, the balconies and the cupolas of the churches, the Torre Nueva, old and unfinished, stood out distinctly.

===Chinese===
“那话该由上了年纪的人去说，不该由我们这些日子才开头的人说。老实讲，我不想死在法国人绕着我们四周画下的这道可怕的圈子里。不过，上一次围城那阵子，神学院的学生全都拿起了武器，我承认，那时的我比现在勇敢。一股异样的热忱灌满了我的血，我奋身往最危险的地方里闯，浑然不知怕死。今天的我可不一样了。我又胆小、又心慌，枪一响，我就发抖。”

“这很自然。人还没把危险放在心上的时候，恐惧根本不存在。要说这个，人家都讲，最勇敢的士兵恰恰是新兵。”

“那倒未必。说实话，加夫列尔，我承认，单是死这件事，在我看来算不得最大的灾祸。可我要是死了，有一桩托付要交给你，希望你像个好朋友似的，仔仔细细替我办到。把我这话听仔细了。你瞧见那座朝这边倾过来的塔了吧？它像是要看看这儿在出什么事，或是听听我们在说什么？”

“新塔？我瞧见了。你要为那位小姐交给我什么差事？”

天正破晓。在城市高高低低的瓦屋顶之间，在尖塔与宣礼楼之间，在教堂的露台与穹顶之间，那座上了年纪、未曾完工的新塔，清清楚楚地凸显出来。

===Original===
“Listen well!” said Augustine. “If I am killed with the first shot on this day which is now dawning, when the battle is ended, and they break ranks, you must go there.”
“To the Torre Nueva? Behold me! I arrive. I enter!”
“No, man, not enter. Listen, I will tell you. You arrive at the Plaza de San Felipe where the tower is. Look yonder! Do you see there near the great pile there is another tower, a little belfry? It seems like an acolyte before his lord the canon, which is the great tower.”
“Yes, now I see the altar-boy. And if I am not mistaken, it is the belfry of San Felipe. And the damned thing is ringing this minute!”
“For mass, it is ringing for mass,” said Augustine, with great emotion. “Do you not hear the cracked bell?”

===Chinese===
“听仔细了！”奥古斯丁说，“要是这正在放亮的这一天里，头一发枪弹就把我送了命，那么等仗打完、队伍解散之后，你必须上那儿去。”

“上新塔去？瞧我的！我到了。我进去啦！”

“不，老兄，不是进去。听着，我说给你听。你走到圣费利佩广场（Plaza de San Felipe），塔就在那儿。往那边瞧！看见那座大建筑旁边还有一座塔吗？一座小钟楼？它侍立在它主人座堂司铎的跟前，活像个辅祭——那座大塔才是司铎。”

“看见了，这会儿我认出那个辅祭来了。要是我没弄错，那是圣费利佩的钟楼。这该死的玩意儿这会儿正敲着钟呢！”

“敲弥撒钟呢，敲的是弥撒钟，”奥古斯丁说，情绪激动万分，“你难道听不见那口破裂的钟？”

===Original===
“Very plainly. Let us know what I have to say to this Mr. Altar-boy who is ringing the cracked bell.”
“No, no, it is nothing about him. You arrive at the Plaza of San Felipe. If you look at the belfry, you will see it is on a corner, and from this corner runs a narrow street. You enter there, and at the left you will find at a little distance another street, narrow and retired, called Antón Trillo. You follow this until you reach the back of the church. There you will see a house. You stop there﻿—”
“And then I come back again?”
“No; close to the house there is a garden, with a little gateway painted the color of chocolate. You stop there.”
“There I stop, and there I am!”

===Chinese===
“听得真真儿的。那就让我知道知道，该对这位敲着破钟的辅祭小先生说些什么吧。”

“不，不，跟他不相干。你到了圣费利佩广场。要是你细看那座钟楼，会瞧见它坐落在一个拐角上，打这个拐角伸出一条窄街。你打那儿进去，左手边走不多远，又是另一条街，又窄又背静，叫安东·特里约街（Antón Trillo）。你顺着它一直走到教堂背后。在那儿你会看见一座房子。你在那儿停下——”

“然后我就往回走？”

“不；房子近旁有座园子，有扇小园门，漆的是巧克力色。你在那儿停下。”

“我停下，我不就到地方了嘛！”

===Original===
“No, old man. You will see﻿—”
“You’re whiter than your shirt, my Augustine. What do all these towers and stoppages signify?”
“They mean,” continued my friend, with increasing embarrassment, “that in a little while you will be there. I desire you to go by night. All right, you arrive there. You stop. You wait a little, then you pass to the opposite sidewalk. You stretch your neck, and you will see a window over the wall of a garden. You pick up a pebble and throw it against the panes of glass lightly, to do little damage.”
“And in a second she will come!”
“No; have patience. How do you know whether she will come or not come?”
“Well, let us suppose that she comes.”

===Chinese===
“不，老伙计。你听着——”

“你比你身上这件衬衫还要白呢，我的奥古斯丁。又是这些塔啦，又是这些个‘停下’，到底都是什么意思？”

“意思是，”我的朋友接着说，越发地窘，“过不了多久，你人就要到那儿了。我要你夜里去。好，你到了那儿。你停下。稍等一会儿，再走到对面的人行道上去。你伸长脖子，就看见园墙上头有一扇窗。你捡一颗小石子，朝那玻璃窗上轻轻地扔，别砸坏多少东西。”

“转眼间她就出来啦！”

“不；沉住气。她来不来，你怎么知道？”

“好吧，那咱们就假定她来了。”

===Original===
“Before I tell you another thing, you must understand that it is there the goodman Candiola lives. Do you know who Candiola is? Well, he is a citizen of Saragossa, a man who, as they say, has in his house a cellar full of money. He is avaricious and a usurer, and when he lends he guts his customers. He knows more about debtors, laws, and foreclosures than the whole court and council of Castile. Whoever goes to law with him is lost.”
“From all this, the house with a gate painted chocolate color should be a magnificent palace.”
“Nothing of the sort. You will see a wretched-looking house that seems about to fall down. I tell you that that goodman Candiola is a miser. He does not waste a real that he can help. And if you should see him about here you would give him alms. I will tell you another thing; he is never seen in Saragossa, and they call him goodman Candiola in mockery and contempt. His name is Don Jerónimo de Candiola; he is a native of Mallorca, if I am not mistaken.”

===Chinese===
“在我告诉你别的之前，你先得弄明白，住在那儿的是老好人坎迪奥拉（Candiola）。你可知道坎迪奥拉是谁？嘿，他是萨拉戈萨的一个市民，据人家说，家里有一地窖的钱。他贪得无厌，是个放债的，放起债来能把主顾开膛破肚。论欠债人的底细、法律的门道、抵押没收的名堂，他比整个卡斯蒂利亚的法庭和议事会懂得还多。谁同他打官司，谁就完了。”

“照这么一说，这扇漆成巧克力色园门的宅子，该是一座了不得的府邸了。”

“根本不是那么回事。你会看见一座寒碜的房子，一副眼看要塌的样子。我告诉你，那位老好人坎迪奥拉是个吝啬鬼。能不破费的里亚尔，他一个也不乱花。你要是在这一带撞见他，你准会施舍他几个钱。我再告诉你一件事；他在萨拉戈萨从来不照面，人家叫他老好人坎迪奥拉，是拿这话嘲弄他、作贱他。他名叫唐·赫罗尼莫·德·坎迪奥拉（Don Jerónimo de Candiola）；要是我没记错，他老家在马略卡（Mallorca）。”

===Original===
“And this Candiola has a daughter?”
“Wait, man, how impatient you are! How do you know whether or not he has a daughter?” he answered, hiding his agitation by these evasions. “Well, as I was just going to tell you, Candiola is detested in the city for his great avarice and wicked heart. Many poor men has he put in prison after ruining them. Worse still, during the other siege he did not give a farthing for the war, nor take up arms, nor receive the wounded into his house, nor could they wring a peseta from him; and, as he said one day it was all one to him whether he gave to John or to Peter, he was on the point of being arrested.”
“Well, he is a pretty piece, this man of the house of the garden of the chocolate-colored gate! And what if when the pebble strikes the window, goodman Candiola comes out with a cudgel and gives me a good beating for flirting with his daughter?”
“Don’t be an idiot! Hush! You must know that as soon as it gets dark, Candiola shuts himself in an underground room, and there he stays counting his money until after midnight. Bah! He is well occupied now. The neighbors say they hear a muffled sound as if bags of coins were being tumbled out.”
“Very well. I arrive there. I throw the stone. She comes, and I tell her﻿—”

===Chinese===
“这位坎迪奥拉有个女儿？”

“等等，老兄，你这个人真是急！他有没有女儿，你怎么知道的？”他答道，拿这些支吾搪塞遮掩自己的心慌。“好吧，我正要告诉你：坎迪奥拉贪心大、心肠黑，城里人人都恨他。多少穷人叫他毁了家，又叫他送进牢里。更糟的是，上回围城，这场战争他一文钱不出，武器也不拿，伤兵也不往家里收，一个比塞塔（peseta）也别想从他身上榨出来；有一天他竟说，钱给约翰还是给彼得，横竖都是一回事，为这话他差点给抓起来。”

“好家伙，这扇巧克力色园门里宅子的主人，可真是个角儿！要是石子砸上窗子，老好人坎迪奥拉拎着根大棒子出来，为我招惹他女儿，结结实实揍我一顿，那可怎么好？”

“别犯傻！嘘！你该知道，天一擦黑，坎迪奥拉就把自己关进一间地下室，在那儿数他的钱，一直数过后半夜。呸！这会儿他正忙着呢。邻居们说，听得见那里头一阵闷响，像是一袋一袋的钱币往外倒。”

“很好。我到了那儿。我扔了石子。她出来了，我对她说——”

===Original===
“You tell her that I am dead. No, don’t be cruel; give her this amulet. No, tell her﻿—no, it will be better to tell her nothing.”
“Then I will give her the amulet?”
“By no means. Do not take the amulet to her.”
“Now, now I understand. As soon as she comes I am to say good night and march myself away singing, ‘The Virgin del Pilar says﻿—’ ”
“No, it is enough that she learns of my death. You must do as I tell you.”

===Chinese===
“你告诉她，我死了。不，别这么狠心；把这个护身符给她。不，告诉她——不，还是什么都不告诉她的好。”

“那我就把护身符给她？”

“万万不可。护身符不要带给她。”

“哦，哦，这下我懂了。她一出来，我就道一声晚安，自己哼着小曲儿开路：‘皮拉尔圣母说——’”

“不，让她知道我的死讯就够了。你必须照我吩咐的办。”

===Original===
“But if you don’t tell me anything.”
“How hasty you are! Wait. Perhaps they’ll not kill me today.”
“True. And what a bother about nothing!”
“There is one thing which I have left out, Gabriel, and I shall tell it to you frankly. I have had many, very many great desires to confide to you this secret which weighs upon my breast. To whom could I tell it but to you, my friend? If I did not tell you, my heart would break like a pomegranate. I have been greatly afraid of telling it at night in my dreams. Because of this fear I cannot sleep. If my father, my mother, my brother, suspected it, they would kill me.”

===Chinese===
“可你什么也没吩咐我呀。”

“你这个人真是急！等等。兴许今天他们还杀不成我呢。”

“倒也是。真是白操心一场！”

“有一件事我一直漏着没说，加夫列尔，我这就坦坦白白告诉你。这个压在我心口的秘密，我有多少回、多少回想向你吐露了。除了你，我的朋友，我还能讲给谁听？要是不讲给你听，我的心就会像石榴一样绽开。我一直怕得很，怕夜里说梦话把它讲了出来。就为这个怕，我睡不着觉。要是叫我父亲、我母亲、我哥哥起了疑心，他们会宰了我的。”

===Original===
“And the fathers at the Seminary?”
“Don’t name the fathers. You shall see. I will tell you what has befallen me. Do you know Father Rincón? Well, Father Rincón loves me very much, and every evening he used to make me come out for a walk by the river or towards Torrero or the Juslibol road. We would talk of theology and literature. Rincón is so enthusiastic about the great poet Horace that he used to say, ‘It is a pity that that man wasn’t a Christian so that he could be canonized.’ He always carries with him a little Elzevir, which he loves more than the apple of his eye. When we were tired walking, he would sit down and read, and between the two of us we would make whatever comments occurred to us. Well, now I will tell you that Father Rincón was a kinsman of Doña Maria Rincón, the deceased wife of Candiola, who has a little property in the Monzalbarba road, with a wretched little country house, more like a hut than a house, but embowered in leafy trees, and with delightful views of the Ebro. One afternoon, after we had been reading the *Quis multa gracilis te puer in rosa*, my teacher desired to visit his relative. We went there; we entered the garden, and Candiola was not there; but his daughter came to meet us, and Rincón said to her, ‘Mariquilla, get some peaches for this young man, and get me a glass of you know what.’ ”
“And is Mariquilla nice?”

===Chinese===
“神学院里那些神父呢？”

“别提那些神父。你听着。我要告诉你，我遭了什么事。你可认识林孔神父（Father Rincón）？嘿，林孔神父顶喜欢我，从前天天傍晚都要带我出来遛弯儿，沿着河边，要么往托雷罗（Torrero），要么往胡斯利沃尔大道（Juslibol）那头去。我们谈神学，谈文学。林孔对大诗人贺拉斯（Horace）热乎得了不得，常这么说：‘可惜那个人不是基督徒，不然都可以给他封圣。’他随身总揣着一小册埃尔泽维尔版（Elzevir），爱它赛过自己的眼珠子。走累了，他就坐下念，我们两个便想到什么品评什么。好，现在我告诉你，林孔神父是堂娜·玛丽亚·林孔（Doña Maria Rincón）的亲戚——她是坎迪奥拉的亡妻——在蒙萨尔巴尔瓦大道（Monzalbarba）上有点田产，带一座寒碜的小乡舍，与其说是房子，不如说是间窝棚，可绿树掩映，望出去埃布罗河的景致美极了。有一天下午，我们念完那首《玫瑰丛中，多少苗条的少年为你痴迷》（*Quis multa gracilis te puer in rosa*），我的老师要去探望他的亲戚。我们去了；进了园子，坎迪奥拉不在，他女儿迎上前来，林孔对她说：‘玛丽基亚（Mariquilla），去给这位少爷摘些桃子来，再给我倒一杯你知道的那个。’”

“那玛丽基亚长得俊吗？”

===Original===
“Don’t ask that. What if she is nice? You shall see. Father Rincón stroked his beard, and turning towards me said, ‘Augustine, confess that in your lifetime you have never seen a more perfect face than this one. Look at those eyes of fire, that angel’s mouth, and that bit of heaven for a brow.’ I was trembling, and Mariquilla laughed, her face all rosy red. Then Rincón continued, saying, ‘To you, who are a future father of the church, an example, a young pattern, without other passion than that for books, this divinity may show herself. Jove! admire here the admirable work of the Supreme Creator. Observe the expression of that face, the sweetness of those glances, the grace of that smile, the freshness, the delicacy of that complexion, the fineness of that skin, and confess that if heaven is beautiful, flowers, mountains, light, all the creations of God are nothing beside woman, the most perfect and finished work of the immortal hand.’ Thus spoke my teacher, and I, mute and astonished, did not cease to contemplate that master work which was certainly better than the *Aeneid*. I cannot tell you what I felt. Imagine the Ebro, that great river, which descends from its springs to give itself to the sea, all at once changing its channel and trying to run upward, returning to the Asturias. The same thing took place in my spirit. I myself was astonished that all my ideas had been changed from their wonted course and turned backward, cutting I know not what new channels. I assure you I was astonished, and I am yet. Looking at her without satisfying the longing of my soul or of my eyes, I said to myself, ‘I love her in a wonderful way! How is it that until now I have never fallen in love?’ I had never seen Mariquilla until that moment.”
“And the peaches?”
“Mariquilla was as much disturbed before me as I before her. Father Rincón went to talk with the gardener about the encroachments that the French had made upon the property (that was soon after the first of September, a month after the raising of the first siege), and Mariquilla and I remained alone. Alone! My first impulse was to cut and run; and she, as she has told me, also felt the same. Neither she nor I ran. We stayed there. All at once I felt an extraordinary movement of my intellect. Breaking the silence, I began to talk with her. We talked about all sorts of indifferent things at first, but to me came thoughts beyond my usual understanding, surpassing the ordinary, and all, all, all, I uttered. Mariquilla answered me little, but her eyes were only more eloquent than when I was talking to her. At last Father Rincón called, and we marched away. I took leave of her, and in a low voice said that we would soon meet again. We returned to Saragossa. Yes, the street, the trees, the Ebro, the cupola of the Pilar, the belfries of Saragossa, the passersby, the houses, the walls of the garden, the pavements, the sound of the wind, the dogs of the street, all seemed different to me, all, heaven and earth had been changed. My good teacher began to read again in Horace, and I said that Horace wasn’t worth anything. He wished me to dine with him, and threatened me with the loss of his friendship. I praised Virgil with enthusiasm, and repeated the celebrated lines﻿—

> Est mollis flamma medullas
 

 interea et tacitum vivit sub pectore vulnus.

===Chinese===
“这还用问。就算她俊，又怎么样？你听着。林孔神父捋着胡子，朝我转过身来说：‘奥古斯丁，你认了吧，你这一辈子没见过比这更周正的脸。瞧那双火一样的眼睛，那张天使的嘴，还有充当前额的那一小片天空。’我抖着，玛丽基亚笑起来，一张脸红得像玫瑰似的。接着林孔又往下说：‘你呢，是教会将来的神父，是表率，是青年的楷模，除了书本再没有旁的爱好，这位神明才肯向你显现。朱庇特啊！来瞻仰至高造物主这令人叹服的杰作吧。你看那面容的神情，那眼波的温存，那微笑的娟好，那容色的鲜润娇嫩，那肌肤的细密，然后你认了吧：就算天国是美的，花朵、山峦、光明，上帝的一切造物，同女人一比都不算什么——她才是那双不朽的手最完美、最完备的作品。’我的老师这么说着，我呢，哑口无言、满心惊愕，只是望着那件杰作出神——它委实比《埃涅阿斯纪》（*Aeneid*）还要好。我说不出我心里是什么滋味。你想一想埃布罗河，那条浩浩荡荡的大河，从源头一路奔下来，本要把自己交给大海，却忽然改了道，掉头往上，要流回阿斯图里亚斯（Asturias）去。我心灵里出的事，就是这一桩。连我自己都吃惊，我全部的心思竟一改往常的流向，倒卷了回去，开出不知什么样的新河床来。我向你担保，我那时吃惊，如今还吃惊。望着她，我心里的饥渴、眼睛的饥渴都解不了，我对自己说：‘我以奇妙的方式爱着她！怎么直到今天，我才头一回恋爱？’在那以前，我从来没有见过玛丽基亚。”

“那桃子呢？”

“玛丽基亚在我跟前心慌意乱，一如我在她跟前。林孔神父去找园丁，打听法国人侵占田产的事（那时是九月初刚过，第一次围城解除后一个月），玛丽基亚同我就单独留了下来。单独两人！我的头一个冲动是撒腿就跑；她呢，后来她告诉我，她也一样。她没有跑，我也没有跑。我们留在了那儿。忽然，我觉得自己心智里起了一场不寻常的运动。我打破沉默，开口同她说话。起先我们净谈些不相干的闲事，可涌上我心头的念头，却超出了我平常的悟性，超乎寻常，我便一股脑儿、一股脑儿、一股脑儿全说了出来。玛丽基亚答得少，可她的眼睛，比开口说话还要雄辩。末了林孔神父一喊，我们就动身了。我向她告辞，低声说我们很快会再见。我们回萨拉戈萨去。是啊，街道、树木、埃布罗河、皮拉尔圣母的穹顶、萨拉戈萨的钟楼、来往的行人、房屋、园墙、石板路、风声、街上的狗，在我眼里全都变了样，一切都变了，天和地都换了个新。我的好老师又捧起贺拉斯来念，我却说贺拉斯一文不值。他要我留下同他吃饭，还拿绝交来吓唬我。我热热烈烈地捧维吉尔（Virgil），诵起那著名的诗句——

> Est mollis flamma medullas
>  interea et tacitum vivit sub pectore vulnus.
>
> （火焰正熔蚀我的骨髓，那无声的创伤仍在胸中隐然存活。）

===Original===
“This was about the first of September,” said I, “and since then?”
“From that day a new life began for me. It commenced with a burning disquiet that robbed me of sleep, making distasteful to me all that was not Mariquilla. My own father’s house was hateful to me; and wandering about the environs of Saragossa without any companion, I sought peace for my spirit in solitude. I hated the college, all books and theology, and when October came, and they wished me to bind myself to live shut up in the holy house, I feigned illness in order to remain in my own. Thanks to the war that has made us all soldiers, I have been able to live free, to go at all hours, day and night, and see and talk with her frequently. I go to her house, make the signal agreed upon; she descends, opens her grated window; we talk long hours. People pass by, but I am muffled in my cloak even up to the eyes. With this and the darkness of night, no one recognizes me. As far as that is concerned, the boys in the street ask one another, ‘Who is this admirer of the Candiola?’ The other night, fearing discovery, we stopped our talks at the grating. Mariquilla came down, opened the garden gate, and I entered. No one could discover us, because Don Jerónimo, believing her to be in bed, retired to his room to count his money, and the old servant, the only one in the house, took us under her wing. Alone in the garden we sat down upon some stone steps and watched the brightness of the moonlight through the boughs of a great black poplar. In that majestic silence our souls contemplated the divine, and we experienced a deep sentiment, beyond words to express. Our felicity is so great that at times it is a living torment. If there are moments in which one might desire to be a hundred beings, there are also moments in which one might desire not to exist. We pass long hours there. The night before last we were there until daybreak. My teacher believed me to be with the guards, so I was not obliged to hasten. When morning first began to dawn, we separated. Over the top of the wall of the garden appeared the roofs of the neighboring houses and the top of the Torre Nueva. Pointing it out to me, Mariquilla said, ‘When that tower stands straight, then only shall I cease to love you.’ ”
Augustine said no more. A cannon-shot sounded from the side of Mount Torrero, and we both turned in that direction.

===Chinese===
“这是九月初前后的事，”我说，“打那以后呢？”

“打那一天起，我开始了新的生活。它打一股火烧似的不安开场，夺走了我的睡眠，叫一切不是玛丽基亚的东西，在我眼里都败了味。连我自己父亲的家，我都恨住；我一个人在萨拉戈萨四郊游荡，不带个伴儿，在孤独里给我的心灵讨一口安宁。我恨学院，恨一切书本同神学；到了十月，他们要我立下誓约，把自己关进那座圣洁的宅子里去住，我便装病，好赖在自己家里。多亏这场战争把我们统统变成了兵，我才活得自由自在，白天黑夜什么时辰都能出门，常见着她，同她说着话。我上她家去，打出约好的暗号；她下来，打开格栅窗；我们一谈，就是好几个钟头。有人打旁边过，可我拿斗篷一直裹到眼睛。有这一层，再加夜色沉沉，谁也认不出我。要说这个，街上的孩子们还彼此打听呢：‘这个追坎迪奥拉家姑娘的是谁呀？’前几天夜里，怕被人撞破，我们便不在格栅窗前谈了。玛丽基亚下来，开了园门，我进了园。谁也发现不了我们，因为唐·赫罗尼莫只当她上了床，自回房里数他的钱去了；家里就一个老女仆，把我们护在她的翅膀底下。我们在园子里，单独坐在几级石阶上，望着月亮的清辉从一株高大黑杨的枝桠间漏下来。在那庄严的寂静里，我们的灵魂瞻望着神圣，尝到一种言语说不出表的深情。我们的幸福太大了，有时简直成了一种活生生的折磨。有的时刻，人恨不得变成一百个人；也有的时刻，人恨不得不曾存在。我们在那儿一待就是老长的时辰。前天夜里，我们一直待到天亮。我的老师只当我在卫队当值，所以我用不着急着赶回去。清早头一线光亮起来，我们才分手。园墙头上，露出左邻右舍的屋顶，还有新塔的塔尖。玛丽基亚指给我看，说：‘等那座塔站直了，我才会不爱你。’”

奥古斯丁不再言语。托雷罗山那边响起一声炮，我们两个一齐转过身去。

## VI / 第六章

===Original===
The French had assaulted with great vigor the fortified positions of the Torrero. Ten thousand men defended them, commanded by Don Philip Saint March and by O’Neill, both generals of great merit. The volunteers of Bourbon, Castile, Campo Segorbino, of Alicante, and of Soria, the sharpshooters of Fernando VII, the Murcia regiment, and other bodies that I do not remember, answered the fire. From the redoubt of Los Mártires we saw the beginning of the action, and the French columns which extended the length of the canal and flanked the Torrero. The fire of the fusileers continued for some time, but the struggle could not be prolonged very long, for that point could not be held without the occupation and fortification of others close by, like Buena Vista, Casa Blanca, and the reservoir of the canal. But none the less our troops did not retire except slowly and in the best order, retreating by the Puente de América, and carrying with them all the pieces of artillery except one, which had been dismantled by the enemy’s fire. Amidst it all we heard a great noise which resounded at a great distance, and as the fire had almost ceased, we supposed that there was another battle outside the town.

“There is the Brigadier Don José Manso,” said Augustine to me, “with the Swiss regiment of Aragón, which Don Mariano Walker commands, the volunteers of Huesca, of whom Don Pedro Villacampa is leader, the volunteers of Catalonia, and other valiant corps. And here are we, hand in hand! Along this side it appears to be about finished. The French will content themselves today with the conquest of Torrero.”

“Either I am greatly deceived,” I replied, “or they are now going to attack San José.”

We all looked at the spot indicated, an edifice of huge dimensions which arose at our left, separated from the Puerta Quemada by the valley of the Huerva.

“There is Renovales,” said Augustine﻿—“the brave Don Mariano Renovales, who distinguished himself so highly in the other siege, who now commands the troops of Orihuela and of Valencia.”

In our position we were all prepared for an energetic defence. In the redoubt del Pilar, in the battery of Los Mártires, in the tower of Del Pino, the same as in the Trinitarios, the artillery stood guard with burning matches, and the infantry waited behind the parapets in positions that seemed to us quite secure, ready to fire if any columns should attempt to assault us. It was cold, and most of us were shivering. One might almost have believed that it was from fear; but no, it was cold, and anybody who had said the contrary would have lied.

===Chinese===
法军以极猛的攻势扑向托雷罗（Torrero）的筑垒阵地。据守这些阵地的一万人马，由唐·费利佩·圣马尔奇（Don Philip Saint March）与奥尼尔（O'Neill）两位功勋卓著的将领统率。波旁义勇军、卡斯蒂利亚义勇军、塞戈尔维诺（Campo Segorbino）义勇军、阿利坎特义勇军与索里亚义勇军，费尔南多七世（Fernando VII）神枪手，穆尔西亚团，还有别的我已记不起来的队伍，一齐开火应战。我们从洛斯·马尔蒂雷斯（Los Mártires）棱堡上望见战斗开场，望见沿运河一线铺开、侧击托雷罗的法军纵队。燧发枪手的火力持续了一阵，但这一仗拖不了多久，因为不把左近几处一并占领筑垒——如布埃纳维斯塔（Buena Vista）、卡萨布兰卡（Casa Blanca）和运河水库——这一点便守不住。饶是如此，我军仍从容不迫、井然有序地缓缓撤退，取道美洲桥（Puente de América）退过河去，将火炮全数携走，只撇下一门——那门炮已被敌军炮火打瘫。正当此时，我们听得一阵巨响，远远传荡开去；这边炮火既几乎停歇，我们猜想城外又开了一场战斗。

“那边是准将唐·何塞·曼索（Don José Manso），”奥古斯丁对我说，“带着阿拉贡瑞士团——由唐·马里亚诺·沃克（Don Mariano Walker）统领——还有韦斯卡（Huesca）义勇军，首领是唐·佩德罗·比利亚坎普（Don Pedro Villacampa），再加加泰罗尼亚义勇军，以及别的好几支骁勇的队伍。可我们呢，倒手挽着手在这儿干瞧！这一侧的战事看来差不多收场了。今天法国人得了托雷罗，也就该知足了。”

“要么是我大错特错，”我答道，“要么就是他们眼下要来进攻圣何塞（San José）了。”

我们大伙儿都朝他所指的地方望去：那是一座体量巨大的建筑，矗立在我们左手边，隔着韦尔瓦河的河谷与克马达门（Puerta Quemada）相望。

“雷诺瓦莱斯在那儿，”奥古斯丁说——“那位英勇的唐·马里亚诺·雷诺瓦莱斯（Don Mariano Renovales），上一次围城中大放异彩的，如今统率着奥里韦拉与瓦伦西亚的部队。”

在我们据守的阵地上，人人都作好了奋力防御的准备。皮拉尔棱堡里、洛斯·马尔蒂雷斯炮台上、皮诺塔上，同三一会修道院里一样，炮兵燃着火绳守在炮位上，步兵候在胸墙背后——那些位置在我们看来颇为牢靠——只待敌军纵队胆敢来攻，便开火痛击。天气很冷，我们大多数人都直打哆嗦。人家几乎要当那是吓出来的；才不是呢，就是冻的，谁要是说句相反的话，那便是撒谎。

===Original===
The movement which I had foreseen was not slow in taking place, and the convent of San José was attacked by a strong column of French infantry. It was an attempt at an attack, or, rather, a surprise. To all appearances, the enemy had a poor memory, and in three months they had forgotten that surprises were impossible in Saragossa. None the less they arrived within gunshot, and doubtless the graceless whelps believed that merely at sight of them our warriors would fall dead with fear. The poor men had just arrived from Silesia, and did not know what manner of warfare there was in Spain. And, furthermore, as they had gained the Torrero with so little difficulty, they believed themselves in train to swallow the world. They were advancing thus, as I have said, and San José was not making any demonstration. When they were nearly within gunshot of the loopholes and embrasures of that edifice, all at once these began vomiting such a terrible fire that my brave Frenchmen took to their heels with the utmost precipitation. Having had enough doubtless, they remained stretched out at full length; and upon seeing the outcome of their valor, those of us who were watching the onset from the battery of Los Mártires broke out into exclamations, applause, cries, and huzzas. In this ferocious manner the soldier celebrates in battle the death of his fellow-creatures. He who instinctively feels compassion at the slaying of a rabbit on a hunt jumps for joy on seeing hundreds of robust men fall﻿—young, happy men who have never done harm to anybody.

Such was the attack upon San José, a futile attempt quickly punished. By that time, the French should have understood that if Torrero was abandoned, it was by calculation and not on account of weakness. Alone, embarrassed, deserted, without external defences, without forces or forts, Saragossa renewed again her earthworks, her defences of bricks, her bastions of mud heaped up the evening before to be again defended against the first soldiers, the first artillery, and the first engineers of the world. Pomp and show of a nation, formidable machines, enormous quantities of power, scientific preparation of materials, force, and intelligence in their greatest splendor, the invaders bring to attack the fortified place which appears to be guarded by boys. It is indeed almost like this: all succumb, all is reduced to powder in front of those walls which might be kicked over. But behind this movable defensive material is the well-tempered steel of Aragón souls, which cannot be broken or bent, nor cast into moulds, nor crushed, nor robbed of breath, and which surrounds the whole region like a barrier, indestructible by human means.

The whole district about the Torre Nueva was resounding with clamors and alarms. When to this district comes such mournful sounds, the city is in danger and needs all her sons. What is it? What is passing? What will happen?

===Chinese===
