我预见的动静没有迟迟不来：圣何塞修道院遭到了法军一支强大步兵纵队的攻击。那是一次强攻的尝试，或者说得更确切些，是一次偷袭。看样子敌人记性太坏，只三个月的工夫，便把萨拉戈萨容不得偷袭这回事忘了个精光。饶是如此，他们还是推进到了火器射程之内，而且毫无疑问，那帮没调教的崽子还满以为光是他们一露面，我们的勇士就得活活吓死。这些可怜人刚从西里西亚（Silesia）开到，全然不知道西班牙的仗是怎么个打法。再说，托雷罗得来如此不费吹灰之力，他们便自以为转眼就要吞下整个世界了。他们就这样往前推进，如我方才所言，圣何塞方面毫无动静。待他们几乎进到那座建筑的枪眼与炮眼射程之内，这些枪眼炮眼骤然喷出好一场可怕的火力，我那些勇敢的法国佬撒腿便逃，一溜烟跑得不亦乐乎。他们想必是受够了，一个个直挺挺地躺倒在地；眼见他们这番英勇的结局，我们这些在洛斯·马尔蒂雷斯炮台上观战的人顿时爆出惊叹、喝彩、呐喊与欢呼。士兵在战场上便是这样，以凶暴的方式庆贺同类的死亡。一个在猎场上本能地不忍宰兔的人，眼见数以百计的壮汉倒下——年轻、快活、从不曾害过任何人的汉子——却要欢喜得跳起来。

对圣何塞的进攻便是如此：一场徒劳的尝试，立时便遭了报应。到了这时候，法国人本该懂得，放弃托雷罗是出于谋算，而非力弱。孤城一座，局促、被弃、没有外围防线，没有兵力也没有堡垒，萨拉戈萨却又翻修起她的土木工事、她的砖砌防线、她前夜才垒起的泥堡，好在第二天再度抵挡世界上头一流的士兵、头一流的炮兵、头一流的工兵。一个王国的威仪排场、骇人的机器、滔天的伟力、经科学配制的器材，再加登峰造极的武力与心智——侵略者带着这一切，来攻这座看去只由孩童把守的筑垒之地。事实也几乎当真如此：在这踹一脚便倒的墙壁面前，人人倒伏，一切化为齑粉。然而在这一堆搬得动的防御材料背后，挺立着阿拉贡灵魂中淬炼精纯的钢铁——砸不碎，折不断，浇不进模子，压不垮，也夺不走一口气；它如一道壁垒环卫着整个地区，非人力所能摧毁。

新塔一带响彻了喧嚷与警号。这片街区一传来这种凄厉之声，便说明城池告急，需要她全体儿女出力了。出了什么事？眼下怎样？往后又将如何？

===Original===
“Matters must be going badly back of the town,” said Augustine.

Meanwhile they attacked us yonder to occupy the attention of the crowd on this side of the river. The same thing was done in the first siege.

“*Al arrabal, al arrabal!*” was our cry. “To the suburb!”

And while we were saying this, the French sent us some balls to show us that we must stay where we were. Fortunately Saragossa had enough people within her walls, and could readily assist and support all parts.

===Chinese===
“城背后那头一定是吃紧了，”奥古斯丁说。

原来他们是在那一头攻打我们，好牵制住河这一岸人群的注意。第一次围城时，他们使的也是这一手。

“*Al arrabal, al arrabal!*”便是我们的呐喊——“到城郊去！”

我们正这么喊着，法军便赏了我们几发炮弹，教我们明白还是乖乖待在原地的好。所幸萨拉戈萨城墙之内人手充足，无论哪一头告急，都能从容驰援、处处接应。

===Original===
My battalion abandoned the wall near Santa Engracia, and began to march towards the Coso. We did not know where we were being conducted, but it is probable that they were taking us to the suburb. The streets were full of people. Old men and women came out, impelled by curiosity, wishing to see at close quarters and near at hand the points of danger, since it was impossible for them to be placed in the same peril. The streets of San Gil, San Pedro, and La Cuchillería, which lead to the bridge, were almost impassable. A great multitude of women were passing through them, all walking in the direction of the Pilar and La Seo.

The booming of the cannon excited rather than saddened the fervent people, and all were jostling one another to get nearest the front. In the Plaza de la Seo, I saw the cavalry which, with all these people, obstructed the bridge and obliged my battalion to look for an easier way to the other side. While we were passing before the porch of this sanctuary, we heard the sound of the prayers wherewith all the women of the city were imploring their holy patroness. The few men who wished to come into the temple were expelled by them.

We went to the bank of the river near San Juan de los Panetas, and took up our place on a mound, awaiting orders. In front and on the other side of the river, the field of battle was divided. We saw at the end nearest us the grove of Macanaz, over there and close to the bridge the little monastery of Altabas, yonder that of San Lazarus, and further on the Monastery of Jesus. Behind this scene, reflected in the waters of the great river, could be seen a horrible fire. There was an interminable turmoil, a hoarse clamor of the voices of cannon and of human yells. Dense clouds of smoke, renewed unceasingly, mounted confusedly to the heavens. All the breastworks of this position, which were constructed with bricks from neighboring brickyards, formed with the earth of the kilns a reddish mass. One might have believed that the ground had been mixed together with blood.

===Chinese===
我们营撤下了圣恩格拉西亚近旁的城墙，朝科索大街开拔。我们不知道自己要被带往何处，不过多半是往城郊去吧。满街都是人。老翁老妪受好奇心驱使也走了出来，想凑近亲眼瞧瞧那些险地——反正他们自己是万不会给摆到同样的险境里去的。通往大桥的圣希尔街、圣佩德罗街和库奇列里亚街（La Cuchillería），几乎挤得水泄不通。大批妇女正打这几条街上走过，全都朝皮拉尔圣母和塞奥的方向而去。

隆隆炮声没有使这些狂热的人们悲伤，反倒使他们亢奋，个个你推我搡，只求挤到离火线最近的地方去。在塞奥广场（Plaza de la Seo），我望见骑兵队同这些人群一道堵住了大桥，逼得我们营只好另觅一条好走的路到对岸去。打这座圣殿的门廊前经过时，我们听见里面祈祷之声嗡嗡不绝——满城的妇女都在恳求她们的主保圣母。少数几个想进殿的男子，被她们轰了出来。

我们开到圣胡安-德洛斯帕内塔斯（San Juan de los Panetas）近旁的河岸，占住一座土丘待命。眼前与河对岸，战场就此分作两片。最近的一头，我们望见马卡纳斯（Macanaz）树林；那边紧挨着大桥的，是阿尔塔巴斯（Altabas）小修道院；再过去是圣拉撒路（San Lazarus）修道院，更远处是耶稣修道院（Monastery of Jesus）。这片景象的背后，映着大河的粼粼水光，还望得见一场骇人的大火。那边是一场没完没了的骚乱，炮声与人喊搅作一片嘶哑的喧嚣。滚滚浓烟一波未息一波又起，乱纷纷地腾上天空。这道阵地上的胸墙都用邻近砖窑的砖砌成，同窑土混成一片暗红，人家简直要当这地面是拌着血和成的。

===Original===
The French held their front towards the Barcelona road and the Juslibol, where more kilns and gardens lie at the left of the second of those two ways. Thence the Twelfth had furiously attacked our entrenchments, making their way by the Barcelona road, and challenging with impetuous intrepidity the crossfires of San Lazarus and that of the place called El Marcelo. Their courage lay in striking audacious blows upon the batteries, and their tenacity produced a veritable hecatomb. They fell in great numbers; the ranks were broken, and, being instantly filled by others, they repeated the attack. At times they almost reached the parapets, and a thousand individual contests increased the horror of the scene. They went in advance of their leaders, brandishing their cutlasses, like desperate men who had made it a question of honor to die before a heap of bricks, and in that frightful destruction which wrenched the life from hundreds of men every minute, they disappeared, flung down upon mother earth, soldiers and sergeants, ensigns, captains, and colonels. It was a veritable struggle between two peoples; and while the fires of the first siege were burning in our hearts, the French came on thirsting for vengeance with all the passion of offended manhood, worse even than the passion of the warrior.

It was this untimely bloodthirstiness that lost them the day. They should have begun by demolishing our works with their artillery, observing the serenity which a siege demands, and not have engaged in those hand to hand combats before positions defended by a people like the one that they had met on the fifteenth of July, and the fourth of August. They ought to have repressed their feeling of contempt or scorn of the forces of the enemy﻿—a feeling that has always been the bad star of the French. It was the same in the war with Spain, as in the recent conflict with Prussia. They ought to have put into execution a calmly considered plan which would have produced in the besieged less of disgust than exaltation.

It is certain that if they carried with them the thought of their immortal general who always conquered as much by his admirable logic as by his cannons, they would have employed in the siege of Saragossa a little of the knowledge of the human heart, without which the pursuit of war, brutal war﻿—it seems a lie!﻿—is no more than cruel carnage.

Napoleon, with his extraordinary penetration, would have comprehended the Saragossan character, and would have abstained from attacking the unprotected columns, whose boast was of individual personal valor. This is a quality at all times difficult and dangerous to encounter, but above all in the presence of nations who fight for an ideal and not for an idol.

I will not go into further details of the dreadful battle of the twenty-first of December, the most glorious of the second siege of Aragón. As I did not see it at close quarters, and can only give the story of what was told me, I am moved not to be prolix, because there are so many and such interesting adventures which I must narrate. This makes a certain restraint necessary in the description of these sanguinary encounters. It is enough now to know that the French believed when night came that it was time to desist from their purpose, and they retired, leaving the plain covered with bodies of the dead. It was a good moment to follow them with cavalry; but after a short discussion the generals, I am told, decided not to put themselves in peril in a sally which could only be dangerous.

===Chinese===
法军战线朝着巴塞罗那大道与胡斯利沃尔大道（Juslibol）一路展开，这两条道中后一条的左手边，砖窑与园圃更多。第十二团便是从那里凶猛地扑向我军堑壕，循巴塞罗那大道一路突进，以一股莽悍无畏的劲头，去挑衅圣拉撒路与那处唤作埃尔马塞洛（El Marcelo）之地交叉的炮火。他们的勇气，全在朝着炮台放手猛攻上头；他们的死缠，则酿成了一场名副其实的大屠杀。他们成片地倒下；队列打散了，旋即又被后来者填满，他们便再攻一回。有几阵子他们几乎扑到胸墙跟前，千百场单打独斗的搏杀，更添了这幅景象的恐怖。他们抢在长官前头，挥舞着短弯刀，活像一群绝望之徒，把死在一堆砖头跟前当成了荣誉攸关的大事；在这场每分钟都从几百条人命里拧走性命的可怖毁灭之中，他们销声匿迹，扑倒在大地母亲身上——士兵与中士、旗手、上尉与上校，一概如是。这是两个民族之间一场货真价实的搏斗；当第一次围城的烈火还在我们胸中烧着，法国人却怀着男子汉受辱之后的全部激愤扑上来讨血债——这股激愤，比战士的激情还要凶。

正是这不合时宜的嗜血，葬送了他们这一天。他们本该先用炮火拆毁我军的工事，端出一场围城战所要求的镇定来，而不该在这样一支民众据守的阵地前打那些贴身肉搏——七月十五日、八月四日，他们领教过的正是这样一支民众。他们本该压下自己对敌军兵力的轻蔑与鄙夷——这份情绪，素来是法国人的灾星。对西班牙的战争如此，近来同普鲁士的冲突也如此。他们本该照一套冷静盘算的方略行事，叫被围者心头生的与其说是同仇敌忾，不如说是灰心丧气。

可以断言，倘若他们心里装着他们那位不朽统帅的英灵——那位统帅打胜仗，靠他的大炮，同样靠他那令人叹服的逻辑——那么在围攻萨拉戈萨的时候，他们就会用上一点关于人心的学问。缺了这门学问，战争这一行——残暴的战争，说来像句谎话！——便不过是酷烈的屠戮罢了。

拿破仑（Napoleon）凭他那超凡的洞察力，本可以参透萨拉戈萨人的性情，也本可以克制住自己，不去强攻那些毫无掩蔽的纵队——须知这些纵队引以为傲的，正是单枪匹马、纯属个人的勇武。这种品质，无论何时都难以招架、凶险万分，尤其是面对一个为理想而战、而非为偶像而战的民族之时。

十二月二十一日那场可怕的大战，我就不再细述了——那是阿拉贡第二次围城中最光荣的一战。因为我未曾近前观战，能讲的不过是旁人告诉我的情形，我便不愿絮烦：往后还有那么多、那么有趣的故事等着我讲述呢。这就使我在描画这些血腥交锋时，不能不加几分节制。眼下知道下面这些也就够了：夜幕降临时，法国人觉得该死了这条心，便退了下去，平原上遗尸遍地。这本是派骑兵衔尾追击的好时机；但据说众将略一合计，便认定犯不着为一趟只有凶险可言的出击去冒性命之险。

## VII / 第七章

===Original===
Night came, and when a part of our troops fell back upon the city, all of the people hastened to the suburb to look at the field of battle from near at hand, and to gladden their imaginations by going over, one by one, the scenes of heroism. The animation, the movement, the clatter of noise in that part of the city were immense. At one side were groups of soldiers singing with feverish joy, on the other bands of merciful people carrying the wounded into their houses. Everywhere was hearty satisfaction, which showed itself in lively dialogues, question, joyous exclamations﻿—tears and laughter mingling with the rejoicings and enthusiasm.
It was, possibly, about nine o’clock before my battalion broke ranks; because, lacking quarters, we did not permit ourselves to leave the position, although there was no danger.
Augustine and I ran to Del Pilar, where a great crowd was rushing. We entered with difficulty. I was surprised to see how some persons jostled and pushed others in order to approach the chapel of the Virgin del Pilar. The prayers, the entreaties, and the demonstrations of rejoicing, taken all together, did not seem like the prayers of any class of the faithful. The prayers were like talks mingled with tears, groans, the most tender words, and other phrases of intimate and ingenuous affection, such as the Spanish people are wont to use with their saints that are most beloved. They fell upon their knees; they kissed the pavement; they grasped the iron gratings of the chapel; they addressed the holy image, calling it by names the most familiar and the most pathetic of the language. Those who could not﻿—because of the crowd of people﻿—come near her were talking to her from afar off, waving their arms wildly about. There were no sacristans to stop these wild ways and seemingly irreverent noises, because they were themselves children of this overflowing delirious devotion. The solemn silence of sacred places was not observed. All there were as if in their own house, as if the house of their cherished Virgin, their mother, their beloved, the queen of Saragossans, were also the house of her children, her servants and subjects.

===Chinese===
夜幕降临，我军一部退回城中，全城百姓便急急忙忙涌向城郊，要就近看一看战场，一处一处地重温那许多英雄壮举，好叫想象也欢畅一番。城中那一带的热闹、人流与鼎沸声浪，可谓盛况空前。这一边，是一群群士兵怀着如火的喜悦引吭高歌；那一边，是一队队好心肠的人们，正把伤员抬进自己家中。到处都是发自肺腑的欢欣，它流露在活泼的交谈、纷纷的询问和欢快的呼喊里——泪水与笑声，同欢庆与热忱交织在一起。
约莫到了九点钟，我们营才解散队伍；因为营舍无着，虽已毫无危险，我们仍不容许自己擅离阵地。
我和奥古斯丁朝皮拉尔圣母教堂（Del Pilar）跑去，一大群人正朝那里蜂拥。我们好不容易才挤了进去。有些人为了接近皮拉尔圣母的小堂，竟互相推搡冲撞，那副光景看得我暗暗吃惊。那些祈祷、恳求与欢庆的表露，合在一起看，全然不像任何一类信众惯常的祷告。这些祈祷更像一场场家常倾谈，夹着泪水、呻吟、最温柔的话语，以及种种亲昵而天真的爱语——西班牙人对他们最敬爱的圣人，向来惯用这样的口吻。他们扑倒跪下；亲吻石板地面；攥住小堂的铁栅栏；对着圣像呼喊，用这语言里最亲昵、最动情的称呼唤她。那些因人潮拥挤而挤不到她跟前的人，便远远地同她说话，双臂狂乱地挥舞。没有教堂司事出来制止这些狂态和看似失敬的喧哗，因为司事们自己也是这漫溢痴狂的虔敬所生的儿女。圣所应有的肃穆寂静，在这里无人遵守。人人都像在自己家里一样，仿佛他们挚爱的圣母——他们的母亲、他们的至爱、萨拉戈萨人的女王——的居所，同时也就是她儿女、她仆人与臣民的居所。

===Original===
Astonished at such fervor which the familiarity made more interesting, I fought my way to the grating, and saw the celebrated image. Who has not seen her, who does not know her, at least by the innumerable sculptures and portraits which have reproduced her endlessly from one end of the peninsula to the other?
She was at the left of the little altar which is in the depth of the chapel in a niche adorned with oriental luxury, a little statue, then as now. A great profusion of wax candles illuminated her, and precious stones covered her clothing and crown, darting dazzling reflections. Gold and diamonds gleamed in the circlet about her face, in the votive bracelets hung upon her breast, and in the rings on her hands. A living creature would have given way under so great a weight of treasure. Her garments, falling without folds, stretched straight from head to feet, and left visible only her hands. The child Jesus, sustained on her left side, revealed a bit of his brown little face between the brocade and the jewels. The face of the Virgin, burnished by time, is also brown. A gentle serenity possesses her, symbol of her eternal blessedness. She looks outward, her sweet gaze scanning constantly the devoted concourse. There shines in her eyes a ray of the clearest light, and this artificial gleam seems like the intensity and fixedness of the human gaze. It was difficult when I saw her for the first time to remain indifferent in the midst of that religious demonstration, and not to add a word to the concert of enthusiastic tongues talking with distinct voices to the Señora.
I was watching the statue, when Augustine pressed my arm, saying﻿—

===Chinese===
这般热诚令我惊叹，而那亲昵劲儿更叫它别有动人之处。我挤到栅栏跟前，见到了那尊著名的圣像。谁没有见过她，谁又不认得她呢？至少，从半岛的这一头到那一头，无数雕塑与画像把她无穷无尽地翻制传扬，凭这些总也该认得她了。
她在小堂深处那座小祭坛的左手边，供在一个装点得富丽堂皇、一派东方气度的壁龛里，是一尊小小的圣像，当年如此，如今亦然。大片大片的蜡烛照得她通明，宝石缀满她的衣袍和冠冕，迸射出耀眼的光芒。她脸庞四周的冠环、胸前悬挂的还愿手镯、双手的指环，处处闪着黄金与钻石。这许多财宝的重量，若换一个血肉之躯来承当，早被压垮了。她的衣袍直直垂下，不见一点褶皱，从头顶直覆到脚，只露出双手。圣婴耶稣（the child Jesus）由她左侧托着，从织锦和珠宝之间露出一点棕色的小脸。圣母的脸庞经岁月摩挲而莹润，也是棕色的。一派温柔的安详占据着她，正是她永恒福乐的象征。她朝外望着，甜柔的目光不住地扫过虔诚的人群。她眼中闪烁着一道至亮的光，这人工的光泽，竟宛如人类目光的那种专注与凝定。我初见她时，置身那样的宗教盛典之中，很难再漠然无动，很难不向那热烈的合唱里添上自己的一言半语——人人正以各自的嗓音，向这位夫人（Señora）絮絮而谈。
我正凝望着那尊圣像，奥古斯丁忽然捏了捏我的手臂，说道——

===Original===
“Look, there she is!”
“Who, the Virgin? I am looking at her now.”
“No, man, Mariquilla! There, in front, close to the column.”
I looked, but I saw only a great many people. We immediately quitted our place, looking about for a way to get through the multitude to the other side.
“She is not with Candiola,” said Augustine, joyously. “She has come with the servant.” And, saying this, he elbowed his way to one side and the other to make a road, punching backs and breasts, stepping on feet, matting down hats, and rumpling clothing. I followed behind him, causing equal destruction right and left. At last we came to the beautiful young girl, and it was really she, as I could see at once with my own eyes.
The enthusiastic passion of my good friend did not deceive me. Mariquilla was worth the trouble of being extravagantly, madly loved. Her pale brunette skin, her deeply black eyes, her perfect nose, her incomparable mouth, and her beautiful low forehead attracted attention to her at once. There was in her face as in her body a certain light and delicate voluptuousness. When she lowered her eyes, it seemed to me as if a sweet and lovely mist surrounded her. She smiled gravely; and when she approached us, her looks revealed timidity. Everything about her showed the reserved and circumspect passion of a woman of character, and she seemed to me little given to talking, lacking in coquetry, and poor in artifices. I afterwards had reason to confirm this, my early judgment. There shone in the face of Mariquilla a heavenly calm, and a certain security in herself. Different from most women, like few among them, that soul would not readily change, except for just and righteous reasons.

===Chinese===
“瞧，她在那儿！”
“谁，圣母吗？我这不正看着她呢。”
“不是，老兄，是玛丽基亚！就在前头，紧挨着那根圣柱。”
我望过去，却只看见黑压压的一大片人。我们当即撇下自己的位置，四下里张望，想寻条路穿过人群到对面去。
“她不是跟坎迪奥拉一道来的，”奥古斯丁喜滋滋地说，“她是同女仆来的。”说着，他便左肘右拱开出一条路来：撞人脊背，抵人前胸，踩人脚面，压扁帽子，揉皱衣裳。我跟在他身后，左右两厢造成不相上下的破坏。我们终于来到那位美丽的姑娘跟前——千真万确是她，我一眼便亲眼认了出来。
我这位好友的一片痴情，并没有蒙蔽我的眼睛。玛丽基亚委实值得人为她神魂颠倒、如痴如狂地爱上一场。她浅褐的肤色、深邃乌黑的眼睛、端正无瑕的鼻子、无与伦比的嘴、秀美的低额，叫人一眼便注意到她。她的脸上，同她的身段一样，都含着一种轻盈而细腻的妩媚。她垂下眼睛的时候，我只觉有一层甜美可爱的薄雾把她笼罩了。她笑得端庄；走近我们时，眼神里又流露出羞怯。她周身上下都显出一种有主见的女子的克制而审慎的热情；在我看来，她不大健谈，不事卖弄，也少有心机。这最初的判断，我后来有充分的理由加以印证。玛丽基亚的脸上焕发着一种天赐的宁静，还有一种对自身的笃定。她与大多数女子不同，世间像她这样的灵魂寥寥无几，不会轻易改变，除非是为了正当而正义的缘由。

===Original===
Other women of quick sensibility pour themselves out like wax before a small fire; but Mariquilla was made of the best metal, yielding only to a great fire, and when that came she was of necessity like molten metal that burns when it touches.
Besides her beauty, the elegance and even luxury of her dress attracted my attention. Having heard much of the avarice of Candiola, I supposed that he would have reduced his daughter to the utmost extremes of wretchedness in matters of dress. It was not so. As Montoria told me afterwards, the stingiest of the stingy not only permitted his daughter some expenses, but now and then made her some little present which he looked upon as the ne plus ultra of mundane splendor.
If Candiola was capable of letting some of his relations die of hunger, to his daughter he gave a phenomenal, a scandalous amount of pocket-money. Although he was a miser, he was a father; he loved his girl very much, finding in his generosity to her perhaps the only pleasure of his arid existence.
Somewhat more must be said in regard to this, but it will appear little by little in the course of the story. And now I must say that my friend had not yet spoken ten words to his adored Mariquilla, when a man approached us abruptly, and after having looked at the two for an instant with flashing eyes, spoke to the young girl, taking her by the arm, and saying, with a show of anger﻿—

===Chinese===
别的女子感觉一触即发，小火一烤，就像蜡一样熔软下来；玛丽基亚却是上好的金属铸的，非大火不能使她屈从，而那大火一旦临头，她便势必成了熔化的金属，触之灼人。
除了容貌之美，她衣着的雅致乃至华贵也引起了我的注意。坎迪奥拉的吝啬我早有耳闻，原以为他会在穿戴一事上把女儿克扣到山穷水尽的地步。事实并非如此。正如蒙特里亚后来告诉我的，这位吝啬鬼中的吝啬鬼，非但容许女儿有些花销，还不时送她一两件小礼物，并且把那看作人间奢华的极致（ne plus ultra）。
坎迪奥拉既然忍心让自家几个亲戚活活饿死，给起女儿零花钱来，数目却大得出奇、大得骇人听闻。他虽是守财奴，到底是个父亲；他深爱自己的姑娘，对她的这份慷慨，或许正是他干涸一生中唯一的乐趣。
关于这一点，还有可说的，但随着故事的推进，自会一点一点显露。此刻我只需说：我这位朋友对他倾心爱慕的玛丽基亚还没说上十句话，忽有一个男人猛地朝我们走来，目光灼灼地逼视了那两人一瞬，便一把抓住姑娘的手臂，对她开口，怒形于色地说道——

===Original===
“What is going on here? And you, good Guedita, what brought you to the Pilar at such an hour? Go to the house, go to the house immediately!”
And pushing before him mistress and maid, he carried them both off towards the door and the street, and the three disappeared from our sight.
It was Candiola. I remember him well, and the remembrance makes me tremble with horror. Further on you will know why. Since the brief scene in the church del Pilar, the image of that man has been engraven on my memory, and certainly his face was not one which would let itself be quickly forgotten. Old, bent, of miserable and sickly aspect, crooked and disagreeable, lean of face, with sunken cheeks, Candiola roused antipathy from the first moment. His nose, sharp and hooked like the beak of a bird, his chin, peaked also, the coarse hair of his grizzled eyebrows, the greenish eyes, the forehead furrowed as if by a ruler with deep parallel wrinkles, the cartilaginous ears, the yellowish skin, the metallic quality of the voice, the slovenly clothes, the insulting grimaces﻿—all his personality from head to foot, from his bag wig to the sole of his coarse shoe, produced at sight an unconquerable repulsion. It can readily be understood that he had not a single friend.
Candiola had no beard; his face, according to the fashion, was quite clean shaven, although the razor did not enter the field more than once a week. If Don Jerónimo had had a beard, it would have made him seem very much like a certain Venetian shopkeeper whom I afterwards came to know very well, travelling in the great world of books, and in whom I find certain traits of physiognomy that recalled the man who had so brusquely presented himself to us in the temple del Pilar.

===Chinese===
“这儿是在干什么？还有你，好个格韦迪塔（Guedita），这么个时辰，什么事把你们带到皮拉尔来？回家去，马上回家去！”
说着，他把主仆二人推在前面，押着她们朝门口和街上而去，三个人随即从我们眼前消失了。
此人正是坎迪奥拉。我记得清清楚楚，那记忆至今让我恐怖得发颤。个中缘故，往后你们自会知道。自从皮拉尔圣母教堂里那短短一幕之后，这个人的形象便镌刻在我的记忆里，而他的那张脸，也实在不是轻易让人忘怀的。年老，佝偻，形容愁苦而多病，歪扭难看，面目可憎，脸上精瘦，双颊深陷——坎迪奥拉打头一眼便激起人的反感。他那尖钩如鸟喙的鼻子、同样尖尖的下巴、灰白粗硬的眉毛、泛绿的眼睛、仿佛用界尺划出道道平行深纹的前额、软骨似的耳朵、蜡黄的皮肤、金属般的嗓音、邋遢的衣衫、轻慢无礼的怪相——他这个人从头到脚，从那顶袋状假发到粗皮鞋底，一望之下便叫人生出一种无法克服的憎恶。不难理解，他连一个朋友也没有。
坎迪奥拉没有胡子；按当时的时尚，他的脸刮得干干净净，尽管一星期里，剃刀至多进入那片地界一回。这位唐·赫罗尼莫若是留起胡子来，准会酷似某一位威尼斯店主——此人是我后来遨游于书籍的大千世界时才得以熟识的；我在他身上发现某些相貌特征，颇能令人想起这个在皮拉尔圣殿里如此莽撞地闯到我们面前来的人。

===Original===
“Did you see that miserable and ridiculous old man?” Augustine asked me when we were alone, looking towards the door where the three people had disappeared.
“He evidently doesn’t like his daughter to have admirers.”
“But I am sure that he did not see me talking with her. He has suspicions, nothing more. If he should pass from suspicion to certitude, Mariquilla and I would be lost. Did you see that look he threw us, the damned miser?﻿—he is black from his soul to his Satanic hide.”
“Bad sort of father-in-law to have.”
“Bad enough,” said Montoria, sadly. “He would be dear in exchange for a spoonful of verdigris! I am sure he will abuse her tonight; but fortunately he is not in the habit of ill-treating her.”

===Chinese===
“你瞧见那个又可鄙又可笑的老头儿了吗？”只剩我们两人时，奥古斯丁望着那三个人消失的门口问我。
“他分明是不乐意女儿有人爱慕。”
“可我敢担保，他并没有瞧见我在同她说话。他不过是起了疑心，仅此而已。他若从疑心变成了确信，我和玛丽基亚就完了。你瞧见他甩给我们的那一眼了吗，那个该死的吝啬鬼？——他从灵魂直到那撒旦般的皮囊，通体都是黑的。”
“这样的丈人，可真够人受的。”
“何止是够人受，”蒙特里亚凄然道，“拿一勺铜绿来换他，我都嫌他贵！我敢说，今晚他准要痛骂她一顿；所幸的是，他素来没有虐待她的习惯。”

===Original===
“And would not the Señor Candiola be pleased to see her married to the son of Don José de Montoria?” I asked.
“Are you mad? I see you talking to him of that! The wretched miser not only watches his daughter as if she were a bag of gold, and is not disposed to give her to anybody; but he has also an ancient and profound resentment against my father, because he freed some unhappy debtors from his fangs. I tell you, that if he discovers that his daughter loves me, he will keep her locked up in an iron chest in that cellar of his where he keeps his hard cash. I don’t know what would happen if my father came to know of it. My flesh creeps just to think of it. The worst nightmare which disturbs my slumbers is that which shows me the moment when señor my father and señora my mother learn of my great love for Mariquilla. A son of Don José de Montoria enamoured of a daughter of Candiola, a young man who is formally destined to be a bishop﻿—a bishop, Gabriel! I am going to be a bishop, in the minds of my parents!”
Saying this, Augustine dashed his head against the sacred wall on which we were leaning.

===Chinese===
“那么，坎迪奥拉先生难道会不愿意看见她嫁给唐·何塞·德·蒙特里亚的儿子吗？”我问道。
“你疯了吗？我看你真敢去同他提这个！那可恶的守财奴不但把女儿当作一袋金子似的看管着，压根儿不打算把她许给任何人；他还对我父亲怀着一腔积年宿怨、深仇大恨，因为我父亲曾把几个可怜的欠债人从他的魔爪下解救出来。我告诉你，他一旦发现女儿爱着我，就会把她锁进他那只存放现钱的铁箱里，搁在他那间地窖里。我父亲若是知道了这件事，天知道会出什么事。我一想到这里，皮肉就发麻。搅扰我睡眠的最可怕的噩梦，就是让我看见我的父亲大人和母亲大人得知我深爱玛丽基亚的那一刻。唐·何塞·德·蒙特里亚的儿子，热恋着坎迪奥拉的女儿；一个小伙子，被郑重其事地指定了要做主教——主教啊，加夫列尔！在我父母的心目里，我将来是要做主教的！”
说着这话，奥古斯丁把头朝我们倚着的那堵圣墙上撞去。

===Original===
“And do you think you will go on loving Mariquilla?”
“Don’t ask me that!” he replied with energy. “Did you see her? If you saw her, how can you ask me if I will go on loving her? Her father and mine would rather see me dead than married to her. A bishop, Gabriel, they wish me to be a bishop! Think of being a bishop and loving Mariquilla for all of my life, here and hereafter, think of that and pity me!”
“But God opens unknown ways,” I said.
“It is true, and sometimes my faith is boundless. Who knows what tomorrow will bring forth? God and the Virgin shall guide me henceforth.”

===Chinese===
“那么你觉得，你还会一直爱着玛丽基亚吗？”
“别问我这个！”他斩钉截铁地答道，“难道你没看见她？既然见了她，你怎么还问我会不会继续爱她？她父亲和我父亲，宁可看我死了，也不愿看我娶她。主教啊，加夫列尔，他们要我做主教！想想吧：做一辈子主教，今生来世都爱着玛丽基亚——想想这个，就可怜可怜我吧！”
“不过，上帝自会开辟人所不知的路。”我说。
“这话不假，有时我的信心更是没有边际。谁知道明天会带来什么呢？从今往后，愿上帝和圣母指引我。”

===Original===
“Are you devoted to this Virgin?”
“Yes. My mother places candles before the one we have in our house, that I may not fall in battle; and I say to her ‘Sovereign Lady, may this offering also serve to remind you that I cannot cease from loving the daughter of Candiola.’ ”
We were in the nave upon which opened the chapel del Pilar. There is here an aperture in the wall, by which the devout, descending two or three steps, approach to kiss the pedestal which sustains the revered image. Augustine kissed the red marble. I kissed it also; then we left the church to go to our abode.

===Chinese===
“你敬奉这位圣母吗？”
“敬奉。我母亲常在家里那尊圣母像前点上蜡烛，求她保佑我不倒在战场上；我便对她说道：‘至尊的圣母啊，愿这份供奉也叫您记得，我不能不爱坎迪奥拉的女儿。’”
我们当时正站在皮拉尔圣母小堂开向的那座正殿里。这里墙上开有一个孔洞，虔敬的人循着两三级台阶下去，便可近前亲吻那承托圣像的柱础。奥古斯丁吻了那红色的大理石，我也吻了；随后我们离开教堂，回我们的住处去。

## VIII / 第八章

===Original===

The following day, the twenty-second, Palafox said to the messenger who came under a flag of truce from Moncey to propose terms of surrender.

“*I do not know how to surrender. After death, we will talk about that.*”

He followed this with a long and eloquent article which was published in the *Gazette*; but, according to general opinion, neither that document nor any of the proclamations which appeared with the signature of the commanding general were his own composition, but that of his friend, Basilio Boggiero, a man of great judgment, who was often seen in situations of danger, in the company of patriots and military leaders.

===Chinese===

次日，二十二日，帕拉福克斯对那位打着休战旗、自蒙塞军中前来商洽投降条款的信使说道：

“*我不会投降。死了以后，再来谈这件事。*”

随后他又写就一篇文辞雄辩的长文，发表在《公报》（*Gazette*）上；但依照众人的看法，无论是那篇文书，还是任何一件以总司令署名发布的公告，都并非出自他本人的手笔，而是出自他的朋友巴西利奥·博希耶罗——此人极有识见，人们常能在险境之中见到他，与爱国志士和军中将领们相伴。

===Original===

It is excusable to say that the army of the defence was very much inspired by the glorious action of the twenty-first. It was necessary to give expression to this ardor, to arrange a sortie, and so in effect it was done; but it happened that all wished to take part in this at the same time, and it was necessary to bury the dead. The sorties, arranged with prudence, were expedient; because the French, extending their lines around the city, were preparing for a regular siege, and had begun upon their outer works.

The district of Saragossa contained many people, which seemed to the common mind a great advantage, but which seemed to the intelligent a great danger, because of the immense destruction of human life which hunger would quickly bring﻿—hunger, that terrible general who is always the conqueror of overcrowded besieged cities. Because of this excess of people, the sorties were timely.

Renovales made one on the twenty-fourth with the troops of the fortress of San José, and cut down an olive grove which hid the works of the enemy.

Don John O’Neill made a sally from the suburb on the twenty-fifth with the volunteers of Aragón and Huesca, taking the chance of advantage from the enemy’s lack of preparation, and killing many of the enemy’s men.

===Chinese===

守城部队深受二十一日那场光荣战斗的鼓舞，这样说是不会错的。这股热忱须得有所抒发，须得安排一场出击，事实上也照办了；不巧的是人人都想同时上阵，而阵亡者还等着安葬。几番出击都安排得谨慎，倒也得当；因为法军正把战线绕着城池四面铺展，准备一场正规的围攻，外围工事已经破土动工了。

萨拉戈萨城区人烟稠密，在寻常人眼里这仿佛是天大的优势，在有识者看来却潜伏着莫大的危险，因为饥饿很快就要带来生命的大批毁灭——饥饿，那位可怕的将军，从来都是人满为患的围城之城的征服者。也正因为人口过剩，这几场出击来得正是时候。

二十四日，雷诺瓦莱斯率圣何塞要塞的部队出城打了一仗，砍倒了一片遮蔽敌军工事的橄榄树林。

二十五日，唐·约翰·奥尼尔（Don John O’Neill）率阿拉贡义勇军和韦斯卡义勇军自城郊出击，趁敌军疏于戒备之机大占便宜，毙敌甚众。

===Original===

On the thirty-first was made the most telling sally of all, striking in two distinct places and with considerable forces. During the early part of the day we had divided to perfection the works of the first French parallel, thrown up about three hundred and twenty yards from the walls. They were working actively, not resting by night, and we could see that they had signals of colored lanterns along the whole line. From time to time we discharged our guns, but we caused very little destruction. If troops were especially needed for a reconnoissance, they were despatched in less than no time.

The morning of the thirty-first arrived, and my battalion was charged to be ready to march upon orders from Renovales to attack the enemy in their centre, from the Torrero to the Muela road, while General Butron did the same by the Bernardona, that is to say, by the French left, sallying with sufficient forces of infantry and cavalry by the gates of Sancho and Portillo.

In order to distract the attention of the French, the general commanded that a battalion should be divided into skirmishing parties by the Tenerías, calling the attention of the enemy in that direction. In the meantime, with some of the soldiers of Olivenza and part of those of Valencia, we advanced by the Madrid road straight towards the French lines. The skirmishing parties were on both sides of the road when the enemy became aware of our presence, and now we were quicker than deer in doing up the first troop of French infantry which came to meet us. Behind a half-ruined country house some fortifications had been thrown up, and they began firing with good aim and much slaughter. For an instant we remained undecided, then some twenty men of us flanked the country house, while the rest followed the high road, pursuing the fugitives; but Renovales dashed forward and led us on, cutting down and bayoneting those who were defending the house. At the moment when we set foot within the first defence I noticed that my rank was thinned out. I saw some of my companions fall, breathing their last sighs. I looked to my right, fearing not to find my beloved friend among the living; but God had preserved him. Montoria and I were unharmed.

===Chinese===

三十一日那天，打得最见成效的一场出击开始了，兵分两处，兵力可观。当日早间，我们已用炮火把法军第一道平行壕的工事切得七零八落——那道壕线就筑在距城墙约三百二十码的地方。法军干得起劲，夜里也不歇工，我们看得出他们沿整条战线都挂起了彩色灯笼作信号。我们不时开炮轰击，收效却甚微。一旦特别需要部队前去侦察，他们转眼之间便能派出。

三十一日的早晨到了，我营奉命整装待发，一得雷诺瓦莱斯号令，便直捣敌军中路，从托雷罗直到穆埃拉大道；与此同时，布特龙将军（Butron）在贝尔纳多纳大道，也就是法军左翼，采取同样的行动，率足够的步骑兵自圣乔门与波蒂略门出城。

为牵制法军的注意，将军下令将一个营分成几队散兵，开到特内里亚斯（Tenerías）一带，把敌军的注意力引向那个方向。与此同时，我们带着一部分奥利文萨（Olivenza）的士兵和一部分瓦伦西亚的士兵，沿马德里大道径直朝法军战线推进。散兵队散在大道两侧，待敌军察觉我们的行踪，我们已比奔鹿还快，把迎面而来的头一队法国步兵收拾掉了。在一座半塌的乡舍后面，敌军仓促垒起了几处工事，开始放枪，打得又准又狠。我们迟疑了片刻，随后我们中间有二十来人从侧翼包抄那座乡舍，其余的人沿大道追击溃兵；雷诺瓦莱斯却向前猛冲，率领我们砍的砍、刺刀捅的捅，把守屋之敌一一了结。就在我们踏进第一道防线的那一刻，我发觉自己这一排的队伍稀落了。我看见几个同伴倒下去，咽了最后一口气。我朝右边望去，生怕在活着的人当中找不到我亲爱的朋友；但上帝保住了他。我和蒙特里亚都毫发无伤。

===Original===

We could not spend much time in communicating to each other the satisfaction that we felt at finding ourselves still alive, because Renovales gave orders to follow on, in the direction of the line of entrenchments that the French were raising. We abandoned the high road and made a deflection, turning to the right with the intention of joining the volunteers of Huesca, who were attacking by the Muela road.

It may be understood by what I have related that the French did not expect that sortie, and that, taken completely unawares, they were holding there, besides the scanty force that kept the works, the engineers occupied in digging the trenches of the first parallel. We attacked them vigorously, turning upon them a murderous fire, improving the minutes well before the dreaded reinforcements should arrive. We took prisoners those whom we met without arms; we shot those who had them. We took the picks and spades﻿—all this with unequalled energy, animating one another with fiery words, exalted above all by the thought that they were watching us from the city.

In this attack we were fortunate, for while we were destroying those at work on the entrenchments, the troops who had made the sortie on the left were carrying on a successful struggle with the detachments which the enemy had in the Bernardona. While the volunteers of Huesca, the grenadiers of Palafox, and the Walloon guards defeated the French infantry, the squadron of Numancia and Olivenza cavalry cautiously emerged through the Puerta de Sancho, and making a wide detour occupied the Alagón road on one side, and the Muela on the other, exactly when the French drew back from the left to the centre, in need of greater auxiliary forces. Finding themselves in their element, our fiery cavalry sprang forward, destroying whatever was encountered in the way, and then the disgraced infantry, who were fleeing towards Torrero, fell, and were trampled underfoot.

===Chinese===

彼此发现都还活着的这份欢喜，我们来不及细诉，因为雷诺瓦莱斯已下令继续前进，直扑法军正在挖掘的堑壕线。我们离开大道，折向右方，打算去同正沿穆埃拉大道进攻的韦斯卡义勇军会合。

从我上面讲述的情形可以明白，法军不曾料到这次出击；他们被杀了个措手不及，那里除看守工事的少量兵力之外，还有正在挖掘第一道平行壕的工兵。我们向他们猛扑过去，泼以一场杀气腾腾的火力，趁着那令人生畏的援军未到，把分分秒秒都用到了十足。遇上手无寸铁的，我们俘虏；手里持枪的，我们枪毙。我们夺下鹤嘴锄和铁铲——这一切都干得劲头无双，彼此用火热的言语互相打气，而最令我们意气飞扬的，是想到城里的人们正望着我们。

这一仗我们打得顺当：我们在这边歼灭掘壕之敌，从左翼出击的部队也在同敌军置于贝尔纳多纳大道的几支分队较量中占了上风。当韦斯卡义勇军、帕拉福克斯的掷弹兵和瓦隆卫队（Walloon guards）击溃法军步兵之际，努曼西亚与奥利文萨的骑兵中队悄然出了圣乔门，兜一个大圈，一面占住阿拉贡（Alagón）大道，一面占住穆埃拉大道——恰恰赶在法军因急需更多援兵、从左翼向中路收缩的当口。我们的骑兵如鱼得水，奋蹄前冲，路上撞见什么便摧毁什么；那些丧尽了体面的步兵朝托雷罗方向逃窜，纷纷倒毙，被踏在马蹄之下。

===Original===

In their dispersion, many fell beneath our bayonets, and if their desire to flee from the horses was great, great also was our anxiety to receive them in manner worthy of our swords. Some ran, throwing themselves into the trenches, not being able to jump over them; others surrendered at discretion, throwing down their arms; some defended themselves with heroism, permitting themselves to be slain before giving up; and at the last there failed not a few who, shutting themselves up in the brick kiln filled up with boughs and timber, set fire to it, preferring to die by roasting, rather than be taken prisoners.

All this which I have related in detail passed in a very short time, while the French commander, having seen enough in this hour, detached sufficient forces to hold back and punish our too audacious expedition. They beat the drum in Monte Torrero, and we saw a great force of cavalry coming against us; but we who were with Renovales had had our desire, the same as those with Butron, and were not obliged to wait for those horsemen who arrived at the end of the action; so we retired, giving them from a distance a “Good day” of the most sharp and pointed phrases in our vocabulary. We still had time to make useless some pieces placed ready for employment on the following day. We took a multitude of tools and spades, and we destroyed in all haste whatever we could of their entrenchments without losing hold upon the dozens of prisoners, of which we had taken up a collection.

Juan Pirli, one of our companions in the battalion, was carrying home to Saragossa the steel helmet of an engineer for the admiration of the public, and also a frying-pan in which were still the remnants of a breakfast begun in camp before Saragossa and ended in the other world.

===Chinese===

溃散之中，许多人倒在我们的刺刀下；他们想躲开马匹的念头有多急，我们想以配得上手中刀剑的方式“接待”他们的心愿就有多切。有的奔逃时跳不过堑壕，一头栽了进去；有的掷下武器，无条件投降；有的拼死抵抗，宁可被杀也不肯放手；到头来也不乏那样一些人，把自己关进那座堆满枝条木料的砖窑，放起火来，宁可被活活烤死，也不愿被俘。

我如此详述的这一切，都在极短的工夫里过去了；与此同时，法军指挥官在这一个钟头里已经看够了，分出足够的兵力来阻截并惩办我们这支过于大胆的远征。托雷罗山上鼓声大作，我们望见一支强大的骑兵朝我们开来；但追随雷诺瓦莱斯的我们已称了心，布特龙那边也是一样，用不着再等那些赶在战事尾声才到的骑兵；于是我们后撤，隔着老远，用我们词汇里最尖酸扎人的字句，送了他们一声“日安”。我们还来得及毁掉几门已经架好、备作次日之用的大炮，掳走大批工具铁铲，又火速捣毁了他们堑壕中一切来得及捣毁的东西，手里始终没放掉收罗来的那几十名俘虏。

营里我们的一个伙伴胡安·皮尔利（Juan Pirli），正把一顶工兵的钢盔带回萨拉戈萨给公众开眼，还捎着一口平底锅，锅里还留着些早餐的残迹——那顿早餐在萨拉戈萨城前的营地里开的头，到另一个世界里才收的尾。

===Original===

We had had nine killed and eight wounded in our battalion. When Augustine rejoined me near the Carmen gate, I noticed that one of his hands was stained with blood.

“Are you wounded?” I asked, examining the hand. “It is nothing more than a scratch.”

“It is a scratch,” he replied; “but it was not made by ball, lance, nor sabre, but by teeth, because when I gripped that Frenchman who lifted up his pick to brain me, the damned fellow set his teeth into my hand like a dog at bay.”

===Chinese===

我们营里阵亡九人，负伤八人。奥古斯丁在卡门城门附近同我会合时，我看见他一只手上沾着血。

“你受伤了？”我一面察看那只手，一面问道。“不过是擦破点儿皮。”

“是擦破点儿皮，”他答道，“可它不是枪弹、长矛也不是马刀留下的，是牙齿咬的——我揪住那个举起鹤嘴锄要砸碎我脑袋的法国佬时，那该死的家伙像条被逼上绝路的狗，一口咬进了我的手。”

===Original===

When we entered the city, some by the Puerta del Carmen, some by the Portillo, all the pieces of the redoubts and forts of Mediodía poured a fire against the columns which were coming after us. The two sorties combined had done damage enough to the French. In addition to losing many men, a small part of their entrenchments had been made useless to them, and we had possessed ourselves of a considerable number of their tools. Besides this, the official engineers that Butron took with him on that daring venture had had time to examine the works of the besiegers, and measure them, and could give descriptions of them to the commanding general.

The rampart wall was invaded by the people. They had heard within the city the shooting of the skirmishes, and men and women, old people and children had run out to see what glorious action was bulletined on the plaza. We were received with exclamations of rejoicing, and from San José all the way to the Trinitarios, the long line of men and women, looking towards the battlefield, climbed upon the walls, and clapping their hands at our arrival, waving their handkerchiefs, presented a magnificent sight. After the cannon sounded, the redoubts together poured a fire upon the field that we had just abandoned, and their voices seemed a triumphal salvo, as it mingled with the huzzas and shouts of joy.

In the surrounding houses, the windows and balconies were filled with women, and the interest or curiosity of some of those in the streets was such that they went into the hurrying crowd in numbers, and up to the cannons, to congratulate the brave souls and soothe with kind words their nerves, high strung with the noise of artillery, which is unlike anything else in the world. It was necessary to command the multitude to depart from the fortress at the Portillo. The crowd in the Santa Engracia gave that place the aspect of a theatre, of a public festival. The fire of the cannon ceased at last, having no more need to protect our retreat, and the Castle Aljafería alone sent an occasional shot against the works of the enemy.

In reward for our action on that day it was granted us on the next to wear a red ribbon on the breast by way of decoration; in justice to the hazards of that sortie, Father Boggiero told us, among other things uttered by the mouth of our general, “Yesterday you marked the last day of the year with an action worthy of yourselves. At the sound of the bugle your swords leaped from their scabbards and struck to the ground haughty heads humbled by your valor and patriotism. Numantia! Olivenza! I have now seen that your light horse will know how to preserve the honor of this army and the enthusiasm of these sacred walls! Wear these bloodstained swords that are the sign of your glory and the protection of your country.”

===Chinese===

我们进城时，有的走卡门城门，有的走波蒂略门，梅迪奥迪亚（Mediodía）各棱堡与堡垒的火炮一齐朝尾追我们的敌军纵队倾泻炮火。两路出击合在一起，已够法军受的了：除了折损大批人马，他们一小部分堑壕已形同废物，相当数量的工具也落到了我们手里。此外，布特龙带去参加那次冒险的随军工兵还来得及察看围攻者的工事并加以丈量，得以向总司令一一禀报其形势。

城墙被百姓们占领了。城里的人听见了散战的枪声，男男女女、老老少少都跑出来，要看广场上战报里通报的是怎样一场光荣的战斗。我们在一片欢呼之声中受到迎接；从圣何塞直到三一会修道院，长长的男女人流面向战场，爬满城墙，见我们到来便鼓着掌、挥着手帕，蔚为大观。炮声响过之后，各棱堡一齐朝我们刚刚撤离的原野倾泻炮火，那炮声同欢呼声、欢乐的呐喊声交织成一片，恰似一排凯旋的礼炮。

周围人家的窗口和阳台上挤满了妇女；街上有些人出于关切或者好奇，竟成群结队地涌进匆匆的人流，一直挤到大炮跟前，向勇士们道贺，用好言好语抚慰他们被炮声震得紧绷的神经——炮声这东西，世上再没有一样比得上。只得下令让人群撤出波蒂略门的那座炮台。圣恩格拉西亚的人群，使那地方显出一副剧场的气象、一场公众节庆的气象。炮声终于停息，因为再不必掩护我们的撤退，只剩阿尔哈费里亚城堡还不时朝敌军工事发上一炮。

为犒赏我们当日的战功，次日我们获准在胸前佩一条红绶带，以作荣典。为表彰那次出击的艰险，博希耶罗神父向我们转达了出自我们将军之口的种种言辞，其中有这样一段：“昨天，你们用一场无愧于自身的战斗，给这一年的最后一天作了记号。号声一响，你们的剑便跃出剑鞘，把一颗颗高傲的头颅斩落在地——它们已被你们的勇武与爱国心压服。努曼西亚！奥利文萨！如今我亲眼见到了：你们的轻骑兵必能守住这支军队的荣誉和这几堵圣墙的热忱！佩上这些染血的剑吧，它们是你们荣耀的标志，是你们祖国的干城。”

## IX / 第九章

===Original===
From that day, as memorable in the second siege as Eras in the first siege, began the great work in whose frenzy and exaltation both besiegers and besieged lived for the next month and a half. The sorties made during the first days of January were not of much importance. The French, having finished their first parallel, advanced in a zigzag towards opening their second, and worked on it with so much activity that very soon we saw our two best positions in the Mediodía, San José and the redoubt del Pilar, threatened by siege batteries, every one with a dozen cannon. I must be excused for saying that we did not cease to make trouble for them, keeping up an incessant fire, and surprising them with sudden skirmishes, but this was all. Junot, who now took the place of Moncey, carried forward the work with great diligence.

Our battalion remained in the redoubt raised at the outer end of the Huerva bridge. The radius of our fire was considerable, crossing that of San José. The batteries of Los Mártires, of the Botanical Garden, and of the Torre del Pino further within the city were less important than the two bodies holding the advanced positions, and served as auxiliaries.

Numbers of Saragossan volunteers were with us in the garrison, some of the soldiers of the guard, and various armed peasants who rather elected themselves to our corps than came into it by our choice. Eight cannons held the redoubt. Don Domingo Larripa was our leader. The artillery was commanded by Don Francisco Betbezé. As chief of engineers, we had the great Simono, high official of that distinguished service, and a man of such quality that he was able to quote himself as a model of all good military men, both in valor and in knowledge.

The redoubt was a work sufficiently strong for the purpose, and not lacking in any material requisite for defending itself. Over the entrance gate at the extremity of the bridge its constructors had placed a tablet with this inscription﻿—

> The indestructible redoubt of our Lady of the Pillar. Saragossans! Die for the Virgin del Pilar or conquer!

===Chinese===
从那一天起——它在第二次围城中的分量，恰如埃拉斯（Eras）之于第一次围城——那桩浩大的工程动工了；此后一个半月，围攻者与被围者都活在它的狂热与亢奋之中。一月头几天的几次出击无足轻重。法军筑好第一道平行壕，又蛇形掘进，着手开辟第二道，干得热火朝天，不多时我们便眼看着自己在梅迪奥迪亚（Mediodía）的两处最佳阵地——圣何塞（San José）与皮拉尔棱堡（redoubt del Pilar）——受起了攻城炮群的威胁，座座炮台都配着十几门炮。请恕我直言：我们可没有停止给他们捣乱，火力昼夜不息，还常以突发的遭遇战惊扰他们——不过也就到此为止了。朱诺（Junot）此时已接替蒙塞元帅（Moncey）的位置，推进这项工程极尽勤勉。

我们营仍驻在韦尔瓦河（Huerva）桥外端筑起的那座棱堡里。我们火力的半径相当可观，与圣何塞的火力交叉成网。洛斯·马尔蒂雷斯（Los Mártires）炮台、植物园（Botanical Garden）炮台，以及更靠城内的皮诺塔（Torre del Pino）炮台，都不如扼守前哨阵地的那两处吃重，只充辅助之用。

与我们同驻棱堡的，有一批萨拉戈萨义勇军战士、几名卫队士兵，还有各色武装起来的庄稼汉——与其说是我们挑中了他们入队，不如说是他们自己选了自己进我们这支队伍。棱堡上安着八门大炮。唐·多明戈·拉里帕（Don Domingo Larripa）是我们的长官；炮兵由唐·弗朗西斯科·贝特贝泽（Don Francisco Betbezé）指挥；担任工兵长官的，则是那位了不起的西莫诺（Simono），那一赫赫兵种里的高级官员——他这人质地之优，竟能拿自己当作一切好军人的楷模，论勇武如此，论学问亦然。

这座棱堡就其用途而言足够坚牢，自卫所需的一切器材物资无一欠缺。在桥尽头的大门上方，建造者们嵌了一方石碑，镌着铭文——

> 皮拉尔圣母坚不可摧棱堡。萨拉戈萨人！为皮拉尔圣母而死，否则就夺取胜利！

===Original===
We had our lodging within the redoubt, and though the place was not altogether bad, we went on poorly enough. The rations were provided by a committee recommended by the military administration; but this committee, to our sorrow, was not able to attend to us properly. By good fortune, and to the honor of that generous people, food was sent to us from the neighboring houses, the best of their provisions; and we were frequently visited by the charitable women, who since the battle of the thirty-first had taken it upon themselves to nurse and care for our poor wounded heroes.

I have not spoken of Pirli. Pirli was a boy from outside the city, a rustic about twenty years of age, and in such jolly condition that the most dangerous situations only moved him to a nervous and feverish joy. I never saw him sad. He met the French singing; and when the bullets whistled past his head, he capered about, making a thousand grotesque gestures, throwing up his hands and fairly dancing. When the fire was thick as hail, he called the bullets “hailstones.” He called the cannonballs “hot cakes;” he called the hand-grenades, “señoras;” and the powder he called “black flour,” using other queer terms which I do not now remember. Pirli, although not at all a serious person, was a charming companion.

I do not know whether I have spoken of Tío Garcés. He was a man of forty-five years, a native of Garrapinillos, very brave, bronzed, sawed-off looking, with limbs of steel; there was no one so active or so imperturbable under fire. He was somewhat talkative, and was a little inclined to be imprudent in his conversation, but with a certain wit in his garrulity. He had a small estate in the environs, and a very modest house; but he had levelled it with his own hands, and cut down his pear-trees, so that the enemy could not use them. I heard of a thousand of his valorous deeds in the first siege, and he wore a decoration on his right sleeve, the embroidered shield of distinction of the sixteenth of August. He dressed badly, and went almost half-naked, not because he lacked clothing, but because he had not time to put it on. He and others like him were without doubt those who inspired the celebrated phrase of which I have already made mention: “Their bodies were clothed only in glory.” He slept without shelter, and ate less than an anchorite; indeed with two pieces of bread and a couple of bites of dried beef hard as hide, he had rations for the day.

===Chinese===
我们的宿营就在棱堡里。这地方虽说并不算坏，日子却过得够呛。口粮由军政当局推荐的一个委员会经办；可惜——说来痛心——这委员会实在照应不周。所幸，这也足为那个慷慨的民族增光：邻近的人家把家中最好的吃食给我们送来；慈善的妇女们也频频过访——自三十一日之战以后，她们便主动担起了看护照料我们那些可怜的伤员英雄的责任。

我还没有讲过皮尔利（Pirli）。皮尔利是城外来的小伙子，乡野人，二十岁上下，天生一副快活性子，最凶险的处境也只能激起他一阵亢奋狂热的欢喜。我从没见过他发愁。他唱着歌去会法军；子弹贴着脑袋呼啸而过，他连蹦带跳，做出一千种怪模怪样的姿势，双手直往天上扬，活脱脱是在跳舞。炮火密得如冰雹时，他管子弹叫“冰雹粒”；管炮弹叫“热煎饼”；管手榴弹叫“太太们”（señoras）；火药呢，他叫“黑面粉”——还有别的古怪名目，我如今记不起来了。皮尔利虽说压根儿不是个严肃的人，却是个招人爱的伙伴。

我不知道讲没讲过加尔塞斯大叔（Tío Garcés）。此人四十五岁，加拉皮尼约斯（Garrapinillos）人，极为勇猛，面孔晒得黝黑，矮墩墩的五短身材，四肢筋骨如钢；枪林弹雨之下，再没有谁比他更利索、更沉得住气的了。他有点儿话多，谈吐间不大有把门的，可絮叨里自有一股机趣。他在城郊有一小块田产、一所顶朴素的房子；可他亲手把房子铲平了，还把自家的梨树砍倒，免得被敌人利用。第一次围城时他的千百桩英勇事迹我时有所闻；他右边袖子上还佩着一枚功勋标志——绣制的八月十六日功勋盾章。他衣衫不整，几乎半裸着身子，倒不是缺衣少穿，而是没工夫往身上套。他，以及与他相仿的人们，无疑正是那句名言所咏叹的人——那话我前头已经提过：“他们的身躯，只以荣耀为衣。”他露天宿夜，吃得比隐修士还少；真的，两块面包、几口硬得像皮革的牛肉干，便够他一天的口粮。

===Original===
He was a man somewhat given to meditation. When he saw the works of the second parallel, he said, looking at the French: “Thanks be to God, they are drawing near. *Cuerno! Cuerno!* these people are a trial to one’s patience!”

“What a hurry you are in, Uncle Garcés,” we said to him.

“I should say so. I want to plant my trees again before winter is over. And next month I want to build my little house again.”

Truly Tío Garcés should have worn a tablet on his brow like that on the bridge, reading, “An unconquerable man.”

===Chinese===
他这人还算有点好沉思的脾气。见到第二道平行壕的工事，他望着法军说道：“感谢上帝，他们靠近了。*Cuerno! Cuerno!*（见鬼！见鬼！）这帮家伙真能磨人的性子！”

我们对他说：“加尔塞斯大叔，瞧您急的。”

“可不嘛。我要赶在冬尽之前，把我的树重新栽上。下个月，我还要把我的小房子再盖起来。”

说真的，加尔塞斯大叔额头上本该也嵌一方碑，就像桥上那块一样，上书：“一个不可战胜的人。”

===Original===
But who comes there, advancing slowly along the valley of the Huerva, leaning upon a thick stick and followed by a lively little dog which barked at all the passersby, merely for mischief, without any intention of biting? It is the friar, Father Mateo del Busto, reader and qualifier of the order of Saint Francis de Paula, chaplain of the second company of Saragossa volunteers, an important man, who, in spite of his age, was seen during the first siege in all places of danger, succoring the wounded, helping the dying, carrying ammunition to the well, and cheering all by his gentle accents. Entering the redoubt, he showed us a large and heavy basket which he had toiled to bring here, and in which was food better than that of our ordinary table.

“These cakes,” said he, placing it on the ground, and taking out one by one things which he named as he produced them, “have been given me in the house of that most excellent lady, the Countess de Bureta, and this in the house of Don Pedro Ric. Here you have a couple of slices of ham from my convent, which was for Father Loshollos, whose stomach is not strong, but he renounced this luxury and gave it to me to take to you. See, how does this bottle of wine look to you? How much would those foreign fellows yonder give for it?”

We all looked towards the plain. The little dog, leaping impudently upon the wall, began to bark at the French lines.

“I have also brought you a couple of pounds of dried fruit which has been kept in the dispensary at our house. We were going to preserve them in liquor, but you taste them first of anyone, my brave boys. I have not forgotten thee at all, my beloved Pirli,” he continued, turning to the boy of that name; “and as you are half naked, and without a cloak, I have brought you a magnificent covering. Do you see this bundle? Well, here is an old gown that I have kept to give to a poor man. Now, I present it to you as a covering for your body. It is unsuitable clothing for a soldier; but if the gown does not make the monk, neither does the uniform make the soldier. Put it on, and you will be very comfortable in it.”

===Chinese===
可是那边来的是谁？只见他拄着一根粗手杖，顺着韦尔瓦河谷缓缓走来，身后跟着一只欢腾的小狗，见着过往行人一概汪汪直叫——纯粹是促狭，全无咬人之意。来者是那位修士，马特奥·德尔·布斯托神父（Father Mateo del Busto），圣方济各·德·保拉修会（order of Saint Francis de Paula）的诵经师兼审订员、萨拉戈萨义勇军第二连随军神父，是位非同小可的人物：第一次围城时虽说上了年纪，各处险地却都见得到他，救护伤者，扶助垂死，往井边搬运弹药，还以温言软语鼓舞众人。一进棱堡，他就给我们看一只又大又沉的篮子——那是他一路辛劳提到这儿来的——里头装的吃食，比我们平日的饭桌强得多。

他把篮子往地上一放，一样一样往外掏，掏出一样便报一样名目：“这些点心，是那位极贤德的夫人布雷塔伯爵夫人（Countess de Bureta）府上给的；这一样，是唐·佩德罗·里克（Don Pedro Ric）府上的。这儿有几片火腿，出自敝修道院，本是给洛斯霍约斯神父（Father Loshollos）的——他胃口不强，可他把这份口福让了出来，叫我带给你们。瞧，这瓶酒在你们眼里如何？那边那些外国佬肯出多少价钱换它？”

我们齐朝平原望去。那只小狗放肆地纵身跳上胸墙，冲着法军战线吠了起来。

“我还给你们带来了两磅果干，一直存在我们会院的配药室里。我们本打算拿烧酒把它们浸藏起来，可你们倒比谁都先尝了鲜，我勇敢的孩子们。我可一点儿没忘了你，我心爱的皮尔利，”他转向那个名字的少年，接着说道，“你半裸着身子，又没有斗篷，我给你带来了一件堂皇的遮身之物。看见这个包袱没有？喏，这里头是一件旧长袍，我一直留着要舍给哪个穷人的。如今我把它送你遮体。这衣裳给当兵的穿固然不合规矩；可是修士袍造不出修士，军装也同样造不出士兵。穿上吧，穿上保管你舒舒服服。”

===Original===
The friar gave our friend his parcel, and Pirli put on the garment, laughing and dancing about; and as he was still carrying on his head the engineer’s helmet which he had taken in the enemy’s camp on the thirty-first, he presented a figure stranger than can readily be imagined.

A little later, several women also arrived with baskets of provisions. The arrival of femininity swiftly transformed the aspect of the redoubt. I do not know from where they produced a guitar; it is certain that they produced one from somewhere. One of those present graciously began to play the measures of that incomparable, divine, immortal dance, the *jota*, and in a moment a great revelry of dancing was going on.

Pirli, whose grotesque figure began in a French engineer and ended in a Spanish friar, was the most carried away of any of the dancers, and could not keep tune with his partner, a most graceful girl in Spanish highland dress, who was called Manuela, whom I noticed the first moment that I saw her. She was about twenty-two years of age, and was slender, of a pure pale complexion. The excitement of the dance quickly flushed her cheeks, and by degrees her movements grew more lively, unmindful of fatigue. With her eyes half shut, her cheeks rosy, her arms moving to the music of the sweet strains, she shook her skirts with lively grace; taking her steps lightly, and presenting to us now her brow, and now her shoulders, Manuela held us enchanted.

The ardor of the dancing crowd, the lively music, and the enthusiasm of the rest of the dancers augmented her own, until at last, breathless with fatigue, she dropped her arms and fell to earth like a stone or a pomegranate.

===Chinese===
修士把包袱给了我们的朋友。皮尔利又笑又跳地把衣裳套上身；由于他头上还顶着三十一日那天从敌人营地里得来的工兵头盔，那副尊容之古怪，绝非轻易想象所能企及。

过不多时，又有几位妇女也提着食品篮子到了。女性一驾到，棱堡的面貌登时改观。我说不上她们从哪儿弄来一把吉他；反正确凿是从哪儿弄来了一把。在场的有一位大大方方地拨动琴弦，奏起那无可比拟、神圣而不朽的舞蹈——霍塔舞（jota）的节拍，转眼间一场欢腾的群舞便闹腾开了。

皮尔利那副怪相——上半截起于法国工兵，下半截收于西班牙修士——是众舞者里最忘乎所以的一个，偏又合不上他舞伴的拍子。那舞伴是个身段极为曼妙的姑娘，一身西班牙山地装束，名叫玛努埃拉（Manuela），我头一眼见她便留了意。她年约二十二岁，身形苗条，肤色素净白皙。舞蹈的兴奋很快染红了她的双颊；她的动作渐渐愈见活泼，全不顾疲倦。半阖着眼，两颊泛红，双臂和着甜美的乐声起落，她轻盈曼妙地抖动裙裾；舞步轻轻，一时将额头对着我们，一时又将肩头送将过来——玛努埃拉把我们众人全都迷住了。

舞人群的热烈、音乐的活泼，还有其余舞者的兴头，把她的兴头也愈扇愈高；到末了她累得喘不过气来，双臂一垂，像块石头、又像只石榴似的跌坐在地。

===Original===
Pirli stood over her, and surrounded her by a sort of corral formed of himself and the basket of provisions.

“Let us see what you have brought us, Manuelilla,” said Pirli. “If ’twere not for thee and Father Busto, we should die of hunger. And if it were not for this little dance with which we get rid of the bad taste of the ‘hot cakes’ and the ‘señoras,’ what would become of us poor soldiers?”

“I bring you whatever there is,” replied Manuela, opening the basket of provisions. “Wait a little. If the siege lasts, you will be eating bricks.”

“We shall have bullets mixed with black flour,” said Pirli. “Manuelilla, have you got over being afraid of the bullets yet?” Saying this, he seized his gun, and shot it off into the air. The girl gave a sharp scream, and, startled, sprang up as if to escape.

===Chinese===
皮尔利守在她跟前，拿自己这条身子连同那只食品篮，圈成一道畜栏似的围栏，把她围在里头。

“来瞧瞧你给我们带了什么，玛努埃莉利亚（Manuelilla），”皮尔利说，“若不是有你和布斯托神父，我们非饿死不可。再说，若不是靠这支小舞把‘热煎饼’和‘太太们’留下的坏味道赶跑，我们这些可怜的大兵可怎么好？”

“有什么我就带什么呗。”玛努埃拉一面打开食品篮，一面答道，“稍待片刻。这围城要是再拖下去，你们就该吃砖头了。”

“那我们就子弹拌着黑面粉吃。”皮尔利说，“玛努埃莉利亚，你那怕子弹的毛病改掉了没有？”说着，他抓起枪，朝天放了一响。姑娘尖叫一声，吃了一惊，跳将起来，像要逃走似的。

===Original===
“It is nothing, daughter,” said the friar; “brave women are not afraid of powder. On the contrary, they should take as much pleasure in it as in the sound of castanets and mandolins.”

“When I hear a ball,” said Manuela, coming slowly and timidly back, “there is not a drop of blood left in my veins.”

At this moment the French, wishing to try the artillery of their second parallel, shot off a cannon, and the ball came against the wall of the redoubt, shattering the loose bricks into a thousand pieces. Everybody rose to look at the enemy. The highland girl cried out in terror; and Tío Garcés was moved to scream through a loophole at the French, heaping upon them the most insolent words, accompanied by many exclamations. The little dog, running from one end of the place to the other, barked furiously.

“Manuela, let us dance another jota to the sound of this music, and viva the Virgin del Pilar,” cried Pirli, jumping about like one out of his senses.

===Chinese===
“没什么，闺女，”修士说，“勇敢的女人不怕火药。恰恰相反，她们该像听响板和曼陀林的声响那样，从火药声里品出乐趣来。”

“我一听见炮弹响，”玛努埃拉慢慢腾腾、怯生生地挪回来，说道，“我血管里就一滴血也不剩了。”

正当此时，法军要试试他们第二道平行壕的炮，放了一炮；炮弹打在棱堡墙上，把松动的砖石崩成千片。人人起身去看敌人。山地姑娘惊叫出声；加尔塞斯大叔按捺不住，凑着射击孔朝法军大喊，把顶不客气的话一股脑儿砸过去，还夹着连声的叫骂。小狗从阵地这头跑到那头，狂吠不止。

“玛努埃拉，咱们就着这路音乐的声响，再跳一支霍塔舞，皮拉尔圣母万岁！”皮尔利喊道，蹦来跳去，像失了魂一般。

===Original===
Manuela rose on tiptoe, impelled by curiosity, and slowly stretched up her head to look at the camp from the wall. Then, casting her glance over the level plain, she seemed to dissipate, little by little, the terrors of her fainting spirit; and at last we saw her surveying the enemy’s lines with a certain serenity, and even with a little complacency.

“One, two, three cannon!” she said, counting the fiery mouths which were discernible at that distance. “Come, little boys, don’t be afraid. This is nothing to you!”

Over near San José was heard the booming of guns, and on our redoubt sounded the drum calling to arms. From the neighboring stronghold had sallied forth a little column that exchanged distant shots with the French workmen. Some of these, running to their left, placed themselves within arm’s length of our fire. We all ran to the walls, disposed to send them a few hailstones, and without waiting orders, some of us discharged our guns with loud huzzas.

All the women fled by the bridge towards the city except Manuela. Did fear prevent her from moving? No. Her fear was great; she trembled, and her teeth chattered; her face grew pale; but an irresistible curiosity kept her in the redoubt. She fastened her gaze on the sharpshooters, and on the cannon that was about to be discharged.

===Chinese===
玛努埃拉受好奇心驱使，踮起脚尖，慢慢探出头去，从墙头张望敌营。而后她放眼平坦的原野，仿佛一点一点驱散了心头昏晕的恐惧；末了我们见她打量敌军战线时，神情竟颇为镇定，甚至还带着几分自得。

“一门、两门、三门炮！”她数着隔着那么远也辨认得出的喷火之口，说道，“来吧，小家伙们，别怕。这对你们算不得什么！”

圣何塞近旁响起了枪炮声，我们棱堡上也敲起了备战鼓。从邻近堡垒里出击了一小队人马，同法军工兵远远地对射。工兵里有几个朝他们左翼跑去，离我们的火力只一臂之遥。我们全都奔上墙头，打算赏他们几颗“冰雹粒”，有几个人不待号令，便高声呐喊着放了枪。

妇女们全都取道大桥往城里逃，唯独玛努埃拉除外。是恐惧绊住了她么？不。她怕得厉害；她发着抖，牙齿打战；脸也白了：可一股遏制不住的好奇心把她拴在了棱堡里。她的目光牢牢钉在那些射手身上，钉在那门即将开火的大炮上。

===Original===
“Manuela, are you not going?” said Augustine. “Doesn’t it frighten you to look at all that?”

The girl, with her attention fixed on the spectacle, terrified, trembling, with white lips and palpitating bosom, neither moved nor spoke.

“Manuelilla,” said Pirli, running up to her, “take my gun and shoot it off.”

Contrary to what we expected, Manuelilla did not show any sign of terror.

“Take it, please,” cried Pirli, making her take the gun. “Put your thumb here. Aim over yonder. Fire! Viva the second artillery woman! Viva Manuela Sancho and the Virgin del Pilar!”

The girl took the gun, and, to judge by her actions, and the stupor of her looks, it seemed as if she did not know what she was doing. But, raising the gun with a trembling hand, she aimed at the field, pulled the trigger, and fired.

===Chinese===
“玛努埃拉，你还不走么？”奥古斯丁说，“看着这一切，你不害怕？”

姑娘的心思全在那景象上，惊恐万状，浑身发抖，嘴唇刷白，胸脯起伏，却一动不动，一言不发。

“玛努埃莉利亚，”皮尔利跑到她跟前说，“拿我的枪放一响。”

出乎我们意料，玛努埃莉利亚没有露出半点惊惶之色。

“拿着吧，劳驾，”皮尔利喊道，把枪塞进她手里，“拇指扣在这儿。朝那边瞄准。开火！第二位炮兵女杰万岁！玛努埃拉·桑乔（Manuela Sancho）万岁，皮拉尔圣母万岁！”

姑娘接过枪来；看她那举动、看她那失神的眼光，仿佛并不知道自己在做什么。可是，她用颤抖的手举起枪，朝田野瞄准，扣了扳机，开了火。

===Original===
A thousand fiery shouts of applause greeted the discharge, and the girl left the gun. She was radiant with satisfaction, and her delight deepened the roses in her cheeks.

“Do you see? You have already lost your fear?” said the priest. “There is nothing more in these things than taking hold of them. All the Saragossan women ought to do the same, and then Augustina Casta Álvarez would not be the one glorious exception to her sex.”

“Bring another gun,” exclaimed the girl; “I wish to fire again.”

“They have already marched off, if you please! Aren’t you a good one!” said Pirli, preparing to make an onslaught on the provision basket. “Tomorrow, if you like, you shall be invited for a few ‘hot cakes.’ Well, let us make ourselves comfortable and eat.”

===Chinese===
千百声火热的喝彩迎接这一响，姑娘放下了枪。她满脸称心如意的光彩，那份欢喜把双颊的红晕渲得更深了。

“瞧见没有？你已经不怕了吧？”神父说，“这些事无非在于上手一试。萨拉戈萨的女人人人都该照样办，那样一来，阿古斯蒂娜·卡斯塔·阿尔瓦雷斯（Augustina Casta Álvarez）就不会成为众姐妹中独一无二的光荣例外了。”

“再去拿一支枪来，”姑娘喊道，“我还要再放一响。”

“你瞧，人家居然早开走了！你可真是好样的！”皮尔利说着，准备向食品篮子发动总攻，“明天你若愿意，请你吃几发‘热煎饼’。好啦，咱们安顿下来，吃吧。”

===Original===
The friar, calling his little dog, said to him: “That is enough, my son; don’t bark so, nor take it so much to heart that you make yourself hoarse. Keep your boldness until tomorrow. Today, we have no wish to employ it, for if I am not mistaken they are hurrying away to get behind their works.”

In fact the skirmish at San José had concluded, and for the moment the French were not in sight. A short time afterwards the sound of the guitar was renewed, and the women returning, the sweet undulations of the jota began again with Manuela Sancho and the great Pirli in the first line.

===Chinese===
修士唤过小狗，对它说道：“够了，我的孩子；别这么叫，也别这么上心，叫得自己嗓子都哑了。把你的胆气留到明天吧。今天我们用不着它——要是我没弄错，他们正赶着撤回工事后面去呢。”

果然，圣何塞那边的接战已经收场，法军一时不见了踪影。片刻之后，吉他声又起，妇女们也回来了，霍塔舞甜美的波荡再度开始，领头的正是玛努埃拉·桑乔和了不起的皮尔利。

## X / 第十章

===Original===
When I woke at daybreak the next morning I saw Montoria, who was passing by the wall.

“I believe that the bombardment is going to begin,” he said to me; “there is a great activity in the enemy’s lines.”

“They will try to demolish this redoubt,” I said, getting up lazily. “How gloomy the sky is, Augustine! Day dawns very sadly.”

“I believe they will attack on all sides at once, until they have made their second parallel. Do you know that Napoleon in Paris, knowing the resistance shown by this city in the first siege, was furious with Lefebre Desnouettes because he assaulted the plaza by the Portillo and the Castle Aljafería? He called for a plan of Saragossa, and they gave it to him, and he showed that the city should be attacked by Santa Engracia.”

“By this place? A black day is indeed dawning for us if the orders of Napoleon are carried out. Tell me, have we anything to eat here?”

“I did not show it to you before because I wished to surprise you,” he said to me, showing me a basket which served as the tomb of two cold roast fowls, some comfits and fine preserves.
===Chinese===
次日拂晓我醒来时，看见蒙特里亚正打墙边走过。

“我看炮击就要开始了，”他对我说，“敌军阵地上动静很大。”

“他们是想把这座棱堡轰塌。”我懒洋洋地爬起来说道，“天色多么阴沉啊，奥古斯丁！这一天破晓得多么凄惨。”

“我看他们会一齐进攻四面，直到掘成第二道平行壕。你可知道，拿破仑在巴黎听说了这座城在第一次围城中显示的抵抗力，对勒费布尔·德努埃特（Lefebre Desnouettes）大发雷霆，因为他竟从波蒂略门和阿尔哈费里亚城堡去强攻城池。他叫人取萨拉戈萨的城防图来，人们把图呈上，他指出，这座城应当从圣恩格拉西亚下手攻打。”

“从这地方下手？倘若拿破仑的命令果真施行，我们迎来的确实是一个黑暗的日子。告诉我，咱们这儿有什么吃的没有？”

“我先前不给你看，是想让你惊喜一下。”他对我说着，给我看一只篮子，那篮子权充着两只冷烤鸡、几色蜜饯和上好果酱的坟墓。

===Original===
“You brought these last night? Indeed! How could you go out of the redoubt?”

“I got leave from the general for an hour, and Mariquilla prepared this feast. If Candiola knows that two of the hens from his chicken-corral have been killed and roasted to regale two of the defenders of the city, the devil will be to pay. Let us eat then, Señor Araceli, while we await the bombardment. Here it comes. One bomb! Another, another!”

The right batteries opened fire upon San José and the Pilar, and what a fire! The whole army seemed behind the cannon. Away with breakfasts, away with the morning meal, away with tidbits!﻿—the men of Aragón will have no food but glory!

The unconquerable fortress answered the insolent besieger with a tremendous cannonade, and soon the great soul of our fatherland moved within us. The balls, beating upon the brick walls and the earthworks, beat down the redoubt as if it were a toy pelted with stones by a boy. The grenades, falling among us, burst with a great noise, and the bombs, passing with awful majesty over our heads, went on to fall into the streets and upon the roofs of the houses.
===Chinese===
“这些是你昨夜带来的？好家伙！你是怎么出的棱堡？”

“我向将军告了一个钟头的假，玛丽基亚备下了这顿盛宴。要是坎迪奥拉知道他家鸡栏里的两只母鸡被宰了、烤了，来款待两个守城的人，那可要闹翻天了。那么就吃吧，阿拉塞利先生，边吃边等炮击。来了。一颗炸弹！又一颗，又一颗！”

右翼各炮台朝圣何塞和皮拉尔棱堡开火了，好一场炮火！仿佛整支大军都压在大炮后面。早饭也罢，早点也罢，可口的美味也罢，统统见鬼去吧！——阿拉贡人不要别的食粮，只要光荣！

不可征服的堡垒以雷霆万钧的炮击回敬那无礼的围攻者，不多时，我们祖国的伟大魂魄便在我们胸中鼓荡起来。炮弹敲打在砖墙和土垒上，把棱堡砸得如同顽童拿石子投掷的一件玩具。手榴弹落进我们中间，訇然炸裂；炸弹带着骇人的威仪从我们头顶掠过，一路向前，落进街巷，砸上屋顶。

===Original===
Everybody out! Let there be no idle or cowardly people in the city. The men to the walls, the women to the bloody hospitals, the children and priests to carry ammunition! Let no notice be taken of these dreadful and burning things which bore through roofs, penetrate dwelling-houses, open gates, pierce floors, descend to the cellars, and, bursting, scatter the flames of hell upon the tranquil hearth, surprising with death the aged invalid on his couch and the child in his cradle. Nothing of this sort matters. Everybody out into the street, and thus save honor though the city perish, and the churches and convents and hospitals and the estates which are but earthly things! The Saragossans, despising material good as they despised life, lived by their spirits in the infinite spaces of the ideal.

In the first moments the Captain-General and many other distinguished personages visited us﻿—such as Don Mariano Cereso the priest of Sas, General O’Neill, San Genís, and Don Pedro Ric. There was also there the brave and generous Don José Montoria, who embraced his son, saying to him: “Today is the day to conquer or to die. We will meet each other in heaven.”

Behind Montoria, Don Roque presented himself; he had become a brave fellow, and as he had been employed in the sanitary service, he began to show a feverish activity before there were any wounded, and displayed to us a good sized pile of lint. Various friars mingled among the combatants during the early firing, encouraging us with mystic fervor.
===Chinese===
全员出动！城里不许有闲人或懦夫。男人上城墙，女人进血污的医院，孩子和教士去搬弹药！那些可怕的燃烧之物，钻透屋顶，窜进住家，撞开大门，穿透地板，直下地窖，炸裂开来，把地狱之火撒在安宁的炉灶上，让病榻上的老弱和摇篮里的婴儿猝然死去——这些都不要放在心上。全员上街！如此方能保全荣誉，纵使城市倾覆，纵使教堂、修道院、医院和一切无非身外之物的产业尽毁！萨拉戈萨人鄙弃财物一如鄙弃性命，凭着精神，活在理想的无限天地之中。

炮击伊始，总司令和许多显要人物莅临我们这里——如萨斯教区神父唐·马里亚诺·塞雷索、奥尼尔将军、桑·赫尼斯（San Genís）和唐·佩德罗·里克。勇敢而慷慨的唐·何塞·蒙特里亚也在那里，他拥抱着儿子对他说：“今天是取胜或赴死的日子。我们天堂再见。”

蒙特里亚身后，唐·罗克也露面了；他已经锻炼成一条好汉，又当着救护勤务的差事，伤员还没出现，他就先干得热火朝天，还给我们亮出一大摞敷伤用的亚麻布绒。开火之初，还有好些修士穿插在战斗人员中间，以神秘的狂热鼓舞我们。

===Original===
At the same time, and with equal fury, the French attacked the redoubt del Pilar and the fortress of San José. The latter, although more formidable in aspect, had less power of resistance, perhaps because it presented a broader target for the enemy’s fires. But Renovales was there with the Huesca and the Valencia volunteers, the Walloon guards, and various members of the militia of Soria. The great lack of the fortress was in its having been constructed for the protection of a vast edifice, which the enemy’s artillery converted into ruins in a little while; pieces of the thick wall were forced in from time to time, and many of its defenders were crushed. We were better off. Over our heads we had only the heavens, and if no roof guarded us from the bombs, neither did masses of masonry fall upon us. They demolished the wall by the front and sides, and it was a pity to see how that fragile mass fell away little by little, placing us in an exposed position. Nevertheless, after four hours of incessant fire by powerful artillery, they were not able to open a breach.

Thus passed the day of the tenth with no advantage for the besiegers from us, even if they had succeeded in getting near San José and opening a wide breach, which, together with the ruined condition of the building, forced the unhappy necessity of its surrender. Yet, in the meantime, the fortress had not been reduced to powder, and, dead or alive, its defenders had hope. Fresh troops were sent there, because the battalions working there since morning were decimated; and when night fell, after the opening of the breach and the fruitless attempt at an assault, yet Renovales held the blood-soaked ruins, among the heaps of corpses, with only the third part of his artillery.

When night interrupted the firing, there had been great carnage on both sides. We ourselves had lost many by death, and more were wounded. The wounded were carried at the time into the city by the friars and the women; but the dead still gave their last service with their cold bodies, for they were stoically thrown into the open breach, which was being stopped up with sacks of wool and earth.
===Chinese===
与此同时，法军以同样的狂怒攻打皮拉尔棱堡和圣何塞要塞。后者虽然外观上更可畏，抵抗力却较差，或许因为它给敌方的炮火摆出了一个更宽大的靶子。但雷诺瓦莱斯在那里，麾下有韦斯卡和瓦伦西亚义勇军、瓦隆卫队，还有索里亚民兵的若干队伍。那座要塞最大的短处，在于它是为护卫一座庞大建筑而修的，而敌军的炮兵不多时便把那建筑轰成了废墟；厚墙的碎块不时被轰得塌陷进来，守军许多人被压死。我们这边处境倒好些。头顶上只有苍天，虽说没有屋顶替我们挡炸弹，可也没有大块的砖石砸到我们身上来。他们把正面和两侧的墙一路拆掉，眼看着那脆弱的墙身一点点垮下去，把我们晾在全无遮蔽的位置上，真是叫人叹息。然而，强大炮兵整整四个小时不停的轰击之后，他们仍未能轰开一道缺口。

十日这一天就这样过去了，围攻者在我们身上没有占到便宜；纵然他们已在圣何塞近旁得手、轰开了一道宽大的缺口——那缺口，连同那建筑残破不堪的光景，已把这不幸的要塞逼到痛心降服的绝地。不过话说回来，那要塞当时还没有化为齑粉，守军无论死活都还怀着希望。新锐部队被派到那里去，因为从清晨起在那边作战的各营已折损大半；及至夜幕降临，缺口既已轰开，强攻又一败涂地，雷诺瓦莱斯却仍然守着那片浸透鲜血的废墟，在尸骸堆间，手头只剩三分之一的火炮。

夜色打断炮击的时候，双方都已尸积如山。我们自己战死了许多人，负伤的更多。伤员当时已由修士和妇女们抬进城去；死者却还在用冰冷的躯体尽最后一份力，因为他们被不动声色地抛进敞开的缺口——那缺口正用一袋袋羊毛和泥土填堵着。

===Original===
During the night we did not rest for one single moment, and the dawn of the eleventh found us inspired by the same frenzy, our pieces already pointed against the enemy’s entrenchments, and already piercing with musket shots those who were coming to flank us, without hindering for a moment the work of stopping up the breach, which was widening, hour by hour its dreadful spaces. So we endured all the morning until the moment when they began the assault upon San José, now converted into a heap of ruins, and with most of its garrison dead. Centring the forces upon these two points, they fell upon the convent, and directed an audacious movement upon us; and it was with the object of making our breach practicable that they advanced by the Torrero road with two cannons protected by a column of infantry.

At that moment we thought ourselves lost. The feeble walls trembled, and the bricks were shattered into thousands of pieces. We ran up to the breach, which was widening every instant, where they poured upon us a horrible fire. Seeing that the redoubt was being shattered to pieces, they took courage to come to the very borders of the fosse itself. It was madness to try to fill that terrible space, and to show an uncovered place was to offer victims without number to the fury of the enemy. We protected ourselves as well as we could with sacks of wool and shovels of earth, and many stood as if petrified on the spot. The firing of the cannon ceased because it seemed necessary; there was a moment of indefinable panic; the guns fell from our hands; we saw ourselves routed, destroyed, annihilated by that rain of fire that seemed to fill the air. We forgot honor, the fatherland, the glory of death, the Virgin del Pilar, whose name adorned the bridge and the “unconquerable” defences. The most dreadful confusion reigned in our ranks. Descending suddenly from the high moral level of our souls, all those who had not fallen desired life of one accord, and, leaping over the wounded and trampling the dead under foot, we fled towards the bridge, abandoning that horrible sepulchre before it should shut us in, entombing us all.

On the bridge we were swallowed up by insupportable terror and disorder. There is nothing more frenzied than a coward. His abject meannesses are as great as the sublimities of his valor.
===Chinese===
整夜里我们没有歇过一分钟，十一日的曙光来临时，我们仍被同样的狂热鼓舞着：炮口早已对准敌人的堑壕，早已用枪弹射向那些前来侧击我们的敌人，而填堵缺口的工作一刻也没有停——那缺口可怕的空档正一个钟头一个钟头地扩大。我们就这样撑过整整一个上午，直到他们开始强攻圣何塞的时候，那地方已化作一片废墟堆，守军大半战死。敌人把兵力集中在这两点上，一面扑向那座修道院，一面对我们发动一路大胆的行动；他们沿托雷罗大道推进，带着两门由一纵队步兵护卫的大炮，为的就是把我们这边的缺口轰成可资突入的突破口。

那一刻我们以为自己完了。单薄的墙身瑟瑟发抖，砖头崩碎成千万块。我们奔上正在每一瞬间扩大的缺口，敌人朝那里朝我们倾泻可怕的火力。眼见棱堡被打得粉碎，他们壮起胆子，竟一直逼到壕沟边沿。想要填住那可怕的空间无异于疯狂，露出一处没有遮蔽的地方，就是向敌人的狂怒献上数不清的牺牲。我们拿羊毛袋和满铲的土尽可能护住自己，许多人像被吓呆了似的钉在原地。大炮的轰击停了，似乎是形势所迫；接着是一阵说不清道不明的恐慌；枪从我们手里落了下去；我们眼看自己被那仿佛塞满天空的火雨击溃、摧毁、歼灭。我们忘记了荣誉，忘记了祖国，忘记了死亡的光荣，忘记了皮拉尔圣母——她的圣名装点着大桥和“不可征服”的城防。我们队列中笼罩着最可怕的混乱。突然间从灵魂的道德高处跌落下来，凡是没有倒下的人都不约而同地只求活命，我们跳过伤员，踩着死人，朝大桥逃去，趁那可怕的坟墓还没有把我们统统关进去、埋掉之前，弃之而走。

在桥上，我们被难以忍受的恐怖和乱作一团的慌乱吞没了。世上再没有比懦夫更疯狂的了。他卑劣时的下贱，正同他奋勇时的崇高一样登峰造极。

===Original===
Our leaders kept crying out to us, “Back, you rabble! The redoubt del Pilar has not surrendered!” striking our swords with their sabres. We turned back on the bridge, unable to go further, as reinforcements came, and we stumbled over one another, the fury of our fear mingling with the impetus of their bravery.

“Back, cowards!” cried our officers, striking us in the faces, “and die in the breach!”

The redoubt was vacated. None but the dead and the wounded were there. Suddenly we saw advance amid the dense smoke and the blackness of powder, leaping over the lifeless bodies and the heaps of earth and the ruins, and the guns we had thrown down, and the shattered works, a figure, dauntless, pale, splendid, of tragic calmness. It was a woman who had made her way forward and, penetrating the abandoned place, was marching like a queen towards the horrible breach. Pirli, who was lying on the ground, wounded in the leg, exclaimed in affright,
===Chinese===
长官们不停地向我们大喊：“回去，你们这群废物！皮拉尔棱堡还没有投降！”一面用军刀敲打我们手里的刀。我们在桥上掉转头，因为增援来到，再往前挤不过去了；我们彼此绊跌，恐惧的狂乱和来人奋勇的冲劲搅作一团。

“回去，懦夫们！”军官们打着我们的脸喊道，“到缺口里去死！”

棱堡空了。那里只剩下死者和伤员。忽然，我们看见浓密的硝烟和火药的黑雾里有一个身影闯上来，越过一具具无生命的躯体、一堆堆泥土、一片片废墟，越过我们扔下的枪炮和炸碎的工事——那身影无畏、苍白、辉煌，带着悲剧性的镇定。是一个女人挤上前来，闯进这被弃守的地方，像一位王后似的朝那可怕的缺口走去。腿部负伤、倒卧在地的皮尔利惊惶地喊道：

===Original===
“Manuela Sancho, where are you going?”

All this passed in much less time than I take to tell it. After Manuela Sancho, first one, then another, then many hurried, then all, urged on by the leaders whose sabre-cuts had prodded us to the point of duty. This portentous transformation came about by the impulse of every man’s heart obeying sentiments which all feel without anyone’s knowing whence the mysterious force emanates. I do not know why we were cowards, nor why we were brave a few moments later. What I do know is that, moved by an extraordinary power, immense and superhuman, we hurled ourselves into the breach behind the heroic woman, at the point where the French were attempting the assault with ladders. Without in the least knowing how to explain it, we felt our strength increased a hundredfold, and crushed them back, hurling into the ditch those men of cotton who a little while ago had seemed to us men of steel. With shots and sabre-cuts, with shells, with shovels full of earth, by blows, and bayonet-thrusts, we fought. Many of our number died to defend others with their dead bodies. We defended the breach, indeed, and the French were obliged to retire, leaving many dead and wounded at the bottom of the wall. The cannons again began firing, and the unconquerable redoubt did not fall on the eleventh into the hands of the French.

When the tempest of fire was calmed, we did not know ourselves. We were transfigured, and something new and unknown palpitated in the depths of our souls, giving us an unheard-of fierceness. The following day Palafox said, with much eloquence: “Nor balls, nor bombs, nor shells shall make our faces change color, nor can all France accomplish that!”
===Chinese===
“玛努埃拉·桑乔，你要去哪儿？”

这一切经过的时间，比我讲述所用的短得多。继玛努埃拉·桑乔之后，先是一个人冲上去，接着又一个，然后是许多人，最后是全体，都在长官们的鞭策之下——正是那些刀口把我们抽回了职分的关口。这一惊天动地的转变，出自人心的冲动，服从着一种人人都感受得到、却无人知道从何处发出的神秘力量。我不知道我们方才为什么是懦夫，也不知道为什么片刻之后又成了勇士。我只知道，在一种非凡、巨大而超人的力量推动下，我们随着那位女英雄扑进缺口，正扑在法军架着云梯强攻的地方。我们丝毫说不明白，只觉得力气陡增百倍，把他们硬压了回去，把方才在我们眼里还是钢铁之躯、此刻却成了棉花做的人，一股脑儿搡进壕沟里。用枪弹和刀劈，用手榴弹，用满铲的土，用拳打脚踢，用刺刀猛捅，我们拼杀了一场。我们中间许多人死了，用死后的躯体护卫着旁人。我们当真守住了缺口，法军被迫退走，在墙脚下丢下大批死伤。大炮重新开火，十一日这一天，那不可征服的棱堡终究没有落入法国人手中。

炮火的风暴平息之后，我们认不出自己了。我们脱胎换骨了，一种崭新而未知的东西在灵魂深处悸动，给了我们闻所未闻的悍勇。第二天，帕拉福克斯以极富雄辩的辞令说道：“无论炮弹、炸弹还是榴弹，都不能叫我们变一变脸色，纵然倾全法兰西之力，也休想办到！”

## XI / 第十一章

===Original===
The fortress of San José had surrendered, or rather the French had entered it when their artillery had reduced it to powder, and all of its defenders had fallen, one by one, to lie among its fragments. The Imperial soldiers, on entering, found heaps of bodies and stones matted together with blood. They could not establish themselves there because they were flanked by the batteries of Los Mártires and the Botanical Garden, so they continued operations by mining, in order to possess themselves of those two points. The fortifications which we held were so nearly destroyed that a general agreement was urgent, and the terrible orders, calling upon all the inhabitants of Saragossa to work in renewing them. The proclamation said that every citizen should carry a gun in one hand and spade in the other.

The twelfth and thirteenth were without rest, the fire diminishing a little because the besiegers, warned by sad experience, did not wish to risk any more hand-to-hand conflicts. Understanding that theirs was a work of patience and skill, rather than of boldness and bravery, they opened slowly, and with security, roads and mines which should lead to the possession of the redoubt without loss of men. It was almost necessary to build our walls anew, or rather to substitute sacks of earth for them, an operation in which many friars, canons, civil officials, children, and women were occupied. The artillery was almost useless, the fosse about filled up, and it was necessary to continue the defence at short range. And so we wore through the thirteenth, protecting the works as we rebuilt them, suffering much, and seeing ourselves constantly decreasing in numbers, although new men came to take the places of the many that we lost. On the fourteenth the enemy’s artillery tried to demolish our new walls, opening breaches for us on the front and at the sides. They did not dare to try a new assault, contenting themselves with opening a mine in such a direction that we could not in any way cover it with our fire, nor with that of any battery near by.

Our valorous tantalizing earthworks would soon be covered by the fires of the French batteries, which were hurling to the four winds the earth of which they were formed. In this situation, surrender was inevitable sooner or later. Indeed we were at the mercy of the French arms as a ship at the mercy of the waves of the ocean. Flanked by roads and zigzags, through which a strong and clever enemy might walk without danger, protected by all the resources of science, our bulwarks of defence were like one man surrounded by an army. We had no serviceable cannons, nor could we bring other new ones, because the walls would not have borne them. Our only resource was to keep watch of the redoubt in order to fly from it at the moment when the French should enter and destroy the bridge, in order to prevent them from following us. This was done; and on the night of the fourteenth they worked without rest on the mine, and we placed small mines at the bridge, hoping that the following day the enemy would try to mount by that wall. But this did not happen. Not daring to make another assault without all the precautions and security possible, they continued their work of digging very nearly up to our fosse. In this labor our indefatigable fusileers did them little damage. We were desperate, but without power to do anything. Our desperation was of no avail; it was a useless force, like the rage of a lunatic in his cage.

===Chinese===
圣何塞要塞算是投降了——或者不如说，是等法军的炮火把它轰成了齑粉，守军一个接一个全都倒下、长眠在断石碎砖之间之后，法国人才进得去。帝国士兵开进去，只见成堆的尸骸与砖石血肉黏连，凝作一处。他们在那里站不住脚，因为洛斯·马尔蒂雷斯炮台和植物园炮台从两翼夹制着他们，于是便改用坑道作业继续进攻，要把这两处据点也夺到手里。我们据守的工事已被摧毁得所剩无几，全城协力已刻不容缓，于是颁下了那道声色俱厉的命令，号召萨拉戈萨全体居民出工翻修。文告说：每个市民应当一手持枪，一手执铲。

十二日和十三日两天没有一刻安歇；炮火稍见稀疏，因为围城的敌军被惨痛的经验教训过，不愿再拿人命去冒险近身肉搏。他们明白这差使靠的是耐心与技艺，而非大胆与剽勇，便不慌不忙、稳稳当当地开挖通路与坑道，指望兵不血刃便拿下棱堡。我们的城墙几乎等于要重新造过——说得确切些，是拿一袋袋土来顶替城墙；许多修士、座堂神父、文职官员、孩童和妇女都投入了这项劳作。炮兵几乎派不上用场，护城壕也填得差不多了，只能以近距离继续防守。十三日就这样熬了过去：我们一边重修工事一边加以掩护，吃足了苦头，眼见人数不断减少，尽管不断有生力军前来，补上我们折损的那些人的空缺。十四日，敌人的炮兵力图摧毁我们的新墙，在正面和两侧替我们“开”出了一个个缺口。他们不敢再尝试新的强攻，只满足于朝这样一个方向开掘坑道：我们无论用自己的火力、还是用左近任何一座炮台的火力，都无法把它压制。

我们那些英勇而又爱撩逗敌人的土垒，很快就要被法军各炮台的火力罩住了——人家正把构成这些工事的泥土轰得四散纷飞。处在这种情势之下，把棱堡拱手让出只是迟早的事。我们确实已任凭法兰西的武力摆布，就像一艘船任凭大洋的波涛摆布。大路和之字形堑壕从两翼包抄过来，强壮而精明的敌人尽可以毫发无伤地从中穿行，又有科学的一切手段护体；我们的防御壁垒，就像一个被整支军队围住的孤身汉子。我们一门堪用的大炮也没有，新的也运不上来，因为这些土墙吃不住。我们唯一的办法就是盯住棱堡，只等法军一冲进来便立刻脱身撤离，并把大桥炸毁，好教他们追不上我们。事情就这么办了：十四日夜间，他们不歇手地赶挖坑道，我们则在桥头埋下了小地雷，指望敌人第二天会企图从那道墙攀上来。可这一幕没有上演。他们不敢在未把一切防备和安全保障做到万全之前再来一次强攻，只顾把掘进的工程继续推进，几乎一直挖到了我们壕沟边上。在这场劳作里，我们那些不知疲倦的燧发枪手没能给他们多大的杀伤。我们急得发狂，却无能为力。我们的绝望于事无补；那是一股白费的力气，就像疯子在笼子里的狂怒。

===Original===
We drew out the nails from the tablet which proclaimed ours to be the unconquerable redoubt, in order to take away with us that witness of our justifiable arrogance. At nightfall the fortification was abandoned, only forty remaining to keep it until the end, and shoot all they could, as our captain said that no chance might be lost to lose the enemy a couple of men. From the Torre del Pino we saw the retreat of the forty at about eight o’clock in the evening, after they had met the invaders with bayonet-thrusts; they retreated fighting bravely.

The interior mine of the redoubt had had little effect, but the small mines of the bridge acquitted themselves so well that the passage was destroyed and the redoubt isolated from the other bank of the Huerva. Gaining this position and San José, the French would have enough protection to open their third parallel and to demolish at their leisure the whole circuit of the city. We were saddened and just a little discouraged; but of what importance is a little depression when on the day following one has a diversion and a feast? After being madly discouraged, a little jollity does not come amiss, especially when time is wanting to bury the dead; nor was there room in the houses for the many who were wounded. It is true that there were hands for all that had to be done, thanks be to God.

The reason for the general rejoicing was that glorious rumors were in circulation of Spanish armies that were coming to succor us, on the heels of the French, in many parts of the Peninsula. The people crowded into the Plaza de la Seo, and in front of the Magdalene arch, waiting until the *Gazette* should appear; and at last it came out, cheering everybody’s spirits, and making all hearts palpitate with hope. I do not know if such rumors had really reached Saragossa, or if they originated in the wits of the chief editor, Don Ignacio Assor. It is certain that they told us in print that Reading was coming to succor us with an army of sixty thousand men, that the Marquis of Lazán, after routing the mob in the north of Catalonia, had entered France, spreading terror in every direction, and that also the Duke del Infantado was coming to our aid, who with Blake and la Romana had routed Napoleon, slaying twenty thousand men, including Berthier, Ney, and Savary, and that at Cádiz had arrived several millions in hard cash sent by the English for the expense of war.
