I do not suppose that one is left. There goes Villacampa alone. Then are not those Mendieta, and Paul, Benedicto, and Oliva? Let us go. I see that indeed none are left in that place.”

In this way the convent of Las Mónicas passed into the hands of the French.

===Chinese===
这场战斗进行的时候，我们正在帕洛马尔街一带的房屋里，朝奉命强攻修道院的法军分队射击。战斗尚未结束，我们便得知拉斯莫尼卡斯已再无守住的指望。同我们在一起的唐·何塞·德·蒙特里亚本人也亲口承认了这一点。

“韦斯卡的义勇军打得不算差，”他说，“谁都知道他们是好样的。眼下咱们得赶紧着守右边这些房子。我看那边怕是一个人也不剩了。瞧，比利亚坎普一个人过去了。那不就是门迭塔（Mendieta）、保罗（Paul）、贝内迪克托（Benedicto）和奥利瓦（Oliva）吗？走吧。我看那地方当真一个人也不剩了。”

就这样，拉斯莫尼卡斯修道院落入了法军之手。

## XXI / 第二十一章

===Original===
On reaching this point in my story, I beg the reader to pardon me if I do not give the dates exactly of that which I relate. In this period of horror, lasting from January 27 to the middle of the next month, the successive events are so confused, so mixed up, so run together in my mind, that I cannot distinguish days and nights, and, in some instances, I do not know whether certain skirmishes of those I recall took place in daylight. It seems to me that all happened during one long day, or in one endless night, and that time was not then marked by its ordinary divisions. Many sensations and impressions are linked together in my memory, forming one vast picture where there are no more dividing lines than those that the events themselves offer﻿—the greater fright of one moment, the unexplained panic or fury of another.

For this reason I cannot tell exactly on what day that took place which I am going to relate now; but if I am not mistaken it was on a day after the fight at Las Mónicas, and somewhere, I should say, between the thirtieth of January and the second of February. We were occupying a house in the Calle de Pabostre. The French were in the one next to it, and were trying to advance through the inside of the block to reach the Puerta Quemada. Nothing can compare with the incessant activity going on there. No kind of warfare, no bloodiest battle on the open field, no sieges of a plaza, nor struggles in a street barricade can compare with the succession of conflicts between the army of an alcove and the army of a drawing-room, between the troops that occupy one floor and those which guard the one above it.

Hearing the muffled blows of the picks at various points, not knowing from what direction the attack might come, caused us some alarm. We went up into attics; we descended into cellars, and glued our ears to partition walls; we tried to learn the intentions of the enemy according to the direction of the blows. At last we noticed that the partition wall was being violently shaken near the very place where we were standing, and we waited fire in the doorway, after heaping up the furniture as a barricade. The French opened a hole, and presently began leaping over beams and broken fragments, showing an intention of driving us from the place. There were twenty of us, fewer of them, and they evidently did not expect to be received in such fashion, and retreated, returning soon with such reinforcement that we were in great danger, and obliged to retire, leaving five comrades behind the furniture, two of them dead. In the narrow passage we ran against a stairway up which we hurried without knowing where we were going, and presently found ourselves in a garret﻿—an admirable position for defence. The stairway was narrow, however, and the Frenchmen who tried to come up it died inevitably. So we remained for some time, prolonging the resistance, and encouraging one another with huzzas and shouts, when the partition at our backs began to resound with loud blows, and we saw immediately that the French, by opening an entrance through there, would catch us between two fires without means of escape. We were now thirteen, as two had fallen in the garret, severely wounded. Tío Garcés, who was in command, shouted furiously: “By heaven, the dogs shall not catch us! There’s a skylight in the roof. Let us go up through it to the tiles of the roof. Go on firing at whoever comes up to try and cut through it! The rest of you enlarge the hole. Away with fear, and viva the Virgin del Pilar!”
===Chinese===
我的故事讲到此处，倘若我叙述的事情在日期上不够确切，还请读者见谅。在这段从 1 月 27 日延续到次月中旬的恐怖日子里，接连发生的事件在我脑海里如此混乱、如此纠缠、如此连成一片，以至我分不清白天与黑夜，有些场合，我竟说不准记忆里的某些小仗究竟是不是在大白天打的。在我看来，一切都发生在同一个漫长的白昼、或是一场无尽的长夜里，那时的时间已不按寻常的分段来计量了。许多感觉与印象在我的记忆里连缀成一幅巨大的图景，其中除了事件自身划出的界线——这一刻更大的惊惶、那一刻莫名的恐慌或狂怒——再没有别的分界。

为此，我说不准现在要讲的这件事究竟落在哪一天；但如果没有记错，那是在拉斯莫尼卡斯之战以后的某一天，依我看，大约在 1 月 30 日到 2 月 2 日之间。我们据守着帕博斯特雷街的一座房子，法军就在紧邻的那座房子里，正设法从街区内部打通过去，直抵克马达门。那里头一刻不停的大动干戈，世间再没有可比的。随便哪种战争、开阔野战中最血腥的会战、围攻一座城池、街垒上的厮杀，都比不上卧房大军与客厅大军之间、占据这一层楼的部队与据守上一层楼的部队之间那连绵不断的交锋。

四处听得到十字镐闷哑的敲凿声，又不知进攻会从哪个方向来，我们心里不免惊惶。我们上阁楼、下地窖，把耳朵紧贴在隔墙上，想凭敲凿的方向揣度敌人的意图。终于，我们察觉到就在立足处的近旁，隔墙正被猛烈震摇，便把家具堆起来充作街垒，守在门口严阵以待。法军凿开一个洞，转眼间就翻过梁木和断砖碎瓦跳将过来，摆出一副要把我们逐出此地的架势。我们有二十个人，他们人少，显然没料到会受这样的接待，便退了下去；但不多时又带着大队增援杀回来，弄得我们险象环生，只得退走，把五名伙伴丢在家具后面，其中两个已经阵亡。在狭窄的过道里，我们撞见一段楼梯，便急急往上爬，也不知去处，转眼发现自己置身一间阁楼——一处防守的绝妙地势。只是楼梯很窄，法军想冲上来的都必死无疑。我们就这样支撑了一阵，把抵抗拖延下去，彼此用欢呼与呐喊打气；这时背后的隔墙忽然响起重重的敲击声，我们立刻看出，法军只要从那边凿开一个入口，就会教我们腹背受敌、脱身无路。此刻我们只剩十三人，因为有两个已在阁楼里倒下，伤得很重。带队的加尔塞斯大叔怒吼道：“老天在上，绝不能让这群狗东西抓住咱们！屋顶上有天窗。咱们打那儿上去，上到屋顶的瓦上。谁想上来凿洞，就朝谁开枪！其余的人把洞口扩大。别怕，皮拉尔圣母万岁！”

===Original===
It was done as he commanded. This was to be a well-ordered retreat, according to the rules of war; and while part of our army was preventing the onward march of the enemy, the rest were occupied in facilitating the retreat. This able plan was put into execution with feverish activity, and very soon the hole of escape was large enough for three men to pass through at once, without the French gaining a single step during the time that we were employed in this way. We quickly got out on the roof. We were now nine. Three had been left in the garret, and another was wounded in trying to get out, falling still alive, into the hands of the enemy. On finding ourselves outside, we leaped for joy. We cast a glance over the roofs of the quarter, and saw at a distance the batteries of the French. We advanced on all fours for a good distance, exploring the lay of the land, leaving two sentinels in the gap to pop off a gun at anyone who should seek to slip up by them. We had not gone twenty paces when we heard a great noise of voices and laughter which seemed to us to be French. And so it was; from a broad balcony those rascals were looking at us and laughing. They were not slow in firing upon us, but protected behind the chimneys, the angles and corners which the roof afforded, we answered them shot for shot, and replied to their oaths and exclamations by a thousand other invectives with which the lively imagination of Tío Garcés inspired us. At last we retreated, jumping to the roof of the next house. We believed it to be in the hands of our own men, and we entered by the window of a little upper room, supposing that the descent from there to the street would be easy, and that there we should be reinforced for the conclusion of the adventure that had carried us through passages, up stairways, through garrets, and over roofs. But we had scarcely set foot there, when we heard in the apartment below us the sound of many blows on the wall.
===Chinese===
众人依令行事。这该是一场合乎兵法、秩序井然的撤退；当我们的一部分人马阻挡敌人前进时，其余的人就忙着给撤退开路。这条妙计以火热的干劲付诸实行，很快，逃生口已开得足以容三人同时通过，而这段时间里法军没能前进一步。我们迅速钻上屋顶。这时我们只剩九个人。三个留在了阁楼里，还有一个在往外钻时负了伤，跌了下去，还有一口气，落在敌人手中。到了外面，我们欢喜得直跳。我们朝这一片街区的屋顶扫了一眼，远远望见法军的炮台。我们手脚并用地爬出好一程，一路察看地势，在缺口处留下两名哨兵，谁想从那儿溜上来，就给他一枪。走出还不到二十步，我们听见一片喧闹的人声和笑声，听着像法国人。果然不错；那群无赖正从一个宽敞的阳台上瞧着我们大笑。他们随即朝我们开火，但我们躲在烟囱和屋顶的棱角旮旯后面，一枪还一枪，他们咒骂叫嚷，我们就用千百句回敬过去——全靠加尔塞斯大叔那股活络的想象力给我们递词。最后我们撤退，跳上隔壁一座房子的屋顶。我们以为那座房子在自己人手里，便从一间小顶楼的窗子钻进去，心想从那里下到街上想必容易，到了那儿还能得到增援，好把这一趟穿甬道、爬楼梯、闯阁楼、越屋顶的奇遇收个尾。可我们脚跟还没站稳，就听见楼下的房间里传来一阵密集的砸墙声。

===Original===
“They are beating in there,” said Tío Garcés, and in a second the French whom we had left in the house next us had passed to this one, where they met comrades.

“*Cuerno! Recuerno!* Let us get out of this! The whole creation’s down below there.”

We passed on into another garret, and found our way to a ladder leading down to a large interior room, from whose doorway came the lively sound of voices, chiefly those of women. The noise of the fight seemed much further off, and we decided it must be at some distance. So we dropped down the ladder and found ourselves in a large room filled with old men, women, and children who had all sought refuge here. Many, lying upon rude mattresses, showed in their faces traces of the terrible epidemic, and one lifeless body lay on the floor, breath evidently having left it but a few moments before. Some were wounded, suffering cruelly and groaning unrestrainedly; two or three old women were weeping and praying. Occasionally voices were heard begging, “Water, water!” From where we entered, I saw Candiola at the end of the room, carefully depositing in a corner a quantity of clothes and kitchen utensils and crockery. With an angry gesture he drove away the curious children who wished to look over and handle the poor stuff. Anxious, eager only to heap together and guard his treasures without losing a fragment, he was saying﻿—
===Chinese===
“他们正在里面砸呢，”加尔塞斯大叔说。转瞬间，被我们甩在隔壁房子里的那伙法军已经窜到这一座里，在这里遇上了他们的同伙。

“*Cuerno! Recuerno!*（见鬼！真是见鬼！）快离开这儿！底下全都是他们的人。”

我们又走进另一间阁楼，寻见一架梯子，通到底下一间宽大的里屋，门口传来嘈杂的人声，多半是女人的声音。厮杀声似乎远得多了，我们断定战斗必在远处。于是我们顺梯而下，发现自己置身一间大屋，满屋都是到这儿避难的老汉、妇女和孩子。许多人躺在简陋的草垫上，脸上带着那场可怕时疫的痕迹；地上躺着一具没了气息的尸体，显然断气才不过片刻。有些人负了伤，痛苦万分，毫无顾忌地呻吟着；两三个老妇人在哭泣、祈祷。不时听得有人哀告：“水，水！”从我进来的地方望去，我看见坎迪奥拉在屋子那头，正小心翼翼地把一批衣裳、厨房用具和陶器安置到一个角落里。他怒气冲冲地做着手势，赶开那些好奇的、想凑上来翻看摆弄这些破烂的孩子。他焦灼不安，一心只想把他的财宝堆拢来、看守住，一件也不能少，嘴里正说着——

===Original===
“I have already lost two cups. And I have no doubt whatever as to what has become of them. Some one of these people has taken them. There is no security anywhere; there are no authorities to guarantee to a citizen the possession of his property. Out of here, you unmannerly boys! Oh, we are hard pushed! Cursed be the bombs and the one who invented them! Soldiers, you have come in good time. Can you not have two sentinels placed here for me to guard these treasures which I have been able to save only with great trouble?”

My comrades laughed at such pretension, as may readily be believed. We were just about to go, when I saw Mariquilla. The poor girl was sadly changed from lack of sleep, much weeping, and the constant alarms. But the trouble of her brow, and that which looked forth from her eyes, only added to the sweetness of expression of her beautiful face. She saw me, and immediately came eagerly up to me, showing that she wished to speak with me.

“And Augustine?” I asked her.

“He is down there,” she replied in tremulous tones. “They are fighting below. We who took refuge in this house have been apportioned to different rooms. My father came this morning with Doña Guedita. Augustine brought us something to eat, and put us in a room where there was a mattress. Suddenly we heard blows on the partition walls. The French were coming. The troops entered, and made us leave, carrying the sick and wounded to an upper room. They shut us all in, and then the walls were broken through. The French met the Spaniards then, and began real fighting. Yes, Augustine is below.”
===Chinese===
“我已经丢了两只杯子。它们落到哪儿去了，我心里一清二楚：这些人里有谁顺手牵羊拿走了。如今哪儿都没有保障；再没有官府来保障一个公民的财产了。滚开，你们这些没规矩的小崽子！唉，我们这是被逼到什么地步了！可咒的炸弹，可咒的发明炸弹的人！士兵们，你们来得正是时候。就不能给我派两名哨兵守在这儿，看住这些我千辛万苦才抢出来的宝贝吗？”

不难想见，伙伴们对这份妄想哄然大笑。我们正要走，我却看见了玛丽基亚。这可怜的姑娘由于缺少睡眠、哭泣太多、警报不断，憔悴得叫人心疼。但她眉间的愁绪、她眼睛里流露的心事，反而给她那张美丽的脸庞平添了几分温婉。她看见了我，立刻急切地迎上来，示意有话要对我说。

“奥古斯丁呢？”我问她。

“他在下面，”她声音发颤地答道，“下面正在打。我们这些到这房子里避难的人，被分派到各个房间里。今天早晨，我父亲同堂娜·格韦迪塔一块儿来了。奥古斯丁给我们送来一些吃的，把我们安置在一间有草垫的屋里。忽然我们听见隔墙上响起敲凿声。法军来了。队伍进了屋，叫我们离开，一面把病人和伤员搬到楼上一间屋里。他们把我们全关在里面，随后墙壁就被凿穿了。法军同西班牙人撞在一处，真刀真枪地干了起来。是的，奥古斯丁在下面。”

===Original===
She was saying this when Manuela Sancho came, carrying two pitchers of water for the wounded. The poor wretches threw themselves from their beds, disputing even to blows over the water.

“No pushing, no scrambling, señors!” said Manuela, laughing. “There is water enough for all. Our side is winning. It has cost a little labor to drive the French from the alcove, and now they are disputing half of the hall, having gained one half of it. They do not wish to leave us a kitchen or a staircase. The whole place is filled with the dead.”

Mariquilla turned pale with the horror of it.

“I am thirsty,” she said to me.
===Chinese===
她正说着，玛努埃拉·桑乔来了，提着两罐给伤员的水。那些可怜人从铺上扑过来，为着水甚至动手争抢。

“别挤，别抢，先生们！”玛努埃拉笑着说，“水够大家喝的。我们这边正在赢。把法军从卧室里赶出去费了点力气，如今大厅他们已占去一半，剩下那一半还在争夺。他们连一间厨房、一道楼梯也不肯留给我们。整个地方都堆满了死人。”

玛丽基亚听得面无人色。

“我渴，”她对我说。

===Original===
I immediately tried to get some water for her from Manuela; but as the last glass she had was in use, quenching soldiers’ thirst, as she went from mouth to mouth with it, I took, in order to lose no time, one of the cups which Candiola had in his pile.

“Eh, you meddler,” he said, shaking his fist at me, “leave that cup here.”

“I am getting it to give water to the señorita,” I answered indignantly. “Are these things so valuable, Señor Candiola?”

The miser did not reply, but did not oppose my giving his daughter a drink. After her thirst was quenched, a wounded soldier reached out his hands eagerly for the cup, and, lo! it began to go the rounds also, passing from mouth to mouth. When I went to wait upon my comrades, Don Jerónimo followed me with his eyes, and watched with bad grace the forced loan that was so slow in returning to his hands.
===Chinese===
我立刻想从玛努埃拉那里给她弄点水；可她手头最后一杯水正派着用场，正从一张嘴传到一张嘴给士兵们解渴，我为了不耽搁工夫，便从坎迪奥拉那堆东西里拿了一只杯子。

“喂，你这个多管闲事的，”他朝我挥着拳头说，“把那只杯子留在这儿。”

“我拿去是给小姐倒水喝的，”我愤然答道，“这些东西就那么金贵吗，坎迪奥拉先生？”

这吝啬鬼没有回答，也没有拦阻我给他的女儿水喝。她解了渴，一名伤兵又急切地朝那只杯子伸出双手，于是瞧啊！它也开始了巡回，从一张嘴传到另一张嘴。我走开去照看伙伴们时，唐·赫罗尼莫的眼睛一路跟着我，老大不情愿地盯着这笔迟迟不回到他手里的强行借贷。

===Original===
Manuela Sancho was right in saying that our side was winning. The French, dislodged from the main floor of the house, had retired to the one below, where they continued their defence. When I descended, all the interest of the battle was centred in the kitchen, disputed with much bloodshed, but the rest of the house was in our power. Many bodies of French and Spanish covered the gory floor. Some soldiers and patriots, furious at not being able to conquer that dismal kitchen, whence such a fire was pouring, hurled themselves forward into it, defending themselves with their bayonets; and although a goodly number of them perished, their courageous act decided the matter, for behind them others could come, and then all that the room could hold.

The Imperial soldiers, panic-stricken with this violent assault, looked quickly for a way out of the house which had been taken room by room. We pursued them through passages and halls whose confused arrangement would craze the best military topographer. We finished them wherever we could find them, and some of them escaped, dashing in desperation out through the courtyards. In this manner, after reconquering one house, we reconquered the next one, obliging the enemy to restrict themselves to their old positions, which were the first two houses of the Calle de Pabostre.

Afterwards we removed our dead and wounded, and I had the sorrow of finding Augustine Montoria among the latter, although the gun-wound in his right arm was not of a serious nature. My battalion was reduced one-half that day. The unfortunates who had sought refuge in the upper room now wished to make themselves a little more comfortable in the lower rooms; but this was not thought practicable, and they were obliged to leave the place and look for an asylum further from danger.
===Chinese===
玛努埃拉·桑乔说我们这边正在赢，说得不错。法军被从房子的正层逐出后，退到了下面一层，在那里继续顽抗。我下去的时候，战斗的全部焦点都集中在厨房里，正浴血相争，但房子的其余部分都已在我们手中。法军和西班牙人的许多尸体盖满了血污的地板。几名士兵和爱国志士因拿不下那间阴森的厨房而怒火中烧——密集的火力正从那里倾泻出来——便挺身朝里猛扑过去，靠刺刀自卫；虽然他们当中送命的不在少数，但这一勇敢举动决定了战局，因为他们身后可以跟上别的人，再往后，凡是那间屋子装得下的都涌了进去。

帝国士兵被这番猛攻吓破了胆，急急忙忙寻路逃出这座已被一间一间夺回的房子。我们穿过那些格局乱得能把最出色的军用测绘师逼疯的过道和厅堂追击他们。凡是找得到的，我们就把他们结果掉；有几个亡命奔逃，横下心穿过院子跑掉了。就这样，夺回一座房子之后又夺回一座，逼得敌人收缩回他们的老阵地，也就是帕博斯特雷街的头两座房子。

随后我们抬走了死者和伤员，我伤心地发现奥古斯丁·蒙特里亚也在伤员之列，尽管他右臂上的枪伤算不得严重。那一天，我们营折损了一半。先前挤在楼上房间避难的不幸的人们，这时想到楼下的房间里住得舒服些；但大家觉得这行不通，他们只得离开此地，另寻一处离危险更远的栖身之所。

===Original===
Every day, every hour, every instant, the increasing difficulties of our military situation were aggravated by the sight of the great number of unburied victims of battle and of the epidemic. Happy a thousand times those who were buried in the ruins of the undermined houses, as happened to the valiant defenders of the Calle de Pomar, close to the Santa Engracia! The most horrible thing was a great number of the wounded piled up together, so that nobody could get at them to help them. There was no medical aid for a hundredth part of them. The charity of women, the zeal of patriotic citizens, the multiplied activity of the hospitals, really availed nothing.

There came a time when a sort of impassibility, a dreadful apathy, began to take possession of the besieged. We became used to the sight of a heap of dead bodies, as if they were so many sacks of wool. We were accustomed to see, without pity, great numbers of the wounded creeping and tottering to the houses, each one caring for himself as best he could. In the keenness of our sufferings, it seemed as if the usual necessities of the flesh had gone, and that we lived only in the spirit. Familiarity with danger had transformed our natures, infusing them apparently with a new element﻿—absolute contempt of the material, and indifference to life. Everyone expected to die at any moment, without the idea disturbing him in the least. I remember hearing described the attack on the Trinitarios convent, made in the hope of snatching it from the French, and the fabulous exploits, the inconceivable rashness of that undertaking seemed to me natural and ordinary.

I do not know whether I have said that next to the Convent de las Mónicas was that of San Augustine, an edifice of good size, with a large church, spacious cloisters, and vast transepts. It was inevitable that the French, now masters of Las Mónicas, should show great perseverance in the effort to gain possession of this monastery, in order to establish themselves firmly and definitely in that quarter.
===Chinese===
每一天、每一小时、每一刻，大批战死者和时疫罹难者无人下葬的惨状，都使我们军情日益加剧的困厄雪上加霜。那些被坑道炸毁的房屋废墟掩埋的人，真是千倍地有福——波马尔街（Calle de Pomar）那些英勇的守卫者，在圣恩格拉西亚近旁遭的正是这样的下场！最骇人的，是大批伤员堆叠在一处，谁也挤不到他们跟前去施救。医援连他们的百分之一都顾不上。妇女们的慈善、爱国市民的热忱、各家医院加倍再加倍的努力，实在都无济于事。

到了后来，一种麻木不仁、一种可怕的冷漠，开始攫住被围困的人们。我们看惯了一堆堆的死尸，只当是一袋袋羊毛。我们见惯了一群群伤员爬着、踉跄着挪回房子里去，各自想方设法照料自己，心里竟不再有怜悯。在极度的苦难中，肉身的寻常需求仿佛已经消散，我们只靠精神活着。与危险的朝夕厮磨改变了我们的天性，仿佛给它注入了一种新元素——对物质的全然蔑视，对生命的无动于衷。人人都随时等着死，这个念头却丝毫不搅扰他。我记得曾听人描述进攻三一会修道院、指望从法军手中夺回它的那一仗，那一番神话般的功绩、那股难以置信的莽勇，在我听来竟觉得自然而寻常。

我不知道可曾说过，紧挨着拉斯莫尼卡斯修道院的是圣奥古斯丁修道院，一座规模可观的建筑，有一座大教堂、宽敞的回廊和阔大的耳堂。法军既已占据拉斯莫尼卡斯，势必拿出百折不回的韧劲夺取这座修道院，以便在那个街区站定脚跟、一劳永逸。

===Original===
“Since we have not the luck to be in Las Mónicas,” said Pirli to me, “we will, today, give ourselves the pleasure of defending until death the four walls of Saint Augustine. As the Estremadurans are not sufficient to defend it, we are ordered in, too. And how about rank, friend Araceli? Is it true that we two young gentlemen have been promoted to be sergeants?”

“I don’t know anything about it, friend Pirli,” I answered; and it was true that I was ignorant of my elevation to the hierarchical altitude of a sergeant.

“Yes, indeed, the general says so; Señor de Araceli is first sergeant, and Señor de Pirli is second sergeant. We have worked hard enough for it. It’s a good thing we have enough of our bodies left to hang the epaulets on. I heard that Augustine Montoria has been made a lieutenant for his gallantry inside the houses. Yesterday, at nightfall, the battalion of Las Peñas de San Pedro was reduced to four sergeants, a lieutenant, a captain, and two hundred men.”
===Chinese===
“既然咱们没福气待在拉斯莫尼卡斯，”皮尔利对我说，“那今天咱们就领个乐子，来誓死守卫圣奥古斯丁的这四面墙。埃斯特雷马杜拉人（Estremadurans）守它人手不够，我们也都奉命调了进去。再说军衔吧，阿拉塞利老兄，咱们这两位年轻绅士升了中士，是真的吗？”

“这事我一无所知，皮尔利老兄，”我答道；我确实不知自己已高升到中士这等级阶梯的高位上去了。

“一点不假，是将军说的；阿拉塞利先生荣任一等中士，皮尔利先生荣任二等中士。这衔头咱们挣得够苦了。好在咱们身上剩下的肉还够挂肩章。我听说奥古斯丁·蒙特里亚因为在房子里作战英勇，已经升了中尉。昨天日落时分，圣佩德罗岩营打得只剩四名中士、一名中尉、一名上尉和两百号人了。”

===Original===
“Let us see, friend Pirli, if we cannot earn two more promotions apiece today.”

“All that we have to do is to keep our skins whole,” he answered. “The few soldiers of the Huesca battalion who survive think that they are all going to be made generals. There is the call! Have you anything to eat?”

“Not much.”

“Manuela Sancho gave me four sardines. I will divide them with you. How would you like a dozen of these roasted peas? Do you remember how wine tastes? I ask, because it is so many days since they have given us a drop. They will give us a spoonful when the battle of San Augustine is over. Here you are! It would be too bad if they should finish us off before we know what color the stuff is which they are going to pass around tonight. If they would follow my advice, they would give it to us before the fight, so that those who drop off would get a taste. But the committee of supplies has evidently said, ‘There is very little wine; if we give it out now there will scarcely be three drops to a man. We will wait until evening, and as it will be a miracle if a fourth part of those who defend San Augustine are alive then, there will be at least one swallow apiece for the rest.’ ”
===Chinese===
“咱们瞧瞧吧，皮尔利老兄，今天能不能每人再挣两级。”

“咱们要做的，就是保住自己这张皮别破了，”他答道，“韦斯卡营活下来的那几个兵，个个都想着要当将军了。号响了！你有什么吃的没有？”

“没什么。”

“玛努埃拉·桑乔给了我四条沙丁鱼，我跟你对半分。来一打这烤豌豆怎么样？你还记得酒是什么味道吗？我这么问，是因为好多天没发给我们一滴了。等圣奥古斯丁之战打完，他们会赏我们一勺。给，拿好！要是他们还没让我们弄清楚今晚要发的那个玩意儿是什么颜色，就先把咱们结果了，那才叫冤呢。他们若肯听我的主意，就该在开打之前发给我们，这样掉脑袋的也好尝上一口。可补给议会显然是这么说的：‘酒很少；现在就发的话，每人怕是摊不到三滴。我们等到晚上再发，反正到时候，守圣奥古斯丁的人能活下四分之一就算奇迹，余下的人每人至少还能喝上一口。’”

===Original===
He followed this criticism with a general discourse upon the scarcity of provisions. We did not have time to indulge ourselves much on that topic, for we had scarcely joined the Estremadura men at the monastery, when a loud report warned us to be on our guard; then a friar appeared, shouting﻿—

“My sons, they have blown up the middle walls on the side towards Las Mónicas, and they are already in the building! Run to the church. They must have seized the sacristy; but that makes no difference. If you go in time, you will be masters of the nave, of the chapels and the choir. Viva the Holy Virgin del Pilar, and the battalion of the Estremadura!”

We marched serenely into the church.
===Chinese===
发完这通批评，他又就给养匮乏的问题发了一通泛论。我们来不及在这个题目上多所盘桓，因为刚刚在修道院里同埃斯特雷马杜拉人会合，一声巨响便警告我们警惕；随即一个修士出现了，高喊着——

“我的孩子们，他们把朝拉斯莫尼卡斯那一面的中间几道墙炸倒了，人已经进楼了！快到教堂去。圣器室想必已被他们占了去；但那不要紧。你们只要赶得及时，正殿、各小堂和唱诗席便都是你们的。皮拉尔至圣圣母万岁，埃斯特雷马杜拉营万岁！”

我们从容不迫地开进了教堂。

## XXII / 第二十二章

===Original===
The good fathers encouraged us with their exhortations, and some of them, mingling with us in the most dangerous places in the ranks, said to us﻿—

“My sons, do not be discouraged. Foreseeing this event we have saved moderate quantities of food, and we have wine also. Give this mob plenty of powder! Courage, dear boys! Do not be afraid of the enemy’s lead. You do more damage with one of your glances than they with a discharge of lead. Forward, my sons! The Holy Virgin del Pilar is with you. Don’t wince at danger; face the enemy calmly, and in the cloud of battle you will see the holy form of the Mother of God. Viva Spain and Fernando VII!”

We reached the church; but the French, who had preceded us by the sacristy, already occupied the high altar. I had never before seen a churrigueresque altar all covered with sculptures and garlands of gold, serving as a breastwork for infantry; nor had I ever seen niches which served as the lodging places of a thousand carved saints vomiting forth fire. I had never seen the rays of gilded wood which shed their changeless light from pasteboard clouds peopled by little angels, confused with gun-flashes; nor behind the feet of Christ, and back of the golden halo of the Virgin Mary, the avenging eyes of soldiers taking death-dealing aim.

===Chinese===
善良的神父们以劝勉之辞激励着我们，其中几位还穿插到队列中最危险的地方，对我们说道——

“我的孩子们，不要气馁。我们早料到会有这一天，存下了一批数量适中的口粮，酒也备下了。给这帮匪兵狠狠地多喂火药！勇敢些，亲爱的孩子们！不要怕敌人的铅弹。你们瞪上一眼，比他们放一排铅弹杀伤的还要多。前进，我的孩子们！皮拉尔至圣圣母与你们同在。危险当前不要畏缩；沉着地面对敌人，在战场的硝烟云雾之中，你们会看见天主之母的神圣形影。西班牙万岁，费尔南多七世万岁！”

我们赶到了教堂；可是法军已经抢在我们前头，从圣器室过来占据了主祭坛。我从未见过一座丘里格拉式（churrigueresque）的祭坛——周身覆满雕塑和金色的花环——竟被充作步兵的胸墙；也从未见过那些壁龛——千百尊雕刻圣徒的安身之所——竟向外喷吐着火焰。我从未见过一束束镀金木雕的光芒从纸板做的云朵间洒下恒定不变的光辉，云朵上布满小小的天使，如今与枪口的火光混作一片；也从未见过，在基督圣足的背后、在圣母玛利亚金色光轮的后方，竟藏着士兵们复仇的眼睛，正在作致命的瞄准。

===Original===
It is well to say that the high altar of San Augustine was an enormous one, filled with gilded wooden sculptures, like others you have seen in any of the churches of Spain. It extended from the floor to the arch above, and from wall to wall, and represented in row upon row the celestial hierarchies. Above, the bloodstained Christ spread his arms upon the cross; below, and on the altar, a little shrine enclosed the symbol of the Eucharist. Although the whole was supported by the ground and the walls, there were little interior covered ways destined for the special services of that republic of saints, and by them the sacristan could ascend from the sacristy to change the dress of the Virgin, to light the candles before the highest crucifix, or to clean the dust of centuries from the antique fabrics and painted wood of the images.

Well, the French rapidly gained possession of the *camarín* of the Virgin, and the narrow passages I have spoken of. When we arrived, from behind each saint, in every niche, gleamed a gun barrel. Established thus behind the altar, and advancing slowly forward, they were preparing to take all of this upper part of the church.

===Chinese===
应当交代一句：圣奥古斯丁的主祭坛是一件庞然大物，满是镀金的木雕，同你在西班牙任何一座教堂里见过的那些相仿。它从地面直抵上方的拱顶，从这面墙横贯到那面墙，一层又一层地铺陈着天国的各品圣众。最高处，血迹斑斑的基督在十字架上张开双臂；下方，祭坛上，一座小小的圣龛供奉着圣体的标志。整座祭坛虽然依托地面和墙壁支撑，内里却辟有一条条小小的掩蔽道，专供那个圣徒共和国的种种特别差事之用；教堂司事顺着它们可以从圣器室一路登上来，为圣母更衣，给最高处的十字架点上蜡烛，或是拂去圣像古旧衣饰和彩绘木料上积了几个世纪的尘埃。

且说法军很快夺取了圣母的更衣室（*camarín*），连同我方才说起的那几条狭窄通道。我们赶到时，每一尊圣像背后、每一座壁龛里，都有枪管在闪光。

===Original===
We were not entirely unprotected; and in order to defend ourselves from the altarpiece, we occupied the confessionals, the altars of the chapels, and the galleries. Those of us who were most exposed were in the central nave; and while the more daring advanced resolutely towards the altar, others of us took positions in the lower choir; and from behind the chorister’s desk, from behind chairs and benches which we piled up against the choir-screen, we tried to dislodge the French nation from its possession of the high altar.

Tío Garcés, with others as brave, ran to occupy the pulpit, another churrigueresque structure whose sounding board was crowned by a statue of Faith which reached almost to the roof. They mounted, occupying the little stair and the great chair, and from there, by a singular chance, they shut up every Frenchman who dared to show his head in that direction. They also suffered great loss, for the men in the altar were much annoyed by the pulpit, and tried hard to get that obstacle out of their way. At last some twenty Imperials came out, evidently bent upon reducing at all hazards that wooden redoubt without whose possession it was madness to attempt to come out into the broad nave. I have never seen anything more like a great battle, and as in that the attention of both armies is concentrated upon one point, the most eagerly disputed of all, whose loss or conquest decides the outcome of the struggle, so the attention of all was now directed to the pulpit, so well defended and so well attacked. The twenty had to resist a sharp fire from us in the choir, and the hand-grenades which were thrown at them from the galleries. But in spite of great loss, they advanced resolutely, bayonets fixed, upon the pulpit stairway. The ten defenders of the fortress were not intimidated, and defended themselves with empty guns, with the unfailing superiority which they always showed in that kind of conflict. Many of our men who were firing from the chapel altars and the confessionals, ran to attack the French with their swords, representing in that way, in miniature, conditions of a rude field battle; the contest was waged, man to man, with bayonet-thrusts, guns, and blows as each one met his adversary.

===Chinese===
我们并非全无遮护；为了抵御祭坛后屏那边射来的火力，我们占据了告解室、各小堂的祭坛和楼廊。暴露得最厉害的是中殿里的我们这些人；胆大的径直朝主祭坛进逼，我们另一些人则在下层唱诗席占了位子；从领唱席的桌后，从堆抵在唱诗席围屏上的椅子长凳后面，我们努力要把法兰西民族从它占据的主祭坛上撵出去。

加尔塞斯大叔同另外几个同样勇敢的人跑去占据了讲道台。那也是一座丘里格拉式的建筑，顶上的华盖立着一尊信德像，几乎抵到屋脊。他们登上台去，占住小楼梯和大讲椅，从那里——说来也怪——凡是敢朝那边探头的法军，都被他们一个个封了口。他们自己也损失惨重，因为据守祭坛的那些人被这座讲道台搅得不胜其烦，拼命要除掉这个障碍。终于，约莫二十个帝国士兵冲了出来，分明是不惜一切代价也要拿下这座木头棱堡——不先占领它，就妄想冲进开阔的中殿。我从未见过比这更像一场大会战的场面：正如大会战中两军的注意力都凝注在某一个争夺最烈的要点上，它的得失决定整场斗争的结局；此刻所有人的注意力也都投向了这座讲道台，它守得如此出色，攻得也如此出色。那二十个人得承受我们唱诗席方面猛烈的射击，还要挨楼廊里朝他们扔下的手榴弹。但他们不顾惨重的损失，端着上了刺刀的枪，坚定地朝讲道台的阶梯进逼。棱堡的十名守卫者并未胆寒，用打空了的枪应战自卫，拿出他们在这类搏杀中素来显露的优势。我们许多人本来正从小堂的祭坛和告解室里放枪，这时也挥剑奔去攻击法军，以缩影的方式演出了一场粗野野战的情景；搏斗一对一地展开，各人迎着各人的对手，用刺刀捅，用枪杆打，用拳脚相加。

===Original===
The enemy was reinforced from the sacristy, and our rearguard also came out of the choir. Some who were in the gallery on the right jumped upon the cornice of a great reredos at one side, and not satisfied with firing from there, threw down upon the French three statues of saints that capped its three angles. Meantime the pulpit was still held bravely, and in that hell of flame I saw Tío Garcés standing erect, directing the men, and looking like a preacher screaming impudently with a hoarse voice. If I should ever see the devil preaching sin, standing on the great chair in the pulpit of a church invaded by all the other demons of hell in hideous riot, it would not especially attract my attention after that.

This could not last long; and Tío Garcés presently fell, screaming hoarsely, pierced by a hundred balls. The French, who had poured up by way of the sacristy, now advanced in a closed column, and in the three steps which separated the presbytery from the rest of the church, offered us a wall-like defence. When this column fired, the question of the pulpit was instantly settled, and having lost one out of every five of our men, leaving a large number of our dead upon the tiles of the floor, we retreated to the chapels. The first defenders of the pulpit, those who had gone to reinforce them, and Tío Garcés also, were picked up on bayonets, pierced through and tossed over the redoubt. So died that great patriot unnamed in history.

===Chinese===
敌人从圣器室得到了增援，我们的后队也从唱诗席里冲了出来。右边楼廊里有几个人跳上一侧一座大祭坛后屏的檐口，还不满足于从那里放枪，又把后屏三个角顶上的三尊圣徒像掀下去，砸向法军。这时讲道台仍被坚守不摇，在那片火焰的地狱里，我看见加尔塞斯大叔昂然挺立，指挥着手下的人，活像一个讲道者扯着嘶哑的嗓子肆无忌惮地狂吼。往后我若真见到魔鬼站在讲道台的大讲椅上宣讲罪恶，而地狱里其余的群魔正闹作一团涌入那座教堂，我想我也不会大惊小怪了。

这局面维持不了多久；加尔塞斯大叔随即倒下，嘶哑地喊叫着，被上百颗弹丸穿透了身体。从圣器室蜂拥而上的法军，此刻排成密集纵队推进，在圣所与教堂其余部分之间的那三级台阶上，像一堵墙似的挡住了我们。这个纵队一开火，讲道台的归属顷刻见了分晓；我们每五个人里折损一个，把大批阵亡的弟兄留在地面的瓷砖上，退进了各小堂。讲道台最初的守卫者、前去增援他们的人，连同加尔塞斯大叔，都被挑上刺刀顶起来，穿透身体，抛过棱堡。这位伟大的爱国者就这样死去，史册上不曾留下他的名字。

===Original===
The captain of our company remained lifeless also upon the pavement. We retired in disorderly fashion to various points separated from one another, not knowing who would command us. Indeed, the initiative of each one, or of each group of two or three, was the only organization then possible, and no one thought of companies or of military rank. All were obedient to one common purpose, and showed a marvellous instinctive knowledge of rudimentary strategy which the exigencies of the struggle demanded at every moment. This instinctive insight made us understand that we were lost from the time that we got into the chapels on the right, and it was rashness to persist in the defence of the church before the great numbers of the French who now occupied it. Some of our soldiers thought that with the benches, the images, and the wood of an old altarpiece, which could easily be broken to pieces, we ought to raise a barricade in the arch of our chapel, and defend ourselves to the last; but two Augustine fathers opposed this useless effort.

“My sons, do not trouble yourselves to prolong the resistance which will only destroy you, and give our side no advantage,” said one of them. “The French are attacking this moment by the Calle de las Arcadas. Hasten there, and see if you can not harass them; but do not imagine that you can defend the church profaned by these savages.”

These exhortations decided us to leave the church. Some of the Estremadura men remained in the choir, exchanging shots with the French, who now filled the nave. The friars only half-fulfilled their promise of giving us something for which to sing “Gaudeamus.” As a recompense for having defended their church to the last extreme, they were giving us some bits of jerked beef and dry bread, without our seeing or smelling the wine anywhere, in spite of our straining our eyes and our nostrils. But to explain this, they said that the French, occupying all the upper part, had possession of all the principal storehouse of provisions. Lamenting this, they tried to console us with praises of our good behavior.

===Chinese===
我们连的上尉也倒在铺石的地面上，没了气息。我们乱糟糟地退到彼此分隔的各个据点，不知道该由谁来指挥我们。说实话，当时唯一可能有的组织，就是各人各自出头，或两三人一伙地自行其是，谁也不再想到连队和军阶。人人都服从一个共同的意志，对这场搏斗时时刻刻所要求的粗浅战法，表现出一种令人惊叹的本能的通晓。正是这种本能的直觉使我们明白：打从退进右边小堂的那一刻起，我们就已经败了；在如今占据教堂的大股法军面前还要死守教堂，纯属莽撞。我们有些士兵认为，应当用长凳、圣像和一座旧祭坛后屏的木料——这些东西都容易砸碎——在我们小堂的拱门处筑起一道街垒，抵抗到最后；可是两位奥古斯丁会的神父拦阻了这番无谓的努力。

“我的孩子们，别再费心把这场抵抗拖下去了，那只会把你们毁掉，对我方没有一点好处，”其中一位说道，“法军此刻正从阿尔卡达斯街（Calle de las Arcadas）攻打。赶快到那边去，看看能不能骚扰他们；但不要以为，你们守得住这座已被这帮野蛮人玷污的教堂。”

这番劝导使我们决意撤出教堂。一些埃斯特雷马杜拉人留在唱诗席，同挤满中殿的法军对射。修士们先前许诺给我们一些值得为之唱一曲“Gaudeamus”（让我们欢乐吧）的东西，如今只兑现了一半：作为我们把他们的教堂守到最后一刻的报偿，他们端出来的是几小块肉干和干面包；任凭我们睁大眼睛、张大鼻孔，也哪儿都看不见酒、闻不到酒。为了解释这一层，他们说，法军占领了整个上层，把主要的存粮仓库全都攥在了手里。修士们为此痛惜不已，又竭力夸赞我们的出色表现，想以此安慰我们。

===Original===
The failure of the wine made me remember the great Pirli. I happened to recollect that I had seen him at the beginning of the battle. I asked for him, but nobody could account for his disappearance. The French occupied the church, and also some of the upper part of the convent. In spite of our unfavorable position below, we were resolved to go on resisting; and we bore in mind the heroic conduct of the volunteers of Huesca, who defended Las Mónicas until they were buried beneath its ruins. We were maddened, and believed ourselves disgraced if we did not conquer. We were impelled to these desperate struggles by a hidden, irresistible force which I cannot explain except as the strong tension and spiritual exaltation springing from our aspirations towards the ideal.

An order from outside stopped us, dictated doubtless by the practical good sense of General Saint March.

“The convent cannot be held,” it was said. “Instead of sacrificing men with no advantage to the city, let all go out to defend the points attacked in the Calle de Pabostre, and the Puerta Quemada, where the enemy are trying to advance, conquering houses from which they have been repulsed various times.”

We therefore left San Augustine. While we were passing through the street of the same name, parallel with the Calle de Palomar, we saw that they were throwing hand-grenades among the French established in a little opening near the latter of these two streets. Who was throwing those projectiles from the tower? In order to tell it more briefly, and with greatest eloquence, let us open the history and read: “In the tower six or eight peasants had placed themselves, having provided themselves with provisions and ammunition to harass the enemy. They continued to hold it for some days without being willing to surrender.”

There was the glorious Pirli! Oh, Pirli, more happy than Tío Garcés, thou dost occupy a place in history!

===Chinese===
酒既落了空，我想起了伟大的皮尔利。我恰巧记起，开战之初还见过他一面。我向人打听，可谁也说不清他是怎么失踪的。法军占着教堂，还占了修道院上层的几处。尽管我们在下层的处境不利，我们仍决意抵抗下去；我们记着韦斯卡义勇军的壮烈——他们坚守拉斯莫尼卡斯，直到被埋在它的废墟之下。我们都疯狂了，都觉得若不能打胜，便是奇耻大辱。驱使我们投入这些殊死搏杀的，是一股隐秘而不可抗拒的力量，我只能把它解释为：我们对理想的向往所迸发出的强大张力与精神的激扬。

一道来自外面的命令止住了我们，这无疑是出于圣马尔奇将军那讲求实际的明智。

命令里说：“修道院守不住了。与其白白牺牲人手而无益于全城，不如让大家都出去守卫帕博斯特雷街和克马达门正遭攻击的各处——敌人正竭力在那边推进，想夺取那些他们已被击退过多次的房屋。”

于是我们撤出了圣奥古斯丁。途经与之同名的街——它与帕洛马尔街平行——时，我们看见有人正朝一小块空地上的法军投掷手榴弹，那空地就在这两条街中帕洛马尔街的近旁。是谁在塔上往外投这些家伙？要把这件事讲得简短些，也最雄辩些，且让我们翻开史册读一读：“有六至八名乡民据守塔中，自备口粮弹药，专事骚扰敌军。他们一连坚守数日，不肯投降。”

那不正是光荣的皮尔利嘛！啊，皮尔利，你比加尔塞斯大叔更有福气，你竟在史册上占得了一个位置！

## XXIII / 第二十三章

===Original===
Incorporated into the battalion of Estremadura, we went along the Calle de Palomar into the Plaza de la Magdalena, whence we could hear the roar of battle at the end of the Calle de Puerta Quemada. As we have said, the enemy tried to take the Calle de Pabostre in order to get possession of Puerta Quemada, an important point whence they could rake with their artillery the street of the same name towards the Plaza de la Magdalena. As the possession of San Augustine and Las Mónicas permitted them to threaten that central point by the easy way to the Calle de Palomar, they already considered themselves masters of the suburb. In fact, if those in San Augustine managed to advance to the ruins of the Seminary, and those of the Calle de Pabostre to the Puerta Quemada, it would be impossible to dispute with the French the quarter of Las Tenerías.

After a short time they took us to the Calle de Pabostre, and as the battle of the outside and inside of the buildings and of the public way was now all combined, we entered the first block by the Calle de los Viejos. From the windows of the house in which we found ourselves, we could see nothing but smoke, and could tell but little of what was going on there. I saw later that the street was all filled with embrasures and trenches at certain distances made of heaps of earth, furniture, and rubbish. From the windows a tremendous fire was poured forth, and, remembering a phrase of the beggar Sursum Corda, I can say that our souls were turned into bullets. Inside the houses the blood flowed in torrents. The onset of the French was terrible, and that the resistance might not be less terrible the belfries summoned men unceasingly. The general dictated stern orders for the punishment of stragglers. The friars rallied the people of other districts, dragging them forward as in a leash. Some heroic women set an example, throwing themselves into danger, guns in hand.

A dreadful day, whose frightful roar resounds ever in the ears of him who was present! Its remembrance pursues him, an unescaped nightmare, through his whole life. He who did not see these horrors, who did not hear the noise of that shouting, knows not with what expression the depths of the horrible may be uttered to human feeling. Do not tell me that you have seen the crater of a volcano in the most violent eruption; or a great tempest in the open ocean when the ship, tossed to heaven on a mountain of waterfalls, descends next to a giddy depth﻿—do not tell me you have seen these things, for they are nothing at all like the volcanoes and tempests of man when his passions urge him to out-rival the disorders in Nature.

===Chinese===
编入埃斯特雷马杜拉营之后，我们取道帕洛马尔街开进拉马格达莱纳广场（Plaza de la Magdalena），从那里听得见克马达门街（Calle de Puerta Quemada）尽头传来的战斗轰鸣。如前所述，敌人力图夺取帕博斯特雷街，以便占据克马达门——那是一处紧要据点，占了它，他们便可纵炮扫射与之同名的那条街，直扫到拉马格达莱纳广场。圣奥古斯丁与拉斯莫尼卡斯既已在其手中，他们尽可循帕洛马尔街这条便道威胁那一处中枢要点，于是已自视为这片近郊城区的主人了。事实上，倘若圣奥古斯丁方面的法军得以推进到神学院的废墟，帕博斯特雷街方面的法军得以进抵克马达门，那么特内里亚斯区便再也无法同法军相争了。

不多时，我们被调往帕博斯特雷街；此时房屋里外与街道之上的战斗已经浑然连成一片，我们便由洛斯别霍斯街（Calle de los Viejos）进入第一个街区。从我们所在那座房子的窗口望出去，除了硝烟什么也看不见，那里究竟进行到什么地步，几乎无从知晓。后来我才看清，那条街上每隔一段距离，便垒起一道道炮眼与堑壕，全用成堆的泥土、家具和瓦砾筑成。窗口喷吐出猛烈的火力；想起乞丐 Sursum Corda 的那句妙语，我可以说，我们的魂灵都化作了子弹。房屋之内，血如泉涌。法军的攻势凶猛骇人；而为了叫我方的抵抗不输这份凶猛，一座座钟楼不住地把人召唤上前。将军口授下一道道严令，惩办掉队者。修士们把别的教区的百姓聚拢起来，像牵着索链似的把他们朝前拽。一些英勇的妇女以身作则，端着枪直投到危险之中。

可怕的一天啊！那骇人的轰响，永远萦回在亲历者的耳畔！对这一天的记忆追逐着他，像一场再也逃脱不掉的梦魇，纠缠他一生。没有见过这些恐怖、没有听过那片喊杀声的人，便不知道“恐怖”的深处还能以怎样的言辞向人类的心灵诉说。别对我说你见过火山最猛烈喷发时的火山口，或见过外洋上的大风暴——船只被抛上一座飞瀑堆成的高山、甩向半天，转瞬又坠向叫人目眩的深渊——别对我说你见过这些东西，因为它们同人间的火山与风暴毫无一点相像：那要等到人的激情驱策他去赛过大自然的狂暴失序之时。

===Original===
It was difficult to hold us back, and not being able to do much where we were, we descended to the street without noticing the officers who tried to hold us back. The combat had an irresistible attraction for us, and called us as the deep calls unto a man who looks down upon it from a cliff. I have never considered myself heroic; but it is certain that in those moments I did not fear death, nor did the sight of catastrophes terrify me. It is true that heroism, as a thing of the moment, and the direct child of inspiration, does not belong exclusively to the brave. That is the reason it is often found in women and cowards.

I will not go into the details of those struggles in the Calle de Pabostre. They were much like those which I have described before. If they differed in any respect, it was in their excess of constancy, and energy raised to a height where the human ended and the divine began. Within the houses, scenes passed like those I have described elsewhere, but with greater carnage, because victory was believed more certain. The advantage the men of the Empire gained in one place they lost in another. The battles, begun in the attics, descended step by step to the cellars, and were finished there with clubbed muskets, with the advantage always on the side of our peasants. The tones of command with which one or another directed the movements within these labyrinths resounded from room to room with fearful echoes. They used their artillery in the street, and we did also. Often they tried to get possession of our pieces by sudden hand-to-hand struggles; but they lost many men without ever succeeding.

Alarmed on seeing that the force used at one time to gain a battle was not now sufficient to gain two yards of a street, they refused to fight, and their officers drove them forward, beating their laziness out of them with cudgels. On our side such measures were not necessary; persuasion was enough. The priests, without neglecting the dying, attended to everything. If they saw a weakening anywhere, they would hasten to tell the officers.

===Chinese===
我们很难被拦得住；而既然待在原地出不了什么力，我们便下到街上去，全不理会那些想要拦住我们的军官。战斗对我们有一种无法抗拒的吸引力，召唤着我们，就像深渊召唤着一个自悬崖俯视它的人。我从不曾自命英雄；但可以肯定的是，在那些时刻我不惧怕死亡，大难临头的景象也吓不倒我。诚然，英雄主义既是一时之物，又是灵感的亲生儿，并不专属勇士。正因如此，它才常常在女人和懦夫身上寻见。

帕博斯特雷街上那些搏斗的细节，我就不去细述了。它们同我先前描述过的那些大同小异。若说有什么不同，那便是坚忍得过了限度，气力升腾到了人性告终、神性发端的高处。房屋之内，一幕幕场景同我在别处描述过的相仿，只是屠戮更甚，因为人人都相信胜利更加十拿九稳了。帝国士兵在一处占到的便宜，又在另一处丢还了回去。战斗从阁楼开打，一步一步降入地窖，最后在那里以倒持火枪、枪柄互殴收场，占上风的永远是我们这边的庄稼汉。号令的声调指挥着这一座座迷宫之内的调度，从一间屋子响到另一间屋子，激起可怖的回声。他们在街上使炮，我们也使。他们屡屡想凭突如其来的白刃格斗夺走我们的炮；可是人折损了许多，却从未得手一回。

法军眼见从前足以赢得一场会战的兵力，如今连夺取两码街道都不敷使用了，不免惊慌；他们不肯再战，军官们便把他们朝前驱赶，用棍棒把他们周身的懒怠痛打出去。在我们这一边，这样的手段全然用不着；好言劝勉就够了。神父们既不怠慢垂死的人，又事事照管。哪里一见松动，他们便赶紧告知军官们。

===Original===
In one of the trenches in the street, a woman, bravest of all, Manuela Sancho, after having fired with a gun, began serving cannon number eight. She remained unhurt all day, encouraging all with brave words﻿—an example to the men. It was perhaps three o’clock when she fell, wounded in the leg, and during a long time was supposed to be dead, because the hemorrhage made her seem lifeless; she looked like a corpse. Later, seeing that she breathed, we carried her to the rear, and she was restored, and had such good health afterwards that many years later I had the pleasure of seeing her still alive.

History has not forgotten that brave young Maid of Saragossa. The Calle de Pabostre, whose poor houses are more eloquent than the pages of a book, now bears the name of Manuela Sancho.

A little after three o’clock, a tremendous loud explosion shook the houses which the French had disputed with us in such a bloody manner during the morning. Amid the dust, and the smoke thicker than dust, we saw walls and roofs falling in a thousand pieces, with a noise of which I can give no idea. The French had begun to employ mines, in order to gain that which they could in no other way wrench from the hands of the sons of Aragón. They opened galleries; they charged the mines; then the men folded their arms, waiting for the powder to do it all.

===Chinese===
在街上的一处堑壕里，有一位妇女，是所有人当中最勇敢的——玛努埃拉·桑乔；她先是持枪放了一阵，随后便操持起八号大炮。整整一天她毫发无伤，用豪迈的话语鼓舞着众人——给男人们立了个榜样。约莫三点钟，她倒下了，腿部负伤；因为失血使她看上去气息全无，很长一段时间里大家都以为她死了；她那样子活像一具尸体。后来见她还有鼻息，我们把她抬到后方，她竟康复了，而且此后身子骨一直那样硬朗，以至许多年后我还有幸见到她依然健在。

历史没有遗忘那位萨拉戈萨的勇敢少女。帕博斯特雷街那些寒酸的房屋，比书页更有雄辩之力，如今这条街便以玛努埃拉·桑乔为名。

三点刚过，一声惊天动地的爆炸震动了法军上午同我们浴血相争的那几座房屋。尘土弥漫，烟尘更浓于尘土，只见墙垣与屋顶碎作千百块纷纷坠落，那声响我实在无从形容。法军开始动用坑道埋雷，好夺得他们再无别法从阿拉贡之子手中抢走的东西。他们开掘导坑，装药入雷，随后那些人便袖起双手，单等火药去包办一切。

===Original===
When the first house went, we stayed quietly in the next, and in the street. But when the second went with a still louder noise, the retreat began with plenty of disorder. Considering that so many unfortunate comrades were hurled into the air or buried beneath the ruins, men who had been unconquerable by force of arms, we felt ourselves too weak to contend with the new element of destruction. It seemed to us that in all the other houses, and in the street, horrible craters were going to burst forth which would send us flying, torn into a thousand bloody fragments.

The officers held us back, calling﻿—

“Courage, boys, stand firm! That is done to frighten us. We have plenty of powder, too, and we will open mines. Do you think this will give them an advantage? On the contrary, we shall see how they will defend themselves among a lot of fragments.”

===Chinese===
第一座房子飞上天时，我们还安安静静待在隔壁一座里、待在街上。可当第二座以更响的声势塌下去时，溃退便大乱着开始了。想到那么多不幸的战友被抛上半空、或被埋进废墟——那些凭刀枪无法征服的汉子——我们只觉得自己太孱弱，斗不过这新出现的毁灭之力。我们仿佛觉得，其余所有的房子里、连同街上，都有一个个恐怖的火山口即将迸裂开来，把我们撕作千百块带血的碎片抛上天去。

军官们拦住我们，喊道——

“鼓起勇气来，小伙子们，站稳了！那是吓唬咱们的把戏。咱们的火药也足着呢，咱们也会挖坑道埋雷。你们以为这样就能让他们占到便宜吗？恰恰相反，咱们倒要瞧瞧，他们在一堆碎砖烂瓦之间怎么招架。”

===Original===
Palafox appeared at the entrance of the street, and his presence restrained us for some time. The noise prevented me from hearing what he was saying, but by his gestures I understood that he wished us to go on over the ruins.

“You hear, boys! You hear what the Captain-General says!” a friar shouted beside us, one of those who had come with Palafox. “He says that if you will make a little exertion, not one Frenchman will be left alive.” “You are right!” cried another friar. “There will not be a woman left in Saragossa who will even look at you, if you do not hurl yourselves instantly upon those ruins of the houses, and drive the French out.”

“Forward, sons of the Virgin del Pilar!” cried out a third friar. “Do you see those women over there? Do you know what they are saying? They are saying that if you do not go, they will go themselves. Are you not ashamed of your cowardice?”

With that, we stood up a little more bravely. Another house fell on the right. Palafox came into the street. Without knowing how or why, we followed him when he put himself at our head. Now is the time to speak of that high personage whose name and fame are one with that of Saragossa. His prestige is due in large measure to his great courage, but also to his noble origin, and the respect in which the family of Lazán has always been held in Saragossa, and to his handsome and spirited presence. He was young. He had belonged to the Guards. He was much praised for having refused the favors of a very highly-placed lady, as famous for her position as for scandals about her. That which endeared the Saragossan leader more than anything else to his people was, however, his supreme, his indomitable courage, the youthful ardor with which he attacked the most dangerous and difficult obstacles, simply to reach his ideals of honor and glory.

===Chinese===
帕拉福克斯在街口现身了，他的到场把我们稳住了一些时候。喧声震耳，我听不见他在说什么，但凭他的手势我明白了：他要我们踏着废墟继续前进。

“听见没有，小伙子们！听见总司令说什么了！”我们身旁一个修士高声喊道，他是随帕拉福克斯同来的那批人中的一个。“他说，只要你们肯再使一把劲，一个法国佬也别想活着留下。”“说得对！”另一个修士嚷道，“你们要是不立刻扑向那些房子的废墟、把法国佬赶出去，往后萨拉戈萨连一个肯抬眼看你们的女人都剩不下。”

“前进，皮拉尔圣母的儿女们！”第三个修士喊道。“瞧见那边那些女人了吗？知道她们在说什么吗？她们说，你们要是不上，她们就自己上。你们不为自己的怯懦害臊吗？”

听了这话，我们挺身站得勇敢了些。右边又有一座房子塌了。帕拉福克斯走进街来。也不知是怎么、又是为什么，当他把身子朝我们前头一放，我们便跟着他走了。现在正该说一说这位显赫人物了，他的名字与声名，同萨拉戈萨已是融为一体。他的威望，一大部分要归功于他的大勇，但也要归功于他高贵的出身、拉桑家族在萨拉戈萨素来享有的敬重，以及他英俊英武的仪表。他正当年轻，曾在王室卫队供职。他因拒不领受一位地位极高的贵妇人的恩宠而备受称誉——那位夫人既以地位著称，也一样以围绕她的风流丑闻闻名。不过，使这位萨拉戈萨的统帅最得其民之爱戴的，毕竟还是他那至高无上、不可征服的勇气，是他扑向最危险、最艰难的障碍时的那股青春热忱——他为的不过是抵达他心中荣誉与光荣的理想。

===Original===
If he lacked intellectual gifts to direct an undertaking so arduous as this, he had the prudence to know his lack, and to surround himself with men distinguished for their judgment and wisdom. These men did everything. Palafox was the great figurehead, the chief actor in the scene. Over a people so largely ruled by imagination, that young general could scarcely fail to hold an imperious dominion, with his illustrious lineage and splendid figure. He showed himself everywhere, encouraging the weak, and distributing rewards to the brave.

The Saragossans beheld in him the symbol of their constancy, their virtues, their patriotic ideal with its touch of mysticism, and their warlike zeal. Whatever he ordered, everybody found right and just. Like those monarchs whom traditional laws have made the personal embodiment of government, Palafox could do no wrong. Anything wrong was the work of his counsellors. In reality, the illustrious commander did not govern, he reigned. Father Basilio governed, with O’Neill, Saint March, and Butron, the first, an ecclesiastic, the other three noted generals.

In places of danger, Palafox always appeared like a human expression of triumph. His voice reanimated the dying; and if the Virgin del Pilar had spoken, she would have chosen no other mouth. His countenance always expressed a supreme confidence. In his triumphal smile, courage overflowed, as in others it is expressed by a ferocious frown. He was vaingloriously proud of being the prop of that great hour in history. He understood instinctively that the outcome depended more upon him as an actor than upon him as a general. He always appeared in all the splendors of his uniform, with gold lace, waving plumes, and medals. The thundering music of applause, of huzzas, flattered him extremely. All this was necessary. Indeed there must always be something of mutual adulation between the army and the commander-in-chief, in order that the pride of victory may inspire one and all to deeds of heroism.

===Chinese===
如果说他缺乏指挥这样一项艰巨事业的智力禀赋，他却有自知之明的审慎，知道自己的不足，也知道把以判断力与智慧见称的人士延揽到身边。凡事都是这些人做的。帕拉福克斯是那尊巨大的船首雕像，是这场大戏里的头号演员。对这样一个在很大程度上受想象力支配的民族，这位年轻将军凭着煊赫的世系与堂堂的仪表，几乎不可能不握有威严专断的统治权。他四处露面，激励怯弱者，给勇敢者颁发奖赏。

萨拉戈萨人在他身上看见了自身坚忍的象征、自身美德的象征、他们那带一抹神秘主义色彩的爱国理想与好战热忱的象征。他下的任何命令，人人都觉得又对又公道。正如那些被传统法制成政府之人格化身的君主们一样，帕拉福克斯是不会做错的。凡有错处，皆是谋臣的手笔。实际上，这位声名煊赫的统帅并不治理，他只是君临。真正治理的是巴西利奥·博希耶罗神父，与他共事的还有奥尼尔、圣马尔奇和布特龙——头一位是教士，其余三位皆知名将领。

在危险之地，帕拉福克斯露面时总像胜利的一个活人化身。他的嗓音能教垂死者回过魂来；倘若皮拉尔圣母开口说话，除他这张嘴也断不会另挑一张。他的面容永远流露着至高的自信。在他那凯旋的微笑里，勇气横溢而出，正如在另一些人身上，勇气靠狰狞的怒容来表达。他以骄矜得意之心，自居为历史上那伟大时刻的支柱。他本能地懂得，成败与其说系于他作为将军的才具，不如说更系于他作为演员的角色。他露面时总是军装的全部光华加身：金绦、飘拂的羽饰、勋章。掌声与欢呼汇成的雷鸣乐音，把他奉承到了极点。而这一切都是必要的。的确，军队与总司令之间，永远须有一点相互的颂扬，胜利的荣耀方能激励上下一心，共赴英雄的功业。

## XXIV / 第二十四章

===Original===
As I have said, Palafox pulled us together; and although we abandoned almost all of the Calle de Pabostre, we remained strong in the Puerta Quemada. If the battle was bloody until three, the hour when we centred in the Plaza de la Magdalena, it was not less bloody there until night. The French began to raise works in the houses ruined by the mines, and it was curious to see how among the masses of rubbish and beams small armed squares and covered ways were made and platforms to connect the artillery. That was a battle which every moment appeared less and less like any other known warfare.

From this new phase of contest resulted an advantage for us and a hindrance for the French. The demolition of the houses permitted them to place some new pieces, but the men were unprotected. To our misfortune, we could not avail ourselves of this because of the explosions. Fright made us think the danger multiplied a hundredfold, when in reality it was diminished. Not wishing to do less than they in that fiery duel, the Saragossans began to burn the houses in the Calle de Pabostre which they could not hold.

Besiegers and besieged, desirous of coming to an end of this, and not being able to attain it in such intricate burrowing warfare, began to destroy, one side by mining, the other by burning, remaining unprotected like the gladiator who throws away his shield.

===Chinese===
正如我前面所说，帕拉福克斯把我们收拢到一处；我们虽然放弃了帕博斯特雷街几乎全部地段，在克马达门却依旧守得牢固。到三点钟我们收缩集结于拉马格达莱纳广场（Plaza de la Magdalena）那一刻为止，这一仗打得血肉横飞；此后在那里直到入夜，惨烈也分毫未减。法军开始在被坑道炸毁的房屋里修筑工事；说来稀奇，但见成堆的瓦砾和梁木之间，辟出了一个个小小的武装方阵、一道道掩蔽道，还搭起了接连炮位的平台。这一场仗，每时每刻都越来越不像人们所知的任何一种战争。

战事的这一新阶段，于我们是便利，于法军是阻碍。房屋一经拆毁，他们固然得以安放几门新炮，操炮的人却暴露无遗。倒霉的是，由于接连的爆炸，我们无法利用这个破绽。恐惧使我们觉得危险陡增了百倍，实际上它倒是减小了。萨拉戈萨人不肯在这场烈火对决中甘居人后，便动手焚烧帕博斯特雷街上那些守不住的房屋。

围城者与被围者都巴望早点了结，却又在这般盘根错节的掘地战争中了结不得，便都动手毁灭起来——一方用坑道爆破，一方用纵火焚烧——双双不设遮护，恰似丢掉盾牌的角斗士。

===Original===
What an afternoon! What a night! Arriving here, I pause, wearied and breathless. My recollections are obscured, dimmed as my thoughts and my feelings were dimmed on that dreadful night. There came indeed a moment when being unable to resist longer, my body, like that of others of my comrades who had the fortune or misfortune to be still alive, dragged itself back across the gutters, stumbling over unburied bodies that seemed less than human among the debris. My feelings had flung me into an extreme of delirium, and I did not clearly know where I was. My idea of living was a confused, vague mixture of unheard-of miseries. It did not seem as if it was day, because in so many places the murk hung low, obscuring everything. Nor could I think it night, for flames like those we imagine in hell reddened the city on every side.

I only know that I dragged myself, stepping upon bodies, some dead and some still moving, and that farther on, always farther on, I thought I might find a piece of bread and a mouthful of water. What horrible mental dejection! What hunger! What thirst! I saw many running swiftly. I cried out to them. I saw their strange shadows throwing grotesque figures upon the neighboring walls. They were going and coming, I know not whence nor where. I was not the only one who, with body and soul exhausted after so many hours of fighting, had given out completely. Many others who had not the steel nerves of the Aragonese were dragging themselves along like myself, and we begged one another for a little water. Some, more fortunate than the rest, had the strength to look about among the corpses and find crusts of rations not eaten, fragments of meat, cold and dirty on the ground, which they devoured with avidity.

Somewhat revived, we went on looking, and I took my part of the tidbits of the feast. I did not know if I was wounded. Some of those who were talking with me, telling me of their dreadful hunger and thirst, had terrible wounds and burns and contusions. At last we came to some women who gave us water to drink, although it was muddy and warm. We disputed over the jug, and then in the hands of one of the dead we found a kerchief containing two dried sardines and little cakes. Encouraged by these repeated finds, we went on pillaging, and at last the little which we were able to eat, and, more than anything else, the dirty water we drank, gave us back a little strength.

===Chinese===
好一个下午！好一个夜晚！写到这里，我疲惫已极，喘息不定，只得停笔。我的回忆昏昧了，正如在那个可怕的夜晚，我的思绪与情感一般昏昧。确曾有那么一个时刻，我再也支撑不住；我的身子——同我那些有幸亦或不幸还活着的伙伴的身子一样——拖着爬过一道道阴沟，跌跌撞撞，绊在那些未及掩埋的尸身上，它们夹在残砖碎瓦间，几乎不成人形了。情绪把我抛进了谵妄的绝境，我不很清楚自己身在何处。我对活着的意念，成了一团混沌，含混地搅和着种种闻所未闻的苦楚。说这是白天吧，不像，因为黑沉沉的浊雾在那么多地方低低压着，遮蔽了一切；说这是黑夜吧，也不能，因为恰似我们想象中地狱的烈焰，从四面八方映红了全城。

我只记得自己在爬，脚底下踩着尸身，有的已死，有的还在动；只记得再往前，一直往前，我总以为能寻着一小块面包、一口水。心头何等可怕的颓丧！何等的饿！何等的渴！我望见许多人飞跑而过，我朝他们呼喊。我望见他们诡异的影子在近旁的墙上投下奇形怪状的身形。他们来来去去，我不知从哪里来、往哪里去。苦战了那么多钟头、身心俱竭而彻底垮掉的，并非我一人。还有许多没有阿拉贡人那副钢铁神经的人，也同我一样拖着身子爬行，我们彼此乞讨一点水。有些人运气好些，还有力气在死尸中间翻拣，找到没吃过的口粮硬皮、掉在地上又冷又脏的碎肉，便贪婪地吞吃下去。

缓过一点劲来，我们继续搜寻，我也在这场宴席上分得了一份珍馐。我不知道自己是否挂了彩。同我搭话的那几个人，一面诉说着他们可怕的饥渴，身上带着骇人的伤口、烧伤和瘀伤。最后我们遇到几个女人，给了我们水喝，虽然是浑的、温的。我们为着水罐你争我夺，随后又在一个死人手里发现一块手帕，里头包着两条干沙丁鱼和几块小饼。这一连串的意外所得令我们壮起胆来，继续搜掠；到头来，能吃下的那一点点东西，尤其是喝下去的脏水，总算给我们挣回了几分气力。

===Original===
I now felt myself able to walk a little, although with difficulty. I saw that my clothing was all soaked with blood. Feeling a lively smarting in my right arm, I supposed that I was severely wounded; but the hurt turned out to be an insignificant contusion, and the stains on my clothing came from creeping along through the pools of blood and mud.

I could now think clearly again. I could see plainly, and could hear distinctly the shouts and the hurried footsteps, the cannon-shots near and afar in dreadful dialogue. Their crashings here and yonder seemed like questions and replies.

The burning went on. There was a dense cloud over the city formed of dust and smoke, which, with the splendor of the flames, revealed horrible unearthly scenes like those of dreams.

===Chinese===
这时我觉得自己能走上几步了，尽管颇为吃力。我看见浑身衣裳都被血浸透了。右臂一阵火辣辣的刺痛，我只当自己伤得不轻；细看之下，那不过是无关紧要的一处瘀伤，衣上的血渍，是匍匐爬行时蹭过一洼洼血水泥泞沾上的。

我这时又能清醒地思考了。看得真切，也听得分明：呼喊声，匆促的脚步声，远处近处的炮声，在进行一场可怖的对话。这边的轰响与那边的炸裂，俨然一问一答。

大火还在烧。城市上空罩着尘埃与浓烟凝成的沉沉一幕，它连同烈焰的辉光，映现出种种可怖而非人间的景象，宛如梦中所见。

===Original===
The mangled houses, with their windows and openings glaring with the light like hellish eyes, the projecting angles of the smoking ruins, and the burning beams formed a spectacle less sinister than that of those leaping and unwearied figures that did not cease to move about here and there, almost in the centre of the flames. They were the peasants of Saragossa, who were still fighting with the French, and disputing with them every hand’s breadth of this hell.

I found myself in the Calle de Puerta Quemada. That which I have described was seen by looking in two directions from the Seminary, and from the entrance of the Calle de Pabostre. I went on a few steps, but fell again, overcome by fatigue. A priest, seeing me covered with blood, came up to me and began to talk to me of the future life, and of the eternal rewards destined for those who die for their country. He told me that I was not wounded; but that hunger, weariness, and thirst had prostrated me, and that I seemed to have the early symptoms of the epidemic. Then the good friar, in whom I recognized at once Father Mateo del Busto, seated himself beside me, sighing deeply.

“I can keep up no longer. I believe that I am going to die.”

===Chinese===
残破的房屋，窗洞门口喷吐着火光，宛如一只只地狱的眼睛；冒烟的废墟支棱出的墙角，燃烧的梁木——这一切构成的景象，其狰狞还逊于那些腾跃不倦的身影：他们几乎就在火焰正中，来来去去，穿梭不停，没有片刻止歇。那是萨拉戈萨的乡民，他们还在同法军厮杀，同他们争夺这片地狱里的每一掌之地。

我发觉自己正在克马达门街（Calle de Puerta Quemada）上。我方才描摹的种种景象，从神学院那边朝两个方向望去可以看见，从帕博斯特雷街口望去也可以看见。我朝前走了几步，又被疲惫压得跌倒在地。一位神父见我浑身是血，走上前来，开始对我讲来世，讲为国捐躯者命定得享的永恒报偿。他对我说我并没有受伤，只是饥饿、劳顿与干渴把我撂倒了，而且我似乎已露出时疫的初期症状。随后这位好修士——我一眼认出，正是马特奥·德尔·布斯托神父——在我身旁坐下，深深叹息。

“我撑不住了。我想我就要死了。”

===Original===
“Is your reverence wounded?” I asked, seeing a linen cloth bound upon his right arm.

“Yes, my son. A ball has destroyed my shoulder and arm. I am in the greatest pain, but I must bear it. Christ suffered more for us. Since daybreak I have been busy, caring for the wounded and pointing the dying to heaven. I have not rested a moment for sixteen hours, nor have I eaten nor drank anything. A woman tied this linen on my right arm, and I went about my work. I believe that I shall not live long. What a death! My God, and all these wounded with no one to take care of them! But, oh, I can no longer stand! I am dying! Have you seen that trench which is at the end of the Calle de los Clavos? Over there poor Coridon is lying, lifeless, the victim of his own courage. We were passing along there to take care of some of the wounded, when we saw, near the garden of San Augustine, a group of Frenchmen who were passing from one house to another. Coridon, whose impetuous blood impelled him to the most daring acts, threw himself upon them. They bayoneted him, and flung him in the ditch. How many victims in a single day, Araceli! Indeed, you are fortunate in not being hurt. But you will die of the epidemic, and that is worse. Today I have given absolution to sixty who were dying of the epidemic. I give it to you also, my friend, because I know you have committed no sins, only peccadilloes, and that you have borne yourself valiantly in these days. How is it? Do you feel worse? Truly you are yellower than these corpses about us. To die of the epidemic during this horrible siege is to die for one’s country. Courage, young man! Heaven is open to receive you, and the Virgin del Pilar will welcome you with her mantle of the stars. Life is nothing. How much better it is to die honorably, and to gain eternal glory by the suffering of a day! In the name of God, I forgive you your sins!”

Then after murmuring the prayer appropriate to the occasion, he blessed me, and pronounced the *Ego te absolvo*, and then lay down upon the ground. He looked very badly, and although I did not call myself well, I thought myself in a better state of health than the good friar. That was not the only time when the confessor died before the dying one, and the physician before the patient.

===Chinese===
“神父，您挂彩了吗？”我见他右臂上缠着一条亚麻布，问道。

“是的，我的孩子。一颗枪弹毁了我的肩膀和胳膊。疼得钻心，可我必须忍着。基督为我们受的苦更多。天一亮我就忙个不停，照料伤员，为垂死的人指点天国。十六个钟头我没有歇过一刻，也没吃没喝一口。一个女人把这块亚麻布缠上我的右臂，我便接着奔走我的差事。我想我活不了多久了。怎样的死法啊！我的上帝，这满地的伤员，竟没有一个人照管！可是，唉，我站不住了！我死了！你看见克拉沃斯街尽头的那条壕沟了吗？可怜的科里东（Coridon）就躺在那边，没了气，做了他自己血气之勇的牺牲品。我们方才打那里经过，要去照护几个伤员，忽见圣奥古斯丁的园子附近有一伙法军，正从一所房子窜向另一所。科里东那股火爆的血性催着他去干最胆大包天的事，便朝他们扑了过去。他们用刺刀捅了他，把他扔进了沟里。一天之内多少死难者啊，阿拉塞利！说真的，你身上没伤，算是幸运的了。可你会死于时疫，那更糟。今天我已经给六十个染时疫垂死的人赦了罪。我也给你赦罪，我的朋友，因为我知道你没犯过罪，只有些小过失，也知道你这些天表现得英勇。怎么样？你觉得更难受了吗？说真的，你比我们四周这些尸首还要黄。在这场可怕的围城中死于时疫，就是为国捐躯。振作起来，年轻人！天国的大门已为你敞开，皮拉尔圣母会张开她缀满星辰的披风迎接你。生命算不得什么。以一天的苦难受死而赢得永恒的光荣，那是多么合算！奉上帝之名，我赦免你的罪！”

说罢，他低声念了合乎此时此景的祷文，为我祝福，诵了 *Ego te absolvo*（“我赦免你”），随即躺倒在地。他的气色坏极了；我虽不敢说自己安好，却自觉比这位好修士强着一些。听告解的神父先于临终者死去、医生先于病人死去，这已不是头一遭了。

===Original===
I spoke to Father Mateo, and he did not answer me, except with piteous moans. I went a little way to look for someone who might be able to help him. I met several men and women, and told them, “Father Mateo del Busto is over there and cannot move;” but they took no notice of me and went on. Many of the wounded called upon me, begging for aid; but I took no notice at all of them. Near the Coso, I met a child of eight or ten years, who was alone, and weeping in the sorest distress. I stopped him. I asked him where his parents were, and he pointed to a place near where there was a great number of the wounded and dead. Afterwards I met the same child in several places, always alone and always crying aloud very bitterly. No one cared for him. I heard no questions, but, “Have you seen my brother?” “Have you seen my son?” “Have you seen my father?” But none of these were to be found in any direction. No one tried to take any of the wounded to the churches, because all or nearly all were crowded. The cellars and lower rooms which at first had been considered good places of refuge, were now infected with a death-dealing atmosphere. There came a time when the best place for the wounded was in the middle of the street.

I directed my steps towards the centre of the Coso, because they said that there they were giving out something to eat, but I received nothing. I was returning to Las Tenerías, and at last, in front of Almudí, they gave me a little hot food. That which seemed a symptom of the epidemic disappeared, for indeed my malady was only of the sort that can be cured with bread and wine. I remembered Father Mateo del Busto, and with some others went to help him. The unfortunate old man had not moved, and when we came up, and asked him how he found himself, he answered thus﻿—

“What is it? Has the bell sounded for matins? It is early. Leave me to rest. I find myself much fatigued, Father González. I have been picking flowers in the garden for sixteen hours, and I am tired.”

===Chinese===
我向马特奥神父说话，他除了凄惨的呻吟，不再答理我。我走开一段路去寻人帮他。我遇到几个男女，对他们说：“马特奥·德尔·布斯托神父在那边，动弹不得了；”他们却毫不理会，径自走他们的路。许多伤员向我呼救，哀求援助；我却全然没有理会他们。近科索大街处，我遇到一个八九岁或十来岁的孩子，孤身一人，哭得悲苦欲绝。我拦住他，问他父母在哪里，他朝近旁一处指了指——那里横着大群伤员和死者。此后我又在好几处地方遇见同一个孩子，总是独自一人，总是放声痛哭。没有一个人管他。我耳朵里再听不到别的问话，只有：“你看见我兄弟了吗？”“你看见我儿子了吗？”“你看见我父亲了吗？”可这些人哪个方向也寻不见了。谁也不再想把伤员抬进教堂，因为所有教堂，或者说几乎所有的教堂，都已人满为患。那些起初被当作上好避难所的地窖和底层房间，如今都弥漫着致人死命的瘴气。到后来，伤员最好的去处，竟是大街正中。

我举步朝科索大街中央走去，因为有人说那里在发放吃食，可我什么也没领到。我往特内里亚斯区走回去，最后，在阿尔穆迪跟前，他们给了我一点热食。那看似时疫征兆的东西消散了；说实在的，我的病，不过是面包和酒就能治好的那一种。我想起马特奥·德尔·布斯托神父，便同另外几个人回去帮他。这位不幸的老人没有挪动地方；我们走到跟前，问他自觉如何，他这样答道——

“怎么啦？敲夜祷钟了吗？还早呢。让我歇一歇。我累得很，冈萨雷斯神父（Father González）。我在园子里摘了十六个钟头的花，累了。”

===Original===
In spite of his entreaty, we four took him up; but we had carried him only a short distance before he was dead in our arms.

My comrades ran to the front, and I was preparing to follow them, when I happened to see a man whose looks attracted my attention. It was Candiola. He was coming out of a house near by with his clothing scorched, and grasping between his hands a fowl, which cackled at being held captive. I stopped him in the middle of the street, questioning him about his daughter and Augustine. He answered me in a very disturbed way﻿—

“My daughter﻿—I do not know﻿—there she is﻿—somewhere. All, all! I have lost all. The receipts, the receipts were burned. Fortunately I got out of the house, and as I fled I came upon this chicken which, like me, was flying from the dreadful flames. Yesterday, a hen was worth five duros. But my receipts! Holy Virgin del Pilar, and thou, dear little Santo Domingo of my soul, why have ye let my receipts be burned? They, at least, might have been saved. Do you wish to help me? The tin box which held them is still there pinned down under a great beam. Where can you find half a dozen men for me? Good God, this junta, these authorities, this Captain-General, what are they thinking of?” And he went on, calling out to the passersby, “Eh, peasant, friend, dear man, let us see if we cannot lift the beam which has fallen into the corner. Oh, friends, put down that dying man you are carrying to the hospital, and come and help me. Oh, pitiless Saragossans, how God is chastising you!” Seeing that none came to help him, he went into the house, but came out again, crying out in desperation, “Already it is too late to save anything! Everything is on fire. Oh, my Virgin del Pilar, why dost thou not perform a miracle for me? Why not give me such a gift as that bestowed upon the children in the fiery furnace of Babylon, so that I could go into the teeth of the fire and save my receipts!”

===Chinese===
我们没有依他的恳求，四个人把他抬了起来；可是抬出去没有多远，他已经死在我们怀中了。

伙伴们朝前沿跑去，我正要跟上，恰好瞥见一个人，那副模样引住了我的目光。是坎迪奥拉。他从近旁一座宅子里出来，衣裳烧得焦黑，两手紧攥着一只鸡，那禽被擒在手里，咯咯地叫。我在街当中拦住他，问起他女儿和奥古斯丁。他答话时心神大乱——

“我的女儿——我不知道——在那儿——在什么地方。全完了，全完了！我把一切都丢了。字据，字据烧掉了。总算运气，我从宅子里逃了出来，逃出来的时候撞见这只鸡，它跟我一样，正躲那可怕的烈火。昨天，一只母鸡还值五个杜罗（duros）呢。可我的字据！皮拉尔至圣圣母啊，还有你，我心头的小圣多明尼基托，你们怎么忍心让我的字据烧掉？它们，至少它们，本来是可以救出来的呀！你愿意帮帮我吗？装字据的那个白铁盒还在那儿，被一根大梁死死压住。你能上哪儿替我找到半打人来？好上帝啊，这议会，这些官府，这位总司令，他们心里都在想些什么？”他接着又朝过路的人喊：“喂，老乡，朋友，亲爱的人，来试试能不能把那根塌在墙角里的梁抬起来。噢，朋友们，把你们正抬往医院的那个垂死的人放下，来帮帮我吧。噢，没心肝的萨拉戈萨人哪，上帝就是这样惩罚你们！”见没有人来帮，他便走进宅子，随即又冲出来，绝望地喊道：“抢救什么都来不及了！全都烧着了。噢，我的皮拉尔圣母，你为什么不给我行个奇迹？为什么不赐我巴比伦火窑里那些少年所得的恩典，好让我迎着烈火的锋头闯进去，救出我的字据！”

## XXV / 第二十五章

===Original===
Presently he seated himself upon a pile of stones, beating his brow from time to time, and without loosening his hold of the chicken, he laid his hand upon his heart, sighing deeply. I questioned him again about his daughter, desiring to hear news of Augustine; and he said to me﻿—

“I was in that house in the Calle de Añón, where we moved in yesterday. Everybody told me that it was not safe there, and that we had much better be in the middle of the town; but it does not suit me to go where everybody else comes, and the place that I prefer is the one that the rest abandon. This world is filled with thieves and rascals. It is better that I get away from them. We managed with a lower room of that house. My daughter is very much afraid of the cannon, and wished to go elsewhere. When the mines began to burst under the neighboring houses, she and Guedita rushed away, terrified. I stayed alone, thinking of the danger my things are in; and pretty soon some soldiers came with flaming torches ready to set fire to the house. Those wretched cowards would not give me time to collect my things. Far from pitying my condition, they ridiculed me. I hid the box with my receipts for fear that those who think it is stuffed with money would carry it off; but it was impossible to stay inside long. I was surrounded with the bright flames, and choked with the smoke. In spite of everything, I insisted upon trying to save my box; but it was an impossible thing. I had to run. I could not take anything. Great God! I saved nothing but this poor creature, forgotten by its owners in the henhouse. It cost me a good deal of trouble to catch it. I burned one hand almost all over. Oh, cursed be he who invented fire! Why should one lose one’s fortune to amuse these heroes! I had two houses in Saragossa besides the one I lived in. One of them, the one in the Calle de la Sombra, is preserved to me still, although it is without tenants. The other, which was called Casa de los Duendes, back of the San Francisco is occupied by the troops, and everything there has been torn to pieces for me. Ruin, nothing but ruin! Is it a right thing to burn houses merely to retard the conquest by the French?”

===Chinese===
不多时，他在一堆石头上坐下，不时捶着额头，一手仍不放松那只鸡，另一只手按在心口，深深地叹息。我又问起他女儿，想打听奥古斯丁的消息；他对我说——

“我原本就在那座阿尼翁街（Calle de Añón）的宅子里，就是昨天我们搬进去的那座。人人都对我说那儿不保险，劝我们最好住到城中心去；可是大家都往哪儿挤，我就偏不往哪儿去；我中意的地方，正是旁人弃而不顾的地方。这世道到处是贼和流氓，我还是躲他们远些好。我们将就着住了那宅子的一间底层屋子。我女儿怕炮怕得厉害，一直想搬到别处去。等地雷在左邻右舍的房底下接连炸响，她跟格韦迪塔吓得夺路就逃。我独自留下，惦记着我那些东西的安危；不多会儿，来了几个士兵，举着熊熊的火把，就要放火烧房子。那些该死的胆小鬼，连给我收拾东西的工夫都不肯给。他们非但不怜恤我的处境，反倒嘲笑我。我把装字据的盒子藏了起来，生怕那些以为里头塞满了钱的人把它抢走；可是在屋里实在撑不了多久。我四面都是明晃晃的火焰，呛得喘不上气来。我不顾一切，非要抢出我的盒子不可；可那是办不到的事。我只好逃命，什么也带不出来。伟大的上帝啊！我除了这可怜的小东西，什么也没救出来——它被主人忘在了鸡棚里。逮住它还费了我好大的劲呢。我一只手几乎整个儿烧伤了。噢，愿发明火的那个人遭到诅咒！凭什么要人倾家荡产，去给这帮英雄取乐！我在萨拉戈萨除了自住的那一座，还有两座房子。一座在松布拉街（Calle de la Sombra），至今还给我留着，只是没有房客。另一座叫作精灵之家（Casa de los Duendes），在圣方济各修道院背后，被军队占了，里头的东西全给我糟蹋得稀烂。倾家荡产，倾家荡产！就为了拖住法军攻城的脚步，烧掉这些房子，难道是正当的吗？”

===Original===
“War makes it necessary to do these things,” I answered him. “And this heroic city desires to carry her defence to the last extreme.”

“And what induces Saragossa to wish to carry her defence to the last extreme? What good does it do to the dead? You may talk to them of glory, of heroism﻿—of all those notions. Before I ever come back to live in an heroic city, I would go to a desert. I concede that there should be a certain resistance, but not to such a barbarous extreme as this. It is true the burned buildings are worth little, perhaps less than the great mass of charcoal which will result. Don’t let them come to me with their foolish talk. Those fat sharpers are already planning to make a good business out of the carbon.”

This made me laugh. My readers must not think that I exaggerate, since he said all this to me very nearly as I repeat it; and those who have the misfortune to know him would most readily have faith in my veracity. If Candiola had lived in Numantia, it would have been said that the Numantines were merchants of charcoal mixed with heroes.

===Chinese===
“打仗嘛，就免不了要做这些事，”我答道，“再说，这座英雄的城市也决心把防御进行到最后关头。”

“到底是什么逼着萨拉戈萨非把防御进行到最后关头不可？这对死人有什么好处？你尽可以跟他们大谈荣耀啦、英雄主义啦——大谈那些个空想。要我再回一座英雄的城市里来住，我宁可去住沙漠。我承认，抵抗是该有一定的抵抗，可不该抵抗到这么野蛮的地步。说实话，烧掉的那些房子本也值不了几个钱，兴许还不如烧成的那一大堆木炭值钱呢。别拿那些蠢话来糊弄我。那帮脑满肠肥的骗子已经在盘算了，要靠这批木炭做上一笔好生意。”

这一番话把我逗笑了。我的读者千万不要以为我在夸大其词，他这番话差不多就是我照原样复述出来的；那些不幸认识他的人，最肯相信我这话句句属实。倘若坎迪奥拉当年住在努曼西亚，人家就要说了：努曼西亚人是一班木炭商人，其中掺和着几位英雄。

===Original===
“I am lost! I am ruined forever!” he went on, crossing his hands forlornly. “Those receipts were part of my fortune. How am I going to claim the amounts without any documents to show, and when almost all my debtors are dead, and lying rotting about the streets! I said, and I repeat it, those who have made me all this trouble are disobedient to God. It is a mortal sin; it is an unforgivable offence to let themselves be killed when they owe money on such old accounts that their creditor will not be able to collect easily. Paying up is very hard work; so some of these people say, ‘Let us wall ourselves in and burn with the money.’ But God is inexorable with this heroic rabble, and to chastise them He will resurrect them, so that they will yet have to meet the constable and the notary. My God, resurrect them! Holy Virgin del Pilar, Santo Domingo del Val, resurrect them, I pray!”

“And your daughter?” I asked with interest. “Did she come out of the fire unharmed?”

“Do not speak of her to me as my daughter!” he replied sternly. “God has punished me for her faults. I know now who her infamous admirer is. Who can it possibly be, but that damned son of Don José Montoria who studied to be a priest! Mariquilla has confessed it to me. Yesterday she was dressing a wound he has on his arm, and this was done before me. Did you ever hear of anything so shameless?”

As he said this, Doña Guedita, who was looking anxiously for her master, came up with a cup containing some sort of nourishment. He took it hungrily; and then, by force of entreaty, we succeeded in getting him away from there, taking him to the Órgano alley, where his daughter had taken refuge, in a porch, with other shelterless ones. After growling at her a moment, Candiola went on into the house, followed by his housekeeper.

===Chinese===
“我完了！我这一辈子彻底毁了！”他绞着双手，凄凄惶惶地接着说，“那些字据本是我家产的一部分。凭据一张不剩，叫我拿什么去讨账？何况我的债户差不多死光了，正横在街头发烂呢！我说过，如今再说一遍：给我惹出这一切祸事的人，都是违逆上帝的。这是弥天大罪；他们欠着那么陈旧的账——欠得那么久，债主眼看难以讨回——却由着自己被打死，这是不可饶恕的过犯。还账是苦差事；于是这些人里头就有说的：‘咱们把自己砌在墙里，抱着钱一块儿烧死吧。’可上帝对这帮英雄泼皮决不姑息，为了惩罚他们，上帝还要叫他们复活，好教他们照样去见法警和公证人。我的上帝啊，叫他们复活吧！皮拉尔至圣圣母，圣多明戈·德尔·瓦尔（Santo Domingo del Val），求你们叫他们复活吧！”

“那您的女儿呢？”我关切地问，“她从大火里逃出来，没伤着吧？”

“别再向我提她，还说什么我的女儿！”他声色俱厉地答道，“上帝降罚于我，是因她造下的孽。如今我算知道她那个不要脸的相好是谁了。除了唐·何塞·德·蒙特里亚那个读过神学的该死的儿子，还能是谁！玛丽基亚向我招认了。昨天她还在给他包扎胳膊上的伤，就当着我的面。天下竟有这样无耻的事，你听说过吗？”

正说着，堂娜·格韦迪塔一路寻主人寻得心焦，走了过来，手里端着一杯什么滋身的汤水。他饥不择食地接了过来；随后我们好说歹说，总算劝得他离开那里，把他送到奥尔加诺巷（Órgano），他女儿正同其余无家可归的人一起，在一个门廊里避难。坎迪奥拉冲她咆哮了一阵，便自顾自进了屋，女管家跟在后头。

===Original===
“Where is Augustine?” I asked Mariquilla.

“He was here a moment ago; but someone came to tell him of the death of his brother, and he has gone. I heard it said that the family is in the Calle de las Rufas.”

“His brother is dead! Don José’s eldest son!”

“So they said, and he started in haste and in great distress.”

Without waiting to hear more, I also ran to the Calle de las Rufas to do everything I could to help in their trouble the generous family to which I owed so much. Before arriving there, I met Don Roque, who, with tears in his eyes, came up to speak to me.

===Chinese===
“奥古斯丁在哪儿？”我问玛丽基亚。

“他方才还在这儿；有人来报信，说他哥哥死了，他就走了。我听人说，他们一家都在鲁法斯街（Calle de las Rufas）。”

“他哥哥死了！唐·何塞的长子！”

“他们是这么说的，他急急忙忙、悲恸欲绝地走了。”

我不再等下文，也朝鲁法斯街奔去，想在这场灾祸里尽一份力，去帮助那恩义深重的慷慨人家。还没赶到，就迎面遇上唐·罗克，他眼里噙着泪，走上前来同我说话。

===Original===
“Gabriel,” he said, “God has laid his hand heavily today upon our good friend.”

“Is it the eldest son who is dead, Manuel Montoria?”

“Yes, and that is not the only trouble of the family. Manuel was married, as you know, and had a son four years of age. You see that group of women? Well, the wife of Montoria’s poor eldest son is there with her boy in her arms. He is dying of the epidemic, and is already in his agony. Is it not a horrible state of things? There is one of the first families of Saragossa reduced to this sad condition, without a roof to cover them, in want of the most necessary things. That unfortunate young mother was in the street all night, exposed to the weather with her sick child in her arms, expecting every instant that he would breathe his last. After all it is better to be here than in one of those pestilent cellars where no one can breathe. I am thankful that I and other friends have been able to help her a little; but what can one do when there is scarcely any bread to be had? The wine is all finished, and a bit of beef is not to be found, though I gave her a piece of ours.”

===Chinese===
“加夫列尔，”他说，“今天，上帝重重地降罚在我们那位好朋友身上了。”

“是长子死了吗？曼努埃尔·蒙特里亚（Manuel Montoria）？”

“是啊，而且这家人的祸事还不止这一桩。曼努埃尔成了家，这你是知道的，还有个四岁的儿子。你瞧见那边那群妇人了吗？蒙特里亚家可怜的长媳就在那儿，怀里抱着她的孩子。孩子染上了时疫，已经到了弥留之际。这光景岂不骇人听闻？萨拉戈萨数一数二的人家，竟落到这步凄惨的境地，头上没有一片瓦，连最必需的东西都缺。那不幸的年轻母亲整夜待在街上，怀抱病儿任凭风吹雨打，时时刻刻都在等他咽下最后一口气。不过话说回来，待在这儿总比闷在那些叫人喘不上气的瘟窖里强。总算亏得我和其他几位朋友还能帮她一把；可是连面包都几乎弄不到手，又叫人有什么法子呢？酒全喝光了，一小块牛肉也寻不见——虽说我们把自家的一块给了她。”

===Original===
Morning began to come. I went up to the group of women and saw a sorrowful sight. With the anguished effort to save life, the mother and the few women who kept her company were torturing the poor child with remedies which everybody tries at such a time; but it needed only to see the victim of the fever to realize the impossibility of saving that little being whom death had already grasped with his relentless hand.

The voice of Don José de Montoria obliged me to hasten forward more quickly; and in an outer corner in the Calle de las Rufas a second group completed the dreadful picture of that unhappy family. Stretched upon the ground was the body of Manuel, a young man of thirty years, no less amiable and generous in his life than his father and brother. A ball had pierced his head, and from the small external wound, at the spot whence the ball had emerged, a thread of blood still trickled, dropping down the temple, the cheek, and the neck, and falling down upon the skin beneath the shirt. Because of this, the body did not seem like that of one dead.

When I arrived, nobody had been able to make his mother believe that he was dead, and she held his head upon her knees, hoping to revive him with tender words. Montoria, on his knees at the right side, held his son’s hand between his own hands and gazed at him, speechless, not taking his eyes from him. As white as the dead, the father did not weep.

===Chinese===
天开始亮了。我走近那群妇人，看见一幅凄惨的景象。母亲和陪伴她的寥寥几个妇人，怀着救命的焦灼，正拿这种时刻人人都免不了要一试的种种法子，折磨那可怜的孩子；可是只消看一眼那高烧的小受害人，就明白这条小命是万万救不回来了——死亡已经伸出无情的手，攥住了这小小的生命。

唐·何塞·德·蒙特里亚的声音催我加快脚步赶上前去；在鲁法斯街靠外的一个拐角，另一群人补全了这不幸人家的惨象。曼努埃尔的遗体直挺挺躺在地上——一个三十岁的年轻人，生前同他的父亲和弟弟一样和善慷慨。一颗子弹穿透了他的头颅，在弹头穿出的地方，那个小小的外伤口里还有一缕鲜血往外渗，顺着鬓角、面颊、脖颈淌下来，滴落在衬衫底下的肌肤上。正因为如此，这遗体看上去竟不像死人的模样。

我赶到的时候，还没有一个人能让他母亲相信人已经死了；她把儿子的头捧在膝上，指望用温存的话语把他唤醒。蒙特里亚跪在右边，把儿子的手攥在自己两掌中间，凝视着他，一言不发，目光须臾不离。父亲的脸色同死人一样惨白，却没有落泪。

===Original===
“Wife!” he exclaimed at last, “do not pray God for the impossible. We have lost our son.”

“No, my son is not dead!” exclaimed the mother, in despair. “It is a lie. Why deceive me? How could it be possible for God to take our son from us? What have we done to deserve such a punishment? Manuel, my son, why dost thou not answer me? Why dost thou not move? Why dost thou not speak? In a moment we will carry thee into the house﻿—but where is our house? My son grows cold on this bare ground. See how chill are his hands and his face!”

“You must go away from here, wife,” said Montoria, restraining the flood of his tears; “we will take care of Manuel.”

“O my Lord God!” moaned the mother, “what ails my son that he does not speak, nor move, nor wake? He seems to be dead; but he is not, he cannot be dead! Holy Virgin del Pilar, is it not true that my son is not dead?”

===Chinese===
“夫人！”他终于喊出声来，“别再求上帝行不可能的事了。我们的儿子已经没了。”

“不，我的儿子没有死！”母亲绝望地喊道，“这是谎话。为什么要骗我？上帝怎么可能把我们的儿子从我们身边夺走？我们做了什么孽，要受这样的惩罚？曼努埃尔，我的儿，你怎么不答应我？怎么不动一动？怎么不说话？我们这就把你抬进屋里去——可我们的家在哪儿？我的儿子在这光秃秃的地上都凉透了。瞧他的手，瞧他的脸，多冷啊！”

“你必得离开这儿，夫人，”蒙特里亚强忍着如潮的泪水说道，“曼努埃尔有我们照料。”

“噢，主我的上帝！”母亲呜咽着，“我的儿子这是怎么了，不说话，不动弹，也唤不醒？他像是死了；可他没有死，他不可能死了！皮拉尔至圣圣母，我的儿子没有死，这不是真的，对不对？”

===Original===
“Leocadia,” repeated Montoria, wiping away the first tears that had fallen from his eyes, “go away from here a little, go away, for God’s sake! Be resigned, for God has dealt us a heavy blow, and our son no longer lives. He has died for his country.”

“Why has my son died!” exclaimed the mother, straining the body to her in her arms, as if she would not let it go. “No, no, no! What is the country to me? Let my son be given back to me. Manuel, my boy, do not let them separate you from me; those who would tear you from my arms must kill me first.”

“O Lord God, Holy Virgin del Pilar,” said Don José de Montoria, in solemn tones, “never have I knowingly and deliberately offended ye. For the sake of religion and the king I have given my goods and my sons. Why, instead of my firstborn, why have you not taken my life a hundred times, miserable old man, good for nothing? Gentlemen, you who are present, I am not ashamed to weep before you; my heart is utterly broken, but Montoria is still the same. We will say to thee, Happy art thou a thousand times, my son, who hast died at the post of honor. Unhappy those of us who still live, having lost thee.
